The direction worth trying: each feature hands the service **one value** that knows its ports, schema, outbox and routes, and the service iterates a list instead of naming every feature at every step — so a new feature is one line, and forgetting a part of it stops being possible.

**Done when:** an idea carries no link to any view; a view gains a new kind of relation without `ideas` changing; the appearances read model answers in one request; and the board is usable without ever opening an inspector.

*Done. `Idea` is `{ project, title, discarded }` and nothing else, and the proof of the second criterion is in the history: `features/ideas` has not changed since step 3, while the outline learned to note ideas (5), passages to link them (6b) and appearances to follow both (7). Every link is owned by the side that makes it — the outline's `Attachment`, the passage's `passage_ideas` — and each announces it through its own outbox; passages, not event-sourced, learned to announce in the same transaction as the change. Appearances store `Subject` at `Place` generically and stay typed in the domain, with boards deliberately not places. The client's `/ideas/{idea}` became the inspector, and the board docks the same `InspectorState` at the top of its tray, following selection, with renames meeting in the middle by version. Along the way: `Detached` names the section it left, the outline catalog's columns became `attachment_type` / `attachment_id`, the test fixture closes its pools and takes turns, and a client view keeps its state in a `<View>State` ([CONVENTIONS.md](./CONVENTIONS.md#a-type-is-named-for-what-it-is-spelled-out)). Left for later, as listed above: reordering within a group, tree controls, the two-column passage page, a paged link dialog and project ids on messages; the wiring rework moved to M13.*

**Still unknown, and deliberately not gating this:** how often an idea maps one-to-one onto a scene, and what the timeline wants to hold. Those are ergonomics and they shape the *views*; the structural question was settled on lifecycle. Twenty real ideas on a board will answer them, and that is worth doing before the timeline is designed rather than before this.

---

### Milestone 13 — Local mode ✅

**Goal:** an author runs Weaveling on their own machine with no database server and no broker, and their work lives in files they own — written as they work, so it survives a restart.

Sits beside M11 on purpose: *"the real store"* and *"no store at all"* are two answers to the same question, and the ports mean neither has to win.

**Backed by SQLite** — see [the decision](#sqlite-backs-local-mode--decided): one file per feature plus one for messaging, the relay woken by a `tokio::sync::Notify` rather than `LISTEN/NOTIFY`, and a schema twin for every migration.

**One binary, the backend chosen at runtime.** Decided 2026-10-07. Every backend is compiled in and configuration picks: `WEAVELING_DATABASE_URL` → PostgreSQL, a data directory → local SQLite, neither → in memory. A third backend does not fit compile-time features — they stop being additive, and `--all-features`, which CI builds, would need a precedence rule — and one binary for every mode is what an author downloads anyway.

**Export moved out, to [M13a](#milestone-13a--moving-a-project-between-machines).** It was here as the only thing standing between an author and a lost afternoon. With SQLite, work is durable because it was written as it happened, and export becomes what it should always have been: a way to move a project between machines.

**Local is single-user by definition** — no accounts, no authors on other machines. Two tabs on the same computer still collaborate, because the sync socket is local and knows nothing about deployment.

**It follows [M12](#milestone-12--ideas-and-passages-) on purpose**, because local mode writes a SQLite adapter for every port in the codebase and M12 reshaped several of them. Porting first would have meant porting the same stores twice.

**The steps, each reviewable alone:**

0. **What has to be true first.**
   - **0a — passages are evicted, and compacted on the way out.** The [open TODO](./TODO.md) with its intended shape: participants counted inside the map's write lock, a sweeper that collects entries idle for a grace period, and a final persist before anything leaves memory. A process that lives as long as an author's afternoon has no restart to save it.
   - **0b — the wiring is reworked.** Moved here from M12's *later*: each feature hands the service one value that knows its ports, schemas, outbox and routes. Without it a third backend means some ten more edits per feature in `services/api`, and forgetting an outbox keeps compiling.
1. **Groundwork.** Every migration moves into `migrations/postgres/`, with its `migrations/sqlite/` twin arriving beside it in the step that ports that adapter; sqlx gains its `sqlite` feature, the test harness a `SqliteFixture`, and `wiring::sqlite` lays out one file per feature — while `wiring::database` becomes `wiring::postgres`, since there are now two. No adapter yet.
2. **The event store and outbox on SQLite.** **2a** events and snapshots — and `enqueue`, since an append writes its messages in the same transaction. Between the two, **the outbox moved out of `eventsourcing` into its own library**: passages announce through it without being event-sourced, and should not need an event store, or an `events` table, to do so. **2b** the SQLite outbox and its relay, in that library. Claiming is an ordinary `UPDATE … RETURNING`, since one process means one relay.
3. **Deliveries and the registry on SQLite.** Retries and dead letters must survive a crash, or the [durable delivery](#milestone-11b--one-flow-in-every-mode) is a lie in local mode.
4. **The five catalogs on SQLite** — projects, ideas, boards, outline, appearances. Mechanical against existing suites; one commit each, one review.
5. **The passages store on SQLite** — updates, titles, linked ideas.
6. **The service runs locally.** Split in three: **6a** `Storage::Sqlite` and every feature on it, proven by the service tests ported to SQLite and a restart from files alone; **6b** the binary choosing at startup — `WEAVELING_DATABASE_URL` for PostgreSQL, `WEAVELING_DATA` for a data directory, neither for memory, both refused — with every backend compiled in and the local relays polling every 100ms, since the SQLite outbox is never notified; **6c** the browser suite run against local mode as well as in memory.
7. **A refused message reaches the author.** Retries and a durable dead-letter table exist since M11b; in local mode there is no ops staff to read the table, so the client shows what was refused and why.
   - **7a — the server lists, retries and acknowledges.** `Deliveries` learns all three on every backend, and the API serves them under `/api/service/refusals`. Retrying puts the message back to be delivered with its attempts reset. **Acknowledging marks it seen and keeps it** — dismissing was the first idea and was dropped: every dead letter belongs to a listener whose miss corrupts something, a projection or a sweep, and with no rebuild from events a discarded one is a gap for good.
   - **7b — a status bar.** Thin and app-wide along the bottom, quiet enough to forget. Empty for now; it is where the [writing view](#milestone-13c--the-writing-view-)'s save status and word count go, and later the statistics of each view. *Done: a `footer` beside the routes rather than inside each view, so it outlives navigation — which 7c's polling will want — and `sticky` rather than `fixed`, so it stays in sight on a long page yet never covers the page's end. Both are proved by breaking them.*
   - **7c — the alarm.** On the bar's far right, a red flash icon, shown only while something is refused. The client asks every 5 seconds. A new arrival makes the icon pulse a few times — under three flashes a second, and not at all under `prefers-reduced-motion` — and then it stays steady red. Clicking it opens a dialog listing what was refused in plain words, the technical reason folded away but there when expanded, each with **Try again** and **Acknowledge**. The icon shows while anything is unacknowledged; acknowledged ones stay listed, greyed out, and can still be tried again.
     - *7c1 done: the client asks every 5 seconds, from the status bar so the asking outlives navigation, and shows the icon while anything is unacknowledged. It pulses three times, at well under three flashes a second, only for a refusal it has not heard of before, and not at all under `prefers-reduced-motion`. The browser tests stub the route, since a real dead letter takes a listener refusing five times.*
     - *7c2 done: the icon became a button opening the dialog — each refusal in plain words — said by the listener itself through a required `when_refused`, so a new listener cannot ship without them, with a fallback only for a dead letter whose listener is gone; when it was given up on; the listener, message, attempts and reason folded into Details; **Try again** and **Acknowledge**. It closes by its button, Escape or a click outside. One change from the plan: with only acknowledged refusals left, the icon stays, **muted rather than red** — otherwise nothing would lead back to them, and acknowledged ones are meant to stay within reach of Try again.*
   - **Before 7c ships Try again: retries out of order — checked.** A retried message arrives after everything handled since. Of the 16 `Kept` listeners, 11 are safe: the catalogs re-read the aggregate's latest state rather than trusting the message, and the sweeps and discard handlers react to things that cannot be undone. Three need fixing first, each its own step:
     - **A — the pin and attachment indexes forget a discarded board or outline.** A late *pinned* re-reads a discarded board, which still lists its ideas, and writes the pins back; `unpin-discarded-idea` then hits `Discarded` and dead-letters for good. The indexes treat a discarded aggregate as empty, and both discard listeners take `Discarded` as already done. *Done, with a regression test for each that fails on the code before.*
     - **B — appearances from the outline keep versions.** Its writes are bare set operations, so a late *attached* after *detached*, a removed section or a discarded idea brings an appearance back, and a late *detached* drops a live one. Each row keeps the last version it saw and whether it is there, a write only lands if newer, and a removed section or discarded idea is remembered as gone. The version is the envelope's. *Done: `version` and a `present` flag on every appearance, `gone_subjects` and `gone_places` beside them, on all three backends — proved by breaking each guard. Deleted passages are already covered, since gone-for-good needs no version. Known gap, PostgreSQL only: a discard racing a note can still slip one row past the gone check, because the ideas and outline listeners run concurrently; SQLite's single writer cannot.*
     - **C — appearances from passages, likewise.** Same hazard, but the link messages carry no version — `IdeaLinkDTO` is `{ passage, idea }` — and passages are not event-sourced, so they first need a counter per passage on their link and deletion messages. *Done: a `version` on each passage, bumped by every change to it — writing and retitling too — in the same transaction, and carried by `IdeaLinkDTO`; deletion needed none, since gone-for-good already covers it. With A, B and C, Try again cannot break what it repairs.*
   - **7d — a notification.** One dismissible note per arrival rather than per message — "Some changes couldn't be applied" — pointing at the icon. *Done: a note above the bar's right end, a polite live region, raised by the same arrival that pulses the icon, so refusals landing together make one note. **Show** opens the dialog, ✕ dismisses it without acknowledging anything, and it stays until acted on rather than fading before it is read. Step 7 is done.*

**Done when:** an author can work with no database server running, stop the process, start it again, and find their ideas, board, outline and scenes exactly as they left them; a refused message is shown to them rather than only stored; and the browser suite passes against local mode.

*Done. `WEAVELING_DATA` keeps the work in one SQLite file per feature and survives a restart from those files alone; every port has a SQLite adapter behind the same conformance suite as PostgreSQL; the wiring hands the service one value per feature; the outbox became a library of its own; scenes are evicted and compacted on the way out. Refused messages reach the author through the status bar's alarm, a note and a dialog — with **Try again** made safe first by versioning appearances and scene links, and by teaching two indexes to forget what was discarded — and every listener says in plain words what its refusal costs. The browser suite runs in memory and in local mode, in CI as well. Left open, as listed below: the wiring rework, two executables, waking the SQLite relay, refusals scoped to the project and gathered across services, version guards on the catalog upserts, and [the ideas sweep race](./TODO.md).*

---

### Milestone 13b — Passages become scenes ✅

**Goal:** the word for a piece of the book's prose is *scene*, the word writers already use for it.

**Why.** Looking through existing writing tools, nearly all of them call their units of prose *scenes*, and it reads better than *passage* the longer it is used. Better to match the craft's vocabulary while no author has data under the old word.

**Build:** a pure rename, the way [M12 step 1](#milestone-12--ideas-and-passages-) turned pieces into ideas — the `passages` feature becomes `scenes`: crates, `PassageId` to `SceneId`, `PassageLink` and every other `Passage*` type, the `passage.*` routing keys, tables and migrations, the appearances `Place::Passage`, the sync route, the client and its labels ("Passages not in the book" and the rest), the e2e specs, and the docs. No behaviour changes, so it is reviewed by confirming nothing *but* names moved. Some 1,600 mentions across about 95 Rust files, before the client and the docs.

**Kept on purpose:** stored or library names that never meant *passage* — the CRDT fragment stays `"prose"` and ProseMirror stays ProseMirror.

**Decided 2026-10-07: every piece of prose is a scene**, opening lines and epigraphs included. A chapter's first lines are a scene too rather than a second kind of unit, so this stays a straight rename, not a split.

**Done when:** no code, route, message, table or label says *passage*, and every test passes unchanged in behaviour.

*Done. `features/passages` is `features/scenes` and every name inside followed — crates, types, ids (`scene_…`), routes (`/api/scenes`, `/projects/{p}/scenes/{s}`), routing keys (`scene.*`), listener names, tables, migrations, the appearances `Place::Scene`, the outline's `Attachment::Scene`, the client and its labels, the e2e specs and the docs. All 1,375 Rust tests and the browser suite in both modes pass unchanged. **This roadmap keeps *passage* where it records what was built at the time** — finished milestones and their headings, whose anchors other documents link to; open items and everything ahead say *scene*.*

---

### Milestone 13c — The writing view ✅

**Goal:** the place an author actually writes — the heart of the app, discovered early rather than polished late.

**Why now.** Everything so far has been about where scenes live and how they are arranged; the page where prose gets written is still the editor M6 proved could sync, with the metadata stacked underneath. A survey of existing writing tools has produced a list of ideas for it, and they should shape the rest rather than be fitted in afterwards.

**Shape, from the survey** (2026-10-08 — a first overview, more to come):

- **Clean and quiet.** A view that keeps distraction to a minimum; writing is the only thing it asks for.
- **Big, readable type.** Text scalable by the author, already large by default — around 20px — with a line height of at least 1.5, set in **Lexend** for its readability.
- **The book as one flow.** Scenes one after another in reading order, with the section names between them, rather than one scene per page.
- **Only what is in view is loaded.** A book does not fit in a page, so the flow scrolls infinitely *both* ways, loading scenes as they come near and letting go of those far away.
- **Typing is how a scene begins.** A section with nothing written yet can simply be typed into, and doing so creates its scene.
- **A tray on the right, closed by default.** Opened, it shows everything about the scene the cursor is in — the ideas linked, and later the characters and the rest. The two-column scene page noted under [M12](#milestone-12--ideas-and-passages-) is the same idea, now for the whole flow.
- **Spell checking on a toggle**, so the red lines can be switched off while drafting.

**Leaning design, from the first discussion** (2026-10-08):

- **Several editors that read as one.** Each scene stays its own CRDT document, synced on its own, with its own small editor; the flow stacks them so they read as one text, and arrow keys at a scene's edge carry the cursor into the next or previous one. One editor spanning many scenes would need a binding that splits one ProseMirror document across many Y.Docs — uncharted, and the hardest part of the feature — while stacked editors are how block editors work and fit loading only what is in view: scrolling mounts and unmounts whole scenes. Many visible scenes means many sockets; multiplexing them over one connection is the later fix if that hurts.
- **A horizontal line marks where one scene ends and the next begins.**
- **Enter, after Neo.** Once: a new paragraph in the same scene. Twice: the empty paragraph goes and a new scene begins right after this one, in the same section. Three times: a new section after the current one, at the same level, its heading ready to type.
- **Nesting by Tab and Shift+Tab on a section heading**, the gesture the outline already uses, so one habit serves both views.

- **Entered by "Write"** in the masthead's views, beside Board, Outline and Ideas.
- **A quiet status note in the bottom right**, saying "All saved" or "Saving…" — closing the tab between typing and the save landing loses the last change, so it should be visible without asking for attention — together with a few statistics such as the word count.

- **"Write" lands where the author left off**, and at the start of the book the first time.
- **Following a scene from an idea opens the writing view at that scene**, rather than a page of its own.
- **The save status speaks for the whole view**: "Saving…" while any change anywhere in it is in flight.
- **The word count toggles by a click** between the section the cursor is in (the default) and the whole book.

**Design decided 2026-10-10**, from three mockups:

- **The manuscript column is the view.** One centred column of about 680px, part and section headings inline in Newsreader, a thin line between scenes, an empty section inviting the first words. The tray is closed down to a "This scene" tab on the right edge.
- **The book rail and the open tray come from the second mockup.** A narrow list of parts and sections on the left, the current one marked, for finding one's way in a long book; the tray, opened, shows the scene's title, the ideas it draws on, and later its characters. Both are opened by the author, never forced on them.
- **Focus is a mode, and a later one.** The masthead shrinks to a breadcrumb, section names move into the margin, and all but the paragraph being written fades back.

**Structure from the flow, decided 2026-10-11.** Sections are not only made in the outline: the author can shape the book while writing it, and the outline stays the place for seeing the whole and for moving things around.

- **A heading is its own small field, not part of a scene.** It belongs to the outline aggregate — renamed with a version check, saved after a pause or on leaving it — while a scene's text is a CRDT merged keystroke by keystroke. Two authors renaming one heading at once: the last one wins, and that is acceptable.
- **An empty book starts with its first heading**, ready to type, and the first scene below it — no detour through the outline.
- **A "+" in the margin** beside the scene or section the cursor is in, shown on hover or for the current one: *new scene above / below*, *new section above / below*, a section at the same level, nested by Tab afterwards. Enter three times stays the keyboard way. *Added after 3d2:* the "+" should also **bring back a scene** that was taken out of the book — a list of the scenes not in the book, the chosen one placed above or below — so taking one out is not a trip to the outline to undo.
- **A ⋮ in the margin** opens what can be done to the thing beside it. On a section, *Remove section*: its scenes return to the pool rather than being lost, as removing one in the outline does, and a section still holding scenes asks first. On a scene: *Rename*, *Take out of the book*, which keeps its text in the pool, and *Delete scene*, which asks first.
- **Scene titles in the left margin**, level with the top of their scene, as in the focus mockup — so what a scene is about stays in sight while it is written. Read-only there, so a stray click never lands in it; renaming goes through the ⋮, and an untitled scene shows its opening words, faded. The "+" and the ⋮ live in the same margin, so the text column holds nothing but prose. On a narrow screen the margin folds above its scene.

**Slices**, each its own review:

1. **"Write" and an empty view** — the masthead entry, the route, the manuscript column with the book's sections in order as headings and nothing else yet. *Done: `/projects/{p}/write`, the column at 20px Lexend, sections read depth-first and set as `h2`–`h4` in Newsreader, and an unshaped book pointing to the outline.*
2. **Scenes in the flow** — each scene's prose in its own editor under its section, a line between scenes, the cursor carried across scene edges by the arrow keys. *Done: one ProseMirror editor and one sync connection per scene, the write state holding them in the book's reading order; an arrow key at a scene's first or last line — or left and right at its very start and end — hands the cursor to the neighbouring scene, across section headings too; so do the word and paragraph jumps (Ctrl, or Cmd and Alt on a Mac), but only from a scene's very start or end. Where the cursor is gets read from the browser's own selection, since ProseMirror's copy can lag a key behind. Every scene in the book is mounted for now; slice 6 trims that to what is in view.*
3. **Typing makes scenes, and sections** — an empty section takes the first words as a new scene; Enter twice starts the next scene, three times a new section; an empty book starts with its first heading; headings are renamed in place.
 *3a done: headings are the heading elements made editable, saved after a pause or on leaving, Enter leaving them; an empty book starts with its first heading, made on the first keystroke with anything typed meanwhile carried over; sections and scenes render keyed, so a rename or a new section never rebuilds the editors around it; an empty scene shows itself and an invitation to write. The arrow keys walk headings and scenes as one sequence: Up and Down always leave a heading, Left and Right only from its very edge, and a section with no scenes leads straight on to the next heading. Found on the way: a title typed in the outline was wiped when an older answer about another section landed — each field now follows only its own title.* *3b done: an empty section shows a writing line under its heading, reached by the arrow keys like any other line; the first keystroke creates a scene, attaches it to the section, and its editor takes over the words typed meanwhile with the caret after them. An empty book now goes from its first heading straight into its first scene without the mouse.* *3c done: Enter twice at the end of a scene opens a line after it, which the first keystroke makes a scene; Enter on that line — or on an empty section's — opens a heading after the current section, at its level and after everything nested in it, which the first keystroke makes a section; a line left empty goes away and makes nothing; Enter in a heading moves on into its section. Nothing is created on the server until something is typed. Splitting a scene by Enter twice in its middle is not done: there it stays a paragraph.*
3d. **The margin** — scene titles beside their scenes, the "+" and the ⋮ with their menus. *3d1 done: each scene's title stands in the left margin, level with its top and out of the text column; an untitled scene shows its opening words, faded and following the writing as it is typed, cut as the outline cuts them; below 72rem the margin folds above its scene. Read-only, as decided. 3d2 done: a ⋮ beside each scene and each heading, shown on hover or focus. A scene's menu renames it in place (Enter or leaving saves, Escape keeps the old title), takes it out of the book, or deletes it after asking. A section's menu removes it, asking first only when it holds scenes, which go back among the scenes not in the book. The ⋮ buttons stay out of the Tab order, so Shift+Tab from a scene reaches its heading; Shift+F10 opens the menu of whatever is being written (the context-menu key was tried and dropped: the browsers keep it for their own menu), the arrow keys step through it, and Escape hands the cursor back. The server half came first: a deleted scene is now taken out of the outline by `detach-deleted-scene`, and building it turned up the "not yet indexed" race in every tidier, fixed with tombstones (see TODO). **Decided after a first cut of 3d3a: the "+" lives in the gaps, not beside a scene.** Every gap — between a heading and its first scene, between two scenes, after a section's last scene — is an insertion point whose "+" shows when the pointer comes near, so where a new thing appears is never in question and the menu needs no *above / below*. The ⋮ stays beside its scene or heading, because it acts on a thing rather than a place. Slices: **3d3a** the "+" in the gaps with *Bring back a scene…*, **3d3b** a search in that dialog (filtered in the client for now; **later** a search route on the server, `GET /api/scenes?project=…&find=…` with paging, so a book with thousands of scenes does not ship every scene to the browser to fill one dialog — the same pressure as the board's ten thousand ideas), **3d3c** *New scene*, **3d3d** *New section* where nothing has to move: at a section's end, and in a gap of its own **at the very end of the book**, after the last section and everything nested in it, which adds a section at the top level; and likewise **before the first section**, the one gap that offers nothing but *New section*. **Later:** *New subsection* in a gap between two scenes — in a chapter with a scene at the top and a subsection below, a new subsection can start mid-way, taking the scenes below the gap with it and becoming the chapter's first subsection. A section of the *same* level there is not wanted. It is a structural move behind a "+", so it asks or shows what moves. 3d3a done: every gap carries a "+" in the ⋮ column that shows when the pointer comes near, with a dashed line where the scene would go; *Bring back a scene…* lists the scenes not in the book by title or opening words and places the chosen one in that gap. A gap's reach covers only empty space, so the writing beside it stays clickable. 3d3b done: the dialog opens on a search field that narrows the scenes by their shown name, ignoring case; nothing matching says so, and Enter brings back the first match. 3d3c done: *New scene* in a gap opens the same pending line Enter twice does, right in that gap — a scene only once something is typed, gone again if left empty; at a section's start it is a line before the first scene, and in an empty section the cursor simply goes to its opening line. 3d3d done: *New section* is offered in the gap after a section's last scene — the next section at its level, or its first subsection when it already has some, so the heading lands exactly where the gap was — and in two gaps of the book's own, before the first section and after everything, which offer nothing else. The heading is made at once, empty and focused: choosing from a menu is deliberate, so it needs no pending line the way a stray Enter does. **Nesting**, found missing right after: Tab in a heading nests its section under the one before, Shift+Tab lifts it out a level — the outline's own keys — and the heading's ⋮ offers the same as *Nest under the previous section* and *Lift out a level*, each only when possible. A title still being typed is saved first, and the cursor stays in the heading even though it is drawn anew at its new depth.*
4. **The status note** — "All saved" / "Saving…" for the whole view, and the word count toggling between section and book. Slices: **4a** the note, for scene text; **4b** the view's other writes — headings, scene titles, structure — counted too; **4c** the word count. *4a done: a quiet note at the right of the status bar. A scene is saving from its first change until the server has stored it; nothing in the sync protocol says so, so the editor asks for the server's state after a change, and since the server stores each edit before reading the next frame, the answer means everything sent before it is stored. One question is out at a time, so typing asks about once per round trip. "All saved" waits half a second before showing, so the note does not flicker while typing. A scene that leaves the flow stops counting.* *4b done: the note also waits for every other write the view makes. A heading counts from its first key, before its pause-save has even gone out, so a title still being typed is never "All saved". A scene renamed in the margin counts until the server has its title, and so does every change to the book's shape. Those already ran one at a time through the outline's write lane, which now counts each write while it is queued or running.* *4c done: the note counts the words of the section the cursor is in — its own scenes and every section nested in it — named by its title, and a click on the count switches to the whole book and back. Before the cursor has been anywhere it counts the first section. A word is anything between spaces holding a letter or a digit, counted by each scene's editor as its text changes, from the keyboard or from sync. It counts mounted scenes only, which today is every scene; slice 6 has to keep the counts of scenes it lets go of. Step 4 is done.*
5. **Where you left off, and following a scene there** — "Write" lands where the author left off, and a scene followed from an idea or the outline opens the view at it. Slices: **5a** where you left off; **5b** following a scene there. *5a done: each scene's editor reports its caret as it moves, and the view keeps the scene and the caret's position in the browser, per project. Accounts do not exist yet, and it is a convenience of one browser, not something the book holds. Opening "Write" lands there once the book is loaded and that scene's text has synced, with the line in the middle of the window, since ProseMirror focuses without scrolling. A scene no longer in the book, or a first visit, lands at the start of the first scene; a book with no scenes, on the first empty section's writing line. Landing never takes focus from something the author already clicked. Only scenes are remembered: a heading being renamed is not where anyone means to come back to.* *5b done: a scene followed from the outline or from an idea's page opens the writing view at it (`/write?scene=…`), at the start of the scene, or where the author left off if it is the scene they left off in. Once there, the address goes back to the view's own, so a reload lands where the author left off. A scene not in the book has no place in the flow, so the view sends it on to its own page, which stays for now for those and for the scene's metadata until the tray takes that over. *Write in* a section in the outline now waits until the new scene is in the book before opening the writing view at it. Before, it went to the scene page while the scene was still being attached. Step 5 is done.*
6. **Only what is in view** — the flow loads scenes as they come near and lets go of those far away, both ways.
   - **Proved by a stress test first, before and after:** a book of 10,000 scenes across a few hundred sections, measured in the browser — time until the first lines can be typed into, memory, open connections, and whether scrolling stays smooth. Today every scene mounts its own editor and opens its own connection, so this is expected to fail outright; the test is what shows the viewport fixed it rather than moved it.
   - **Measured before, 2026-10-14**, in local mode on one machine: `clients/web/stress/seed.mjs` builds a book (sections, scenes, about 100 words of real text each, written over the sync socket), and `playwright.stress.config.ts` runs `stress/measure.spec.ts` against running servers, writing `target/stress/<label>.json`.

     | Scenes | First line typeable | Scenes whose text arrived | Sync sockets open | JS heap | DOM nodes | 20 keys | Scroll frames |
     |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
     | 1,000 | 18.5 s | 255 | 255 | 24 MB | 32,281 | 66 ms | 16.7 ms, none over 50 ms |
     | 2,500 | 138 s | 262 | 255 | 52 MB | 79,246 | 128 ms | 16.7 ms, one over 50 ms |
     | 10,000 | not within 20 min | none, not even the first | 255 | 6.1 GB in the page process | page stopped answering | – | – |

     **Past 255 scenes the view is not slow but wrong.** Chrome allows about 255 WebSockets, so every scene after the 255th never syncs and shows "An empty scene. Start writing…" over text the server holds, with the status bar's word count, from statistics, saying otherwise. That makes the viewport, or one socket for many scenes, a correctness fix for long books rather than a speed-up. **The time to the first typeable line grows faster than the book**: 2.5 times the scenes took 7.5 times as long, which points at work per mounted scene that touches every other scene. Scrolling and typing held up wherever the page answered at all.

     **Seeding the book measured the server too.** Creating 10,000 scenes took 151 s and writing their text 69 s, but attaching them took 35 minutes, and slower with every thousand (38 s for the first, 389 s for the last): every change to the outline loads, snapshots and answers with the whole outline, and the listeners indexing it load it again, so a change costs in proportion to the book. And in local mode SQLite stalled for seconds on one-row updates under that load, once answering `database is locked`; see [TODO](./TODO.md).
   - **6b1 done: only what is near the window mounts.** A scene further than 1,500 px from the window is a placeholder of about its height, worked out from its word count, with no editor and no socket. It mounts as it comes near, through one `IntersectionObserver`, and stays mounted for now. Landing where the author left off brings its scene near first, and the margin names a waiting scene by its opening words from the scenes listing. On the same books, the first line was typeable after 1.2 s instead of 18.5 s for 1,000 scenes, and after 2.8 s instead of 138 s for 2,500. But scrolling through the book got worse, 283 ms per frame instead of 16.7 ms at 2,500 scenes, because mounting moved from loading to scrolling and nothing is let go yet.

     **Measuring 6b1 found the next problem: Chrome throttles opening WebSockets to one host.** After a jump down the book, 21 editors asked for their sockets within 13 ms, and Chrome opened them over 3.3 s, one after another with growing waits. A freshly mounted scene can wait seconds before its text arrives or its first words are saved; two browser tests wait up to 15 s for "All saved" with a `TODO` until this is fixed. So **one sync connection for the whole view is the next slice, not an afterthought**: the client subscribes a scene when it mounts and lets it go when it unmounts, and the server routes frames by scene. It also ends the 255-socket cap. Then 6b2 lets go of far scenes, and 6b3 carries the cursor across placeholders. The 10,000-scene book is measured again once all of that is in.
   - **6c, one sync connection for the view**, in two parts. *6c1 done, the server: `/api/sync` carries many scenes on one socket. Every frame travels in an envelope naming its scene, in lib0's encoding so the browser can write it with what Yjs already ships: the client sends `Join`, `Leave` and `Carry(scene, frame)`, and the server answers `Carry` and `Refused` for a scene that cannot be joined. Behind it each scene is the same live scene as before, and a `Connection` keeps one connection's scenes apart from its socket, so the tests drive it through plain channels. `Peer` no longer owns a socket and sends into whatever queue it is given, as it is or wrapped in an envelope, so `/api/sync/{scene}` kept working on it until the client had moved over; it was removed once 6c2 was in.* *6c2 done, the client:* `js/sync.js` replaces y-websocket. A `SyncConnection` holds the one socket of a writing view, joins and leaves scenes, routes envelopes to them, and reconnects with growing waits, joining every scene again when it is back. A `SceneSync` per editor does what the provider did, the first sync, sending edits and cursors, plus the save confirmation of 4a, now written into it directly rather than hooked into y-websocket's message handlers. Cursors are sent for our own changes only, and other authors' cursors are dropped while the connection is down. The scene page opens its own connection. The two 15-second waits are back to normal. New tests: another author's cursor shows in the scene they write in, and a dropped connection comes back with what was written meanwhile saved. Measured again: one socket where there were 255, and on 1,000 scenes scrolling at 33 ms a frame, p95 50 ms, against 83 ms. **Still slow: scrolling through 2,500 scenes** at over 300 ms a frame. One suspect: the scenes that are near are one set in one signal, so every scene that comes near makes all 2,500 recheck theirs. And the time until the first line is typeable still grows with the book, since every scene's row, margin and gaps are drawn, 71,000 DOM nodes at 2,500 scenes. Both belong with 6b2.
   - **6b2 done: far scenes are let go.** A second observer watches 3,000 px around the window, twice the margin a scene mounts at, so a scene at the edge does not mount and unmount over and over. A scene that leaves it is let go, leaving the connection, and its placeholder keeps the height it was measured at, so nothing below jumps. Never let go: the scene the cursor is in, a scene still saving, which is let go once its save is confirmed, and a scene being landed in, until the window has brought it near. Each scene now has its own signal for being near, so one scene coming near or going far no longer makes every scene check again. On 2,500 scenes scrolling went from 317 ms a frame to 67 ms, p95 100 ms; on 1,000 to 16.7 ms. **What is left is the rows.** Every scene still draws its row, margin, label and gaps, about 28 DOM nodes each and 71,000 at 2,500 scenes, even as a placeholder, which is why scrolling a big book is not yet smooth and the first line still takes longer the bigger the book. Drawing only the rows near the window is the step after 6b3.
   - **6b3 done: the cursor walks into a scene that is still a placeholder.** That happens when the author scrolls away while the cursor stays in its scene, and the scene next to it is let go. An arrow key or a word jump across the edge now holds that scene near, as landing does, and puts the cursor in once its editor has mounted and its text has arrived, at its start going down and at its end going up. The wait for the text is kept on purpose, although the test passes without it: arriving text moved the cursor along with it in every run, but on a slow connection the author could start typing before the text is there.
   - **6b4 done: only the rows near the window are drawn.** A far scene's whole slot, its row with margin and label, its gaps and any line begun after it, is one placeholder, watched and measured as one, so its kept height covers everything that went and nothing below jumps. One never measured is estimated from its words plus the gaps around a scene. The margin's opening words from the scenes listing now matter for a scene coming near: it is named before its own text arrives. Measured on the same books against before slice 6: 1,000 scenes typeable after 0.44 s instead of 18.5 s, 2,500 after 0.57 s instead of 138 s, 11,380 DOM nodes instead of 79,246, 6 MB of heap instead of 52, scrolling at 16.7 ms a frame with none over 50 ms, one socket instead of 255, and every scene that comes near gets its text. **The 10,000-scene book, measured after all of slice 6** on the book seeded for the first measurement: typeable after 1.3 s, where before it was not within 20 minutes; 10 MB of heap where the page went to 6.1 GB and stopped answering; 9 editors and one socket; 39,815 DOM nodes, mostly the two of each placeholder; scrolling at 16.7 ms a frame, p95 33 ms, none over 50 ms; 20 keys in 134 ms. **Slice 6 is done**, apart from statistics catching up on scenes nobody opens, which only matters for scenes written before counts were announced, so none today. [The outline's cost per change](./TODO.md) is not part of it and still stands: on this book a new scene or section costs about half a second on the server.
   - **Found by testing the 10,000-scene book in the browser, next after slice 6:**
     - **A light scenes listing.** The listing stops at 500 scenes, so the outline names only the first 500, and the writing view's margin and the bring-back dialog see no more. It also opens every scene's document and sends every scene's text, about 6 MB for 10,000 scenes, so lifting the limit alone would make a big book slower to open. **Decided: a scene catalog, a read model inside the scenes feature**, the way ideas list themselves from the idea catalog: a scene's id, project, title and opening words, fed by the feature's own announcements, and the listing answered from it, paged, without text and without opening a document. A scene's name is its own business, so the catalog sits with scenes rather than in statistics. Four reviews: **L1** scenes announces what the catalog needs, **L2** the catalog and its listener, **L3** the listing answered from it, **L4** the outline, the margin and the bring-back dialog move to it. *L1 done: `scene.created`, `scene.retitled` with the version it got, and `scene.opening.changed`, which the live scene tells when it counts words and only when the opening words differ from the last ones it told, which is rare. The rule for an opening, the first 40 characters run together on single spaces, moved into `libraries/words` beside the rule for a word, so the server and the browser cut it alike. Writing itself still announces nothing. L2 done: `SceneCatalog` in core, kept by `scenes-catalog` on memory, SQLite and PostgreSQL in the scenes database, with its own migration ledger. A title wins by the version it was set at and an opening by when it was announced, so late or redelivered messages cannot undo newer ones. Any message may arrive first and makes the row, and saying a scene exists never wipes its name. A deleted scene is tombstoned, so nothing late brings it back. The listener `catalogue-scene` hears `scene.created`, `scene.retitled`, `scene.opening.changed` and `scene.deleted`. Nothing reads the catalog yet; that is L3. L3 done: `GET /api/scenes?project=…&after=…` answers from the catalog, 1,000 scenes a page with the id to ask after for the next, each with its id, title and opening and no text. The client walks the pages and its listing callers take a `SceneSummary`, so the outline, the margin, the bring-back dialog and the idea inspector already read the light listing; L4 is left with what they still do with it. Scenes written before L1 are not in the catalog and there is no fill, so older local data is reset rather than carried. A read model now trails the write it follows, and the local suite showed it: SQLite's outbox only polled, every 100 ms, so a view opened right after a rename showed the old name. SQLite's `enqueue` now rings a bell inside the writer's transaction and the relay wakes on it, and since the relay's claim needs the write lock it sees the message the moment the write commits. The catalog then trails a write by 30–45 ms locally, too quick for a person but not for a test, so the e2e helpers that write through the API wait for the listing to catch up, as they already did for the ideas catalog. On 2,500 scenes the three pages come in 49 ms, about 150 KB a thousand, the outline names all 2,500 in 1.6 s where it named 500 before, and the writing view is typeable after 0.63 s as before. 10,000 waits for the outline drawing only what is in view. L4 folded into L3: once the callers took a `SceneSummary` nothing was left to move. The scene page still opens its one scene, and the project sweep still pages the store.*
     - **The outline draws only what is in view.** It draws all 10,001 leaves: 4.3 s to open, and leaving it blocks the page for 6.4 s while they are taken down, whatever view comes next. Following a scene from the outline into the writing view took 8.5 s, 6.6 s of it that block, which looks like a freeze. Only the rows near the window, as the writing view now does, or chapters collapsed to start with. **Chosen: only the leaves near the window.** A row in the outline has one height, so a far group of leaves is a placeholder exactly as tall as its rows, with nothing to measure or keep, and is drawn as it comes near. Collapsing chapters to start with hides the shape of the book and does not help once a big part is opened. Three reviews: **O1** the stress harness measures the outline, **O2** only the leaves near the window are drawn, **O3** measured again, at 10,000 too. *O1 done: the harness opens the outline and reports when the first name shows, the leaves drawn, the DOM nodes, the heap, frames while scrolling, and how long following the last scene into the writing view takes. On 2,500 scenes, before O2: the first name after 1.6 s, all 2,500 leaves drawn, 50,000 DOM nodes, 22 MB, scrolling smooth, and following the last scene 0.83 s. O2 done: a section's leaves come in groups of 50, and a group far from the window is a placeholder as tall as its rows, `--leaf-row` times their count, drawn as it comes within 1,500 px and let go past 3,000 px, as in the writing view. A group holding the focus, or anything while a leaf or section is being dragged, is not let go. The outline scrolls with the window, so the observers watch the window; the IntersectionObserver setup is now shared with the writing view in `nearness.rs`. The e2e book helpers moved to `e2e/support/book.ts` and wait for the scenes catalog once, after the whole book is made. A 250-scene book made alongside the rest of the local suite showed how far SQLite falls behind under load: catalog waits elsewhere passed 5 s, while on its own the catalog trails such a burst by 63 ms. So the long-chapter tests share one book, and the one wait that also crosses the 2 s recount throttle gets 15 s. On 2,500 scenes after O2: the first name after 0.55 s, 75 leaves drawn, 8,400 DOM nodes, 5 MB, scrolling smooth, and following the last scene 0.34 to 0.45 s. The writing view is unchanged at 0.58 s. 10,000 waits for the outline's cost per change, which makes seeding it take 35 minutes. O3 done, after the outline's cost per change was cut (P1 to P3 in TODO.md): 10,000 scenes seeded in 16 minutes instead of 35, the last thousand still 3.7 times slower than the first where it was ten times. Measured twice on that book: the outline shows its first name after 2.7 to 3.0 s with 68 leaves drawn, 22,000 DOM nodes and 12 to 15 MB, and following the last scene into the writing view takes 0.83 s where it took 8.5 s. Scrolling the outline mostly holds 16.7 ms a frame, but about 11 of 300 frames pass 50 ms, likely groups of 50 leaves being drawn as they come near. The writing view is as after slice 6: typeable after 1.2 to 1.3 s, 10 to 17 MB, 9 editors, one socket. Not chased yet: what the 2.7 s before the outline's first name is spent on, with only 68 leaves to draw. The outline and the scenes listing both arrive whole, about ten pages of the listing one after another.*
   - **The viewport:** only scenes within a margin of the visible area have a live editor and connection; the rest are placeholders of the right height. A scene's height is measured once it has been shown and kept, so scrolling back does not jump; one never shown is estimated from its length in the listing. Scenes scrolled far away are let go — editor destroyed, connection closed — after their last change has been sent.
   - **Kept working across it:** the cursor walk crosses into a scene that is not mounted yet by mounting it first; where the author left off and following a scene from elsewhere (slice 5) scroll to a placeholder and mount it.
   - **The word count without every scene mounted, first, as 6a.** Each scene stores its word count beside its text, refreshed as the server stores edits (at most every few seconds per scene, or when it is let go), and the scenes listing carries it. The view starts from those counts, so the book's total is right before any scene is mounted. A mounted editor overwrites its scene's count live, as today, and a scene let go keeps its last count, which is never staler than the server's. **One rule for a word**, in one Rust function both the server and the WASM client use, so a count does not jump when a scene mounts. The client then counts the editor's text in Rust rather than in JS, debounced a little. Another author's writing in a scene not mounted here shows on the next open; [M10b](#milestone-10b--the-outlines-live-channel-) can carry counts later. *Chosen 2026-10-14 over the alternative below: at 10,000 scenes the counts are about 300KB of JSON, around 100KB compressed, once per open, next to an outline and a titles listing that already carry every scene.* Split as **6a1** the rule, **6a2** the server's counts. *6a1 done: `libraries/words` holds `words_in`, with no dependencies so it builds for WASM too. A word is a run between whitespace holding a letter or a digit of any script, so a dash or an ellipsis standing alone is not one. It lives in a library because `core` may not depend on `contract`, and a rule duplicated and pinned by a test is only pinned at the inputs the test happens to try. The editor hands its whole text to Rust on every change, which counts it and derives the margin's opening words from the same string. The JS count is gone. No debounce yet: counting a scene's text takes microseconds, so the stress test decides whether it is needed.* **6a2 changed course 2026-10-14: the counts go to a read model, `statistics`, not onto the scene.** The scene does not need its word count, the writing view does, and so will the history of the work and its goals noted below. Those need counts over time, which a read model can grow into and a column on the scene cannot. A read model also gets its own light route, so the view stops depending on the scenes listing, which opens every scene and stops at 500. Split as: **6a2a** scenes announces counts: the live scene counts after storing an edit, at most every 2 seconds with one trailing count so it is never more than 2 seconds behind, and once more before it is let go, and publishes `scene.words.counted { scene, project, words }` through the scenes outbox. Announcing changes nothing on the scene and does not count it up. **6a2b** the `statistics` feature: a word-count catalog on the three backends, a listener that keeps counts and forgets deleted scenes, tombstoned so a late count cannot bring one back, and `GET /api/statistics/word-counts?project=…`. **6a2c** the view loads its counts from there. **6a2d** catching up: a scene nobody edits is never counted, which today is every scene written before this, and from slice 6 on every scene off screen. So a live scene is counted once when it is opened, and a sweep through a project, paged like `scene.sweep.more`, announces a count for each scene, to fill an empty catalog. *6a2a done; the browser test for counts from the server waits as `fixme` until 6a2c. 6a2b done: `features/statistics`, laid out like `appearances`. A `WordCountCatalog` keeps a scene's count with its project and the moment it was counted, on memory, SQLite and PostgreSQL. A count arriving behind a newer one is dropped, and a deleted scene is tombstoned so a late count cannot bring it back. The listener `keep-word-counts` hears `scene.words.counted`, ordered by when it was counted rather than when it arrived, and `scene.deleted`. `GET /api/statistics/word-counts?project=…` answers with each scene's count. The API assembles it as its own feature, with its own database in local mode. 6a2c done: the writing view loads its counts from statistics, beside the scenes listing it still loads for titles. An editor's own count always wins: a count from statistics fills in only scenes no editor has counted yet, since an answer arriving late can be older than what is on screen. The test once set aside proves the whole path, from typing to the live scene, the outbox, the listener and the route. 6a2d changed: counting a scene when it is opened was tried and dropped. Every stored edit is already counted, so in steady state it only repeats a count, and there is no data from before counts were announced. The case it really covered is a count lost when the service stops while one is still waiting. So the service now **settles before it stops**: a feature can hand the wiring a `Settling`, and after the server stops and before the relays do, scenes stores every live scene's unsaved edits and counts every pending count. That also closes a gap that predates counts: an edit the store refused used to be retried only when the scene was let go, so stopping lost it. The sweep, for scenes nobody opens, moves into slice 6. 6a is done.*
   - **Later, if the stress test says so: totals from the server.** A read model joining the outline with each scene's count keeps a total per section and for the book, and the view loads those few hundred totals instead of every scene's count. A scene mounts with its stored count, and the view shows *total + (live count − stored count)* for the scenes being written. It scales past any book size, at the cost of a second read model following both the outline and every scene's text. Take it when the stress test shows the open is slow, or when books far past 10,000 scenes are the aim. *Not needed, 2026-10-19: the 10,000-scene stress book, a million words, opens and is written in without it.*
   - **Statistics catches up on scenes nobody opens.** Once off-screen scenes are no longer opened, counting on open stops reaching them, so a sweep through the project, paged like `scene.sweep.more`, announces a count for every scene. Moved here from 6a2d.
   - **If connections still hurt:** many visible scenes still mean many sockets, so multiplexing every scene over one connection is the follow-up, once the stress test shows it matters.
7. **The tray** — the scene's title and the ideas it draws on, opened from the tab. Slices: **7a** the "This scene" tab and the tray, opened and closed from the tab and closed by Escape, remembered open or shut in the browser, following the scene the cursor is in and showing its title, read-only for now; **7b** renaming in the tray, the margin and the outline following; **7c** the ideas the scene draws on, by name, each followed to its idea and each unlinked there, the names from the ideas listing loaded when the tray first opens; **7d** linking ideas from the tray with the scene page's dialog. *Decided 2026-10-15:* with the cursor in no scene, the tray keeps showing the scene it was last in, and names it, so reaching for the tray never empties it. Opened, it sits in the room to the right of the column and lies over the text only where the window has no such room; the status note and the alarm move left of it. It is styled as `scene-tray`, since `.tray` is the outline's tray of what is not in the book. Links changed elsewhere show on the next open, as sections do, until [M10b](#milestone-10b--the-outlines-live-channel-). *7a done: a "This scene" tab on the right edge opens and shuts the tray, which names the scene the cursor is in, by its title or, faded, its opening words, and keeps the last one when the cursor leaves the scenes. Open or shut is remembered in the browser, for every project alike. Escape shuts it from the tray or its tab and is left to the margin elsewhere. From 82.5rem the tray stands beside the column, ending above the status bar, which keeps its place; narrower, it lies over the text with a shadow; from 60rem down it is fixed to the window and the tab stays at its edge, as the board's tray toggles on a phone. The tray hangs from the top of the view and its contents stay in sight as the book scrolls, so it never covers the masthead. The tab carries a chevron pointing the way the tray will move. 7b done: the tray's heading is the scene's title as a field, empty with the opening words faded in it until the scene has a title. Enter or leaving the field renames it through the same optimistic path as the margin, so the margin follows at once and the outline on its next open, through the catalog; a cleared title gives the opening words back. Enter and Escape return the cursor to where it was in the scene, by the editor's own remembered selection, or where the author left off if the scene was let go meanwhile; Escape drops what was typed and stays out of the tray's own Escape. A rename made in the margin shows in the open tray. 7c done: under the title the tray lists the ideas the scene draws on, by name, each leading to its idea, each with an unlink that takes it out at once and puts it back with a problem if the server refuses. A scene's links are asked for when the tray first shows it, and the names of the project's ideas once, when the tray first opens; both are kept for the visit. A scene without ideas says so. 7d done: "Link an idea" under the list opens the scene page's dialog, which now takes the ideas on offer and what to do with the choice rather than the page's state, so both use it until 7e. It offers only ideas the scene does not draw on yet; the chosen ones join the list at once in the order picked, and leave it again with a problem if the server refuses. The dialog is drawn at the top of the view, not inside the tray, and Escape closes it and leaves the tray open.*
7e. **Scenes outside the book, in the writing view, and the scene page gone.** *Decided 2026-10-15:* following a scene that is not in the book leads to its own page, which is all that page is still for. Instead the writing view gets a **switch**, "The book" | "Not in the book", above the column, each side with its own address (`/write` and `/write/loose`). The second side shows only the scenes outside the book, in the order they were made, with the same editors, margin and tray; its ⋮ offers bringing a scene into the book and deleting it. A part after the book was considered first and dropped: in a long book it lies past every scene, and cut material is a drawer beside the manuscript rather than a chapter after it. So each side keeps its own flow, its own arrow walk and its own word count, and the book's are untouched. Three reviews: **7e1** the switch and the loose side; **7e1b** bringing a loose scene into the book and deleting it, from its ⋮; **7e2** following a loose scene opens that side at it, and where you left off is kept per side; **7e3** the scene page and its route go, its tests covered by the writing view or dropped where they already are. *7e1 done: "The book" and "Not in the book · n" sit above the column as two tabs, each its own address and each current only on its own. The second side lists the scenes outside the book in the order they were made, read from the scenes listing less what the outline holds, with the same slots, editors, margin, tray and loading by nearness as the book. Its arrows walk only its own scenes, its status note counts only them ("n words not in the book"), and writing there leaves where you left off in the book alone. A loose scene's ⋮ offered Rename only until 7e1b. Enter twice begins a scene only in the book: the editor now breaks lines as usual when no one has asked to hear of it, so in a loose scene Enter twice is two line breaks. 7e1b done: a loose scene's ⋮ offers Rename, "Bring into the book…" and "Delete scene". Bringing in asks which section, listing the book's sections in reading order and indented as in the book; the scene goes at the end of the one chosen, and once the server has it the view turns to the book and lands in it. Deleting asks as in the book and deletes the scene outright, since there is no outline to take it out of first. The switch stays out of the way on the book's side while every scene is in the book; on the other side it stays, as the way back. 7e2 done: a scene followed to the writing view opens on its own side whichever address it came by, so a scene outside the book, followed from an idea, the outline or a link, lands on the other side with the cursor in it, and a scene in the book followed to the other side goes back to the book. That side waits for the scenes listing before it lands, so it knows which scenes are its own. Where you left off is kept per side, under a key of its own for the other side, and with nothing left off there it starts in its first scene. Nothing leads to the scene page any more; it is still reachable by its address until 7e3. A scene begun in the view was not in the listing the view opened with, so taking it out of the book, or removing its section, left the other side empty until a reload; the view now adds the scenes it sends out of the book to what it knows. 7e3 done: the scene page is gone, with its route, its state and the views only it used; the link dialog stays, for the tray. Of its fourteen browser tests ten were covered by the writing view already, and four moved there: two tabs writing in one scene converge, leaving the view closes its connection, a key pressed right after moving the cursor acts where the cursor now is, and the link dialog finds ideas by name. "A scene the server does not know says so" went with the page, which was the only thing to say it. Removing its styles showed that ProseMirror's own rules, among them the name above another author's cursor, applied only inside the scene page's `.prose`, so in the writing view another author's cursor had gone unstyled all along; they now apply to every editor, and the cursor test checks that the name floats over the text. Slice 7 is done.*
8. **The book rail**, **the spelling toggle** and **the book's language**. *Noted 2026-10-16:* scenes are already checked by the browser's own speller, since they are editable without a `spellcheck` attribute, while headings turn it off on purpose. But the page says `lang="en"`, and Firefox chooses its dictionary by `lang`, so a German book is checked against English and lights up red; Chrome checks against every language the reader has enabled and suffers less. So a book gets a language of its own, set as `lang` on the writing view, which also serves hyphenation and screen readers. It is the book's, not the interface's: [M14](#milestone-14--the-app-in-five-languages-) chooses the language Weaveling speaks to the author, this the language the book is written in. *Decided 2026-10-16, in three reviews:*
   - **8a, the writing view's settings.** A settings dialog for the writing view, its first setting the spelling toggle, on by default. The writing view's settings are kept in the browser, as the tray's open or shut is, so they outlast a reload; later settings, such as how the text sits on the line (slice 11), go in the same dialog. *8a done: "Writing settings" in the status note opens a dialog holding "Check spelling", on by default and kept in the browser only when switched off; the book sets `spellcheck` once, and every scene's editor inherits it, while headings keep theirs off. Found on the way, through CI failing almost every run since 2026-10-14: a key typed while a scene was being begun could be lost, "The loom stod silent.", because the line being typed into went away as soon as the server had the scene, a moment before the scene's own editor took the words and the cursor. The line now stays until the editor has both, for an empty section's first scene and for the next scene after Enter twice or "New scene"; two tests slow the answer and the processor to catch it.*
   - **8b, a project's details, and the book's language among them.** The language belongs to the book, so it lives on the project, which is the book today, and the writing view sets it as `lang`. With it comes a short description, and a dialog for making a project, where both can be given at the start, and for changing them afterwards; making a project today is a title and nothing else. In two reviews: **8b1** the project learns them, **8b2** the dialogs and the writing view use them. *8b1 done: a project holds `ProjectDetails`, a `BookLanguage` checked as a language tag and kept in the case `lang` expects ("en-GB", "sr-Latn-RS"), unset for projects made before, and a `ProjectDescription` of at most 4,096 characters that keeps its line breaks. Starting a project takes them and records them at once when there is anything to say; `ChangeDetails` changes them and records `DetailsChanged` only when something changed; the snapshot keeps them. Projects kept before details existed are not carried over, as with every schema change before there is real data. `POST /projects` takes a language and a description, both optional, `PUT /projects/{id}/details` changes them, and reading one project answers with them; the list and its catalog are as they were, since the shelf shows names only. Changed details are published as `project.details.changed`, as every other fact about a project is, though nothing listens yet. 8b2 done: Create beside the title field now opens "New project", with the title as typed, the book's language preset from the browser among thirteen and "Not set", and a short description; Enter in the title creates it, Escape or Cancel keeps the title and creates nothing, and a refused title leaves the dialog open to put it right. The writing view reads its project's language as it opens and sets it as `lang` on the book, or leaves the page's when there is none. Dialogs taller than the window could not be reached at their foot, which the new one, the tallest yet, showed on a short window: the overlay now scrolls and centres a dialog only when it fits. Changing the details later, from inside the project, is 8b3. 8b3 done: the masthead holds the open project for every view, fetched once as it is entered, and shows its real name rather than one read back from the address. Beside the name, a cogwheel opens "Project details", the same title, language and description as "New project", now one form shared by both; saving renames only when the title changed, then changes the details, and the masthead and the book's `lang` follow at once. A refused title is told in the dialog, which stays; Escape or Cancel keeps everything as it was.* The author's name was considered and left out: a book can have several authors, and once there are accounts their names come with them. The name printed on the book, which may be a pen name or two names, is a matter for export's title page, not for a project's details.
   - **8c, the book rail.** The whole outline in a drawer, mirroring the scene tray on the other side: sections shown and nested, each collapsible, and their scenes and ideas folded away until a section is opened; one button folds or opens everything. Choosing an entry goes there as following a scene does. On the side of the scenes not in the book it is not there at all. In two reviews: **8c1** the rail with sections and scenes, **8c2** the ideas pinned to sections, whose titles the writing view does not fetch yet. *8c2 reshaped 2026-10-19: a section's ideas go in the tray on the right rather than in the rail, as a scene's do: the rail stays the contents of the book's text, and the tray shows whatever the cursor is in. In two reviews: **8c2a** the tray follows the cursor into a heading and shows the section's pinned ideas, **8c2b** pinning and unpinning them from there, as the outline does.* *8c2a done: putting the cursor in a heading turns the tray to that section, its tab and its name now "This section", showing the section's title and its pinned ideas in the outline's order, each leading to its idea; a section with none says they are pinned in the outline. The idea titles come from the project's ideas the writing view already loads for linking them to scenes, so nothing new is asked of the server. A heading still being typed, not yet a section, leaves the tray as it was.* *Since it no longer shows only scenes, the scene tray is the writing view's **inspector** in the code, `writing::inspector` beside the board's idea inspector, and its kept state moved to `weaveling.inspector`; on screen it still reads "This scene" or "This section", and its handle "Resize the tray".* *8c2b done: under a section's pinned ideas, "Pin an idea" opens the picker scenes link ideas with, worded for pinning, "Pin ideas" and "Pin 2", offering the project's ideas not pinned anywhere in the outline yet, since an idea has one place there; the chosen ones go after whatever the section already holds, in the order they were ticked. Each pinned idea has "Unpin …" beside it, which takes it out of the outline, and it is offered again. A section with none says only "No ideas pinned here.", the pinning being right below.* *8c1 done: a "Contents" tab on the left opens the rail, kept open or closed across visits as the scene tray is. At first it shows the sections, nested, with every section's own scenes folded away; a section folds and unfolds by its chevron, and unfolding a folded one shows its scenes too. "Unfold all" opens everything and becomes "Fold all". Choosing a section puts the cursor in its heading, and choosing a scene goes there however far down it is, drawing it first if it was let go. On a wide window the book moves aside to make room; narrower, the rail lies over it, and on a narrow window choosing an entry puts it away. Escape inside it closes it.*
   - **8d, one tray for the whole app.** The board and the outline still have the older tray for their ideas and scenes, while the writing view's drawers grew a tab on the edge, a handle to resize them kept per drawer and clamped to the window, lying over the page rather than pushing it, and one at a time where the window is narrow. These become one shared drawer, and the board's and the outline's trays move onto it, so every tray in the app behaves the same. *8d done: one `Drawer` in `drawer/` gives every tray its tab on the edge, its handle with the width kept and clamped to the window, its open or shut state kept, a × and Escape. The writing view's two drawers and the board's and outline's tray are all built on it; the board and the outline share one tray, open at first on a wide window, so it stays as left between them. In the writing view the drawers lie over the book; beside the board and the outline the tray takes its own room on a wide window instead, since lying over a canvas would hide the zoom controls and put "where the author is looking" off-centre, and lies over them only on a narrow one.*
   - **8e, back to where you left off, when asked.** Opening the writing view goes straight to the place last written in, which surprises more than it helps. It opens at the start of the book instead and offers to go back to where the author left off; the offer goes once it is taken or the author starts writing elsewhere. Following a link to a scene still goes to that scene. *8e done: with a place left off on the side being opened, the book opens at its start with nothing chosen, and "Back to where you left off" floats above the status bar with the scene's name; it lands where the cursor was, however far down, and goes once taken or once the cursor is put anywhere in the book. With nothing left off, the cursor lands in the first scene as before, and a scene followed to is gone to directly, with no offer.*
   - **Later: the offer only when it leads somewhere.** *Noted 2026-10-18:* "Back to where you left off" shows even when the scene left off in is already in sight at the top of the window, as it is when that is the book's first scene, and then taking it moves nothing. The offer should show only while the scene it goes to is out of sight. *Done 2026-10-18: the offer looks whether its scene's place is in the window when it is made, and again on every scroll and resize; while the scene is in sight it steps aside, since the scene is there to click into, and it comes back once the scene is out of sight, until it is taken or let go. Six tests had left off in a scene right beside the first and gone back by the offer: the four about going back now leave off a dozen scenes down, or in a short window outside the book, and the two that only needed the cursor back click into the scene in sight.*
   - **8f, a new project opens at once.** *Noted 2026-10-17:* creating a project from the "New project" dialog leaves the author on the list of projects, where the next thing they do is find it and open it. Creating it goes into it instead, at the view a project opens at today; the list has it when they come back. *8f done: once the server has made it, the new project opens on its board. Only a project made while the list is open does that: the workspace keeps the last one made for as long as the app runs, so coming back to the list later stays on the list.*
9. **Focus mode.** *Planned 2026-10-17.* A mode the author goes into and out of, by Ctrl+Shift+F or a small button in the status bar. It is not remembered: every visit to the writing view starts out of it. Escape stays with menus and drawers. It quiets the page rather than taking the screen: no fullscreen, which is one F11 away. In three reviews:
   - **9a, the mode itself.** The masthead shrinks to one quiet line, the project and the section being written in, without the views, the theme or the details; the drawers' tabs and buttons go; the "+" and "⋮" show only on hover; the status bar fades back until it is hovered. Leaving the mode brings it all back as it was. *9a done: Ctrl+Shift+F or the focus button at the start of the status note goes in and out; `body` carries `focused` while the writing view is in the mode, and the view takes it off as it goes, so a reload or another view is never left in it. The masthead gives way to a line of its own above the book, the project and the section the cursor is in; the drawers and their tabs go and come back as they were; the menus in the margin show only for the line hovered, even on a narrow window where they otherwise always show; and the status bar and note fade to a quarter until hovered or reached by the keyboard.*
   - **9b, section names in the margin: tried and dropped, 2026-10-17.** Drawn in the margin beside the scene titles, the names read worse than as headings in the column, so they stay where they are. What came of it instead: in focus mode the prose is set larger, at 1.45rem against 1.25rem, in a column widened to match so a line keeps about as many words.
   - **9c, fading back.** All but the paragraph the cursor is in dims softly, and follows the cursor as it moves; nothing dims while nothing is being written. *9c done: each scene's editor marks the block its cursor is in, and the stylesheet dims everything else in the book, headings, scene titles and the lines between scenes among them, but only while focused and while that mark is in the editor that has the focus. The first cut tracked focus in the editor itself, by a transaction on focus; that transaction ran before the browser had moved its caret and put the selection back at the scene's start, so text typed after landing, arriving across a scene's edge or a handover went in at the front. ProseMirror's own `ProseMirror-focused` says which editor has the focus without one.*
   - **Typewriter scrolling**, as its own setting, is [M13e](#milestone-13e--last-touches-to-the-writing-view-)'s first slice.
10. **Selecting across scenes.** Stacked editors cannot carry a text selection from one into the next, so selection beyond a scene becomes *scene-level*, the way block editors do it: Shift+Up at a scene's top, or Shift+Down at its end, selects whole scenes as highlighted blocks and each further press takes in the next; Ctrl+A selects the scene, pressed again the whole book. Copy copies their text in reading order. Cut and delete would be structural — scenes leaving the book — so they wait. In reviews: **10a** choosing scenes; **10b** headings in the choosing; **10c** Delete; **10d** copying; **10e** choosing with the mouse, a drag that leaves one scene or heading going on into the next ones, as a drag across paragraphs does, if the stacked editors can be made to allow it; **10f** moving scenes by drag and drop, a scene, or the scenes chosen, taken by a handle in the margin and dropped between two others, in its own section or another, as the outline already moves them, with the place it will land shown while dragging; *noted 2026-10-17, done the same day: a grip beside a scene's ⋮, shown on hover, picks the scene up, or every chosen scene when it is one of them; the gap nearest the pointer is where it lands, marked by the accent line the "+" gaps already draw, and the book scrolls on at the window's edge. Let go, the scenes are attached there in their order, which on the server moves them, since attaching detaches first; let go right below itself, nothing is sent; Escape puts it back. Scenes outside the book have no grip, having no place to go but into it. On a touchscreen the grip takes the finger rather than the page scrolling (`touch-action: none`); without it the browser began a scroll and cancelled the drag, and a drag cancelled by the browser now puts its scenes back.*; a far scene's text is not in the browser, but reading one scene answers with its text. *Decided 2026-10-17:* chosen scenes are drawn as the browser's own text selection across them, not as tinted blocks, which read heavy. A heading is a step of its own, so Shift+Up and Shift+Down take in scenes and headings one at a time in reading order, and a choosing can begin or end at one. Delete asks first, in a dialog naming what goes, which offers both *Take out of the book*, sending the scenes to those outside it, and *Delete for good*, which cannot be undone; a section whose heading is chosen is removed as "Remove section" removes it, its nested sections with it, and its scenes go the way the dialog chose. *10c done: Delete or Backspace while scenes are chosen asks "Remove what is chosen?", counting the scenes and sections; Cancel or Escape leaves them chosen. Taken out of the book, the scenes wait among those outside it; deleted for good, they are gone from the server too. A chosen heading takes its section, the sections inside it and all their scenes along.
