Outside the book the dialog offers only deleting for good.* *10d done: copying while scenes are chosen, from the keyboard or the browser's menu, puts their text on the clipboard in reading order: a scene's paragraphs a line apart, scenes and headings a blank line apart, each heading as a line of its own. A scene drawn takes its text from its editor; one far down the book, not drawn, is read from the server first and the clipboard written once it has arrived. Chrome draws the selection across the editors but gives no text for it, so the copy is made by the view rather than the browser. Tested in Chrome only; Firefox, where the drawn selection copies by itself, wants a manual check.* *10e done: a mouse drag that starts in a scene or on a heading and leaves it chooses from there to the scene or heading under the pointer, taking in more as it goes on and giving them back as it comes back, and the choosing stays once the button is let go, for the keys, Delete and copying alike. A drag within one scene is the browser's as before. Shift+click in another scene or heading chooses from the one the cursor is in to the one clicked, and with scenes already chosen moves the far end there. Firefox goes on dragging its own selection even once the book has the focus, so while a drag chooses, the drawn selection is drawn again after every move and on letting go; Chromium did not need that, which is how the first cut passed its tests and failed in Firefox. The selection tests were run in Playwright's Firefox too, through a passing config made for the purpose, all but the copying ones, since Playwright cannot let Firefox read the clipboard. The suite itself still runs in Chromium only.* *10a done: Shift+Down on a scene's last line, or Shift+Up on its first, chooses that scene, and each further press takes in the next one that way or gives the last one back; Ctrl+A selects the scene's text as before, and pressed again chooses every scene, the book's or those outside it. While scenes are chosen the book holds the keys rather than an editor, so typing changes nothing; Escape or an arrow without Shift lets them go and puts the cursor back where it was in the last one reached, and clicking into a scene lets them go too. Chosen scenes are drawn as the browser's own selection running across them, replacing whatever text was selected before, and one taken in from further down is brought into sight and drawn. 10b done: headings are steps of their own, so a choosing can begin or end at one, Escape on a heading puts the cursor into it, and a scene already selected whole as text takes in the next one at the first press beyond it rather than choosing itself again. Found alongside: Ctrl+Up and Ctrl+Down (Alt on a Mac) now go by paragraph, to its start or end and then to the next one's, and on into the neighbouring scene at the edge; with Shift they select that way, and at a scene's edge carry on into choosing scenes. The browser did neither well across wrapped lines.*
11. **How the text sits on the line.** A setting of the author's, kept in the browser rather than in the book: *ragged right*, as now and as reading on screen favours, or *justified* like a printed page — and justified only with hyphenation (`hyphens: auto`), since a 680px column justified without it opens gaps between words. Hyphenation needs the text's language, so the flow carries the book's `lang`, which [M14](#milestone-14--the-app-in-five-languages-) makes a choice. `text-wrap: pretty` in both, so paragraphs end without a lone word. Alignment *inside* the text — a centred epigraph, a signature set right — is formatting stored in the scene, and belongs with formatting, not here. *Done 2026-10-18: "Justified text" in the writing settings, off by default and kept in the browser beside "Check spelling". Justified, a scene's paragraphs are set flush on both sides and hyphenated in the book's `lang`, which the flow already carried; headings stay as they are. `text-wrap: pretty` holds in both. A book with no language takes the page's English, as its quotation marks do, so it is hyphenated as English.*
12. **Emphasis in the prose.** *Noted 2026-10-16:* the editor's schema has had emphasis and strong since M6, but no key makes them, so italics can only arrive by pasting, and a novel needs them all the time, for thoughts, titles and stress. Ctrl+I and Ctrl+B (Cmd on a Mac) toggle them, the menus can name the keys once they show keys at all, and pasted text keeps them. Decide at the same time what else a manuscript needs inside a scene, a break within a scene, an epigraph or a letter set apart, and leave everything else out on purpose: the prose is not a word processor, and every form allowed here is one that export has to set. *Done 2026-10-17: Ctrl+I and Ctrl+B toggle italics and bold on what is selected, or on what is typed next, and both are kept with the scene, so they survive a reload. Lexend has no italic of its own, so the browser slants it; that reads well enough at the size the prose is set in, and an italic face can come with the typography of export. Bold is set at 500, the heaviest Lexend loaded, since the prose is light at 300 and "bolder" alone made 400, which Firefox drew hardly heavier than the text around it.*
13. **Dialogue and its quotation marks.** *Noted 2026-10-16:* many authors set dialogue in guillemets, »like this« in German books, « comme ça » in French, and others in “curly” or „low-high“ quotes; which marks a book uses is house style and belongs to its language. So a book has a **quotation style**, »…«, „…“, “…” or « … », defaulting from the book's language (slice 8) and changeable. **Typing a plain `"` becomes the book's mark**, opening or closing by where it falls, as word processors' smart quotes do, so nobody has to know how to type » or «. **A key for a line of dialogue**, perhaps Ctrl+Shift+2 where German keyboards keep `"`, puts the pair in with the cursor between them, or wraps what is selected. Later, with [threads through the text](#after-phase-3-sketch-only), the same key can ask who speaks and set the line's `spoken_by` there and then; and changing a whole book's style, when a publisher asks for „…“ instead of »…«, is a [find and replace across the book](#after-phase-3-sketch-only). *Decided 2026-10-17: five styles, »…« (pointing-in), „…“ (low-high), “…” (curly), « … » with narrow spaces (spaced-out) and «…» (pointing-out); German books default to »…«; a typed `'` becomes ’ in every book; the key for a line of dialogue is Ctrl+Shift+2. In three reviews: **13a** the style on the project, **13b** the marks put in as `"` and `'` are typed, **13c** the key. 13a done: a project's details hold a quotation style or none, and none means the book's language decides, so changing the language changes the marks until a style is chosen; the server keeps it with the language and description, in the events, the snapshot, the API and what is published. "Project details" and "New project" offer it, the first choice naming the marks the language would give.* *13b done: in a scene, a typed `"` becomes the book's opening mark at the start of a paragraph or after a space, a bracket or a dash, and its closing mark anywhere else; a typed `'` becomes ’. Each scene's editor is told the marks as it opens, and every open one again when the style or the language changes, so a style chosen while writing is used from the next mark on. One Ctrl+Z takes a mark back with the rest of what was typed; headings and titles keep what is typed for now.* *13b redone the same day: marks typed as characters stayed in the old style when the style changed, so what is said became a **speech mark** in the scene's text instead, like italics, and the marks are drawn around it in the book's style. Changing the style redraws every mark already set. A typed `"` begins what is said and the next ends it, over a selection it marks the selection, and the opening mark shows from the moment it is typed. Copying puts the marks in as characters. Left open: marks typed as characters before this stay characters, and far scenes copied from the server come without marks until the server writes them too.* *Both settled by 13d: the marks are characters again, tagged, and since A2 the server rewrites them in every scene, so a far scene copied from the server has them as written.* *Redone again 2026-10-18, as **13d**: a mark that is only drawn is no letter, so the cursor could not stop before or after it, and LanguageTool, the spell checker, screen readers and the server's text never saw it. So a typed `"` is the real mark again, opening after a space, a bracket, a dash or at a paragraph's start and closing elsewhere, and it carries a `quote` mark saying which it is, which is what lets a change of style find exactly the marks to change. Over a selection `"` and Ctrl+Shift+2 put marks around it, and again take them away. **13d-A1 done:** every open editor rewrites its tagged marks in the book's style once it has synced and whenever the style changes, outside the undo history so Ctrl+Z does not take a style back; a scene not opened since keeps the old style on the server until it is. The drawing by CSS, the widget for a mark just begun and the clipboard's own serializers went, since the marks are text now; pasted marks keep their tag. Scenes written while marks were only drawn keep their words and lose their marks: their `speech` mark is not converted, as there is no real data yet to carry over, but it stays in the schema, because y-prosemirror deletes a whole run of text whose mark the schema does not know. **13d-A2, next:** the server rewrites every scene of the book when the style changes, so nothing keeps the old style anywhere. In three reviews: **A2a** the scenes feature goes through the book in batches, as the deletion sweep does, rewriting the tagged marks in each scene's document and keeping how far it got; **A2b** saving a new style in Project details first asks, naming how many scenes with dialogue it will touch, then shows a real progress bar, so many of so many, until every scene is done; **A2c** the dialog may be closed meanwhile, and the progress goes on in the status bar.* *A2a-1 done: the server needs the marks themselves to rewrite them, and only the browser knew them; the projects feature knew the styles by name alone. `libraries/quotation` now holds the five styles, their names, the style a language brings and the marks each opens and closes with, for the browser and the server alike, as `words` holds the rule for a word. The projects feature takes the style and its error from there instead of keeping its own, and the browser's own copy is gone.* *A2a-2 done: a scene can restyle itself. `Scene::restyled` finds the runs tagged `quote` in its document, a mark set partly in italics still being one mark, rewrites each one not in the style asked for, from the back so positions hold, keeps its tag, and hands back the change, or nothing when every mark already matches. The scenes core declares `SceneRestyler` and `LiveScenes` keeps it: it joins the scene, so an open one is restyled where its editors are and they hear it as something that just happened, and a closed one is loaded for it and let go by the sweep as usual, then stores the change. Only one side may rewrite a mark, or two insertions of the same new mark would both survive the merge, so the browser's own restyling goes when the server's begins, in A2a-3, and open scenes then change through the sync like any other change.* *A2a-3a done: `project.details.changed` comes with every edit of the details and says nothing of what they were before, so a listener would have to go through the book on every new description. So the project now also records `QUOTATION_CHANGED` when the marks a book shows change, whether a style was chosen or a new language brought one, and publishes it as `project.quotation.changed` carrying the style's name. Choosing the style the language would bring anyway changes nothing to be seen and records no such event, and neither does starting a project, since a new book has no scenes to rewrite.* *A2a-3b done: the scenes feature hears `project.quotation.changed` and goes through the book's scenes a hundred at a time, as the deletion sweep does, restyling each through `LiveScenes`, so open scenes change where their editors are and closed ones where they are kept; a full batch carries itself on with `scene.quotation.restyle.more`, naming the style, the change and the last scene done. A scene deleted meanwhile is passed over. Should the style change again while a book is still being restyled, the older restyling must not overwrite what the newer one did, so each restyling task claims the book from `QuotationRestylingTasks`, which remembers the latest change per project by the project's version: an older change or its continuations stop where they are; a change heard again goes through the book again, so a redelivery after a crash still finishes it; and a continuation heard after a restart, when the memory is gone, claims the book again rather than leave it half restyled. On SQLite and Postgres the claim is kept in a `quotation_restyling_tasks` table beside the scene catalog, one row per book, written only when the change is not older than the one it holds, so a restart forgets nothing. The browser no longer restyles anything: it only types new marks in the book's style. Left open: a mark typed in the old style in the moment before the change reaches the server keeps that style.* *A2b-1 done: a restyling task now keeps how far it got. Beginning one counts the book's scenes first, a batch at a time, and every batch adds what it did; the last batch, short or empty, says it is finished, since scenes deleted meanwhile would keep the count from ever reaching the total. Only the change holding the book counts, so an older change neither resets nor adds to the newer one's progress. `GET /api/scenes/quotation-restylings/{project}` tells it as the change, the total, how many are done and whether it is finished, never more done than the total, and not found for a book never restyled. The dialog saves against the project's version it holds, and the new style is recorded after it, so it waits for a restyling whose change is newer than that version; the version saving answers with will not do, as a snapshot taken right after takes a version of its own.* *A2b-2a done: the dialog did not in fact save against a version; the client knew none, and the server took whatever came last. Now a project as the client knows it carries its version, the details are saved with `If-Match` against it, or against the version a rename in the same save just reached, and a project changed elsewhere meanwhile is refused with 412 and told in the dialog, "This project was changed elsewhere meanwhile", rather than overwritten. That is what lets the progress bar wait for a restyling newer than the version saved against.* *A2b-2b done: saving details that change the marks the book shows, by a chosen style or by a language that brings other marks, first asks: "Changing the quotation marks from “…” to „…“ rewrites them in every scene of the book. In a long book that takes a while.", with Back and "Change the marks"; details that leave the marks as they look, a description, Danish for a German book, are saved as before without asking. Nothing is saved before the change is confirmed. Confirmed, the dialog stays open and asks the server every half second how far the restyling has come, showing a bar and "n of m scenes", the bar running without a value until the restyling newer than the version saved against has begun, so an earlier one finished long ago never ends it; finished, it says "Every scene now quotes „…“." and offers Close. The number of scenes is not named in the question, as planned, since it would cost a listing of its own and the bar names it a moment later. Found on the way: the dialog was built from the project as held, tracked, so keeping the saved project rebuilt it from scratch; it is now built once, when it opens.* *A2c postponed 2026-10-19: with 10,000 scenes the whole book was restyled in about three seconds, so a dialog that may be closed meanwhile and progress in the status bar buy nothing yet. See [work in progress in the status bar](#later--polish-and-what-can-wait) under Later.* *13c done: Ctrl+Shift+2 (Cmd on a Mac) does what a typed `"` does: it begins or ends a line of dialogue at the cursor, and over a selection it makes the selection dialogue or plain again. It is bound to the key's place, not its character, so it is the same key on a German and an English keyboard.*

**Later: where "Write" sits among the views.** It is the heart of the app, yet it comes last in the masthead, after Board, Outline and Ideas, because it was added last. Worth reconsidering whether it should come first — and with it, which view a project opens on, which today is the board. Not urgent; revisit once the writing view is something an author can actually work in. *Decided 2026-10-17: it should.* The writing view is the heart of the app, so "Write" comes first among the views and a project opens on it, a new one included ([8f](#milestone-13c--the-writing-view-) opens it on the board today); Board, Outline and Ideas follow. *Done the same day: the masthead reads Write, Board, Outline, Ideas; the board moved to `/projects/{p}/board`, and a project's own address, which the list and a new project go to, hands over to the writing view, replacing itself in the history so the back button still leaves the project.*

---

### Milestone 13d — Simplify ✅

**Goal:** a lighter codebase before the next big step.

**Build:** a look at the project as a whole once M13c is done, rather than one slice at a time: what grew more layers, crates, abstractions or special cases than it needs, what is duplicated across features and backends, and what could be removed or merged. Each finding is weighed, and the ones worth it are taken in reviewable slices.

**Done when:** the findings are written down, and the ones chosen are done with every suite still passing.

**Findings, 2026-10-18.** Four surveys across the features, the libraries and backends, the web client and the tests, with every claim counted before it was written here. Taken in this order, each a review of its own:

1. **Dead code and wrong boundaries.**
   - Publishing straight from the event store, from before the outbox: `PublishingEventStore`, `EventPublisher`, `NoopEventPublisher`, `MessagingEventPublisher` and the four `*EventPublisher`s in the features are used by one ideas test only. That test moves onto the outbox, and the rest goes, about 650 lines; ARCHITECTURE still describes the old path.
   - All 19 listeners restate `delivery()`, which is the trait's default.
   - Unused dependencies and re-exports, and two `Default` impls in the client nobody calls.
   - `eventsourcing::testing` and `registry::suite` are public and built into every binary; they go behind `cfg(test)`.
   *Done 2026-10-18: the old publish path is gone and the ideas sweep test uses a plain store, since it only reads what the sweep itself publishes; ARCHITECTURE, CONVENTIONS and both libraries' READMEs describe the outbox path instead. Thirteen dependencies nothing used are dropped. `Overlays::none()` and `Workspace::listing()` replace `new()` plus a `Default` nobody called. Both suites are test-only now, which turned up a sample command no test sends. `delivery()` is left for step 2, which rewrites every listener anyway. The re-exports stay: those nobody outside names are still types and constants in other items' signatures, and the listeners' helpers change in step 2. Left for step 4: the in-memory store has no test that only what its mapping publishes reaches the outbox, which the SQLite and Postgres stores have.*
2. **Listeners say only what they do.** Each of the 19 listeners repeats the same frame: a name parsed with `expect`, a subscription parsed with `expect`, `when_refused`, `handle` mapping errors to `NotHandled`, and mostly an error enum only so `?` works. A small trait in `messaging` takes a listener's name, routing keys and refusal as constants and its work as one method returning a boxed failure, and makes a `Listener` of it; names and keys are checked once, in a test. About 450 lines.
   *Done 2026-10-18: `messaging::Handler` takes `NAME`, `LISTENS_TO` and `WHEN_REFUSED` as constants and the work as `take_in`, returning a boxed `Failure`, and a blanket impl makes a kept `Listener` of it, which also settles `delivery()` from step 1. All 19 listeners are handlers now; an error enum stayed where its variants say something of their own and went where every variant was transparent. The `when_*` and `every_event` helpers went with them, and the tests that used them read the contract's patterns, or a listener's own `LISTENS_TO`, which showed the outline catalog also hears discards. About 500 lines fewer. Breaking subscriptions found three that no test needed: the boards and outline catalogs forgetting a swept board or outline, and the ideas sweep asking for its next batch; each has a test through the wired dispatcher now.*
3. **Wiring says only what is the feature's own.** Each feature builds its `Ports` in `Ports::{in_memory, postgres, sqlite}` and `Feature` only forwards to them; the bodies move into `Feature`, which takes the `Context` whole rather than its publisher and clock one by one. The event store with its outbox, codec and `message_for`, and its two migrations, are wired the same in boards, ideas, outline and projects, and become one helper in `libraries/wiring`. About 60 lines a feature.
   *Done 2026-10-18: each feature builds its ports in `Feature::in_memory`, `on_postgres` and `on_sqlite` from the `Context`, and the `Ports` constructors are gone; `Ports`, `wire` and `service` stay public for the feature tests. `wiring::EventSourced` builds an event store enqueuing into its outbox for boards, ideas, outline and projects. The schema lists stay written out per feature, since they are where a feature says which tables it lays down. About 210 lines fewer, and the wiring crates no longer need `messaging`. Breaking the in-memory pairing went unnoticed, because the feature tests build their own ports and only SQLite and PostgreSQL had a journey through the service; a journey in memory now carries an idea to its catalog.*
4. **Rust tests written once, not once per backend.** `services/api/tests/on_sqlite.rs` and `on_postgres.rs` become one journey suite run on both, which also gives Postgres the scene-deletion journey it lacks today. The outbox gets the conformance suite CONVENTIONS asks for, and the scenes store and eventsourcing backend tests move into their suites. About 1,800 lines.
   Four reviews: **4a** the service's journeys, **4b** the outbox's suite, **4c** the scenes store, **4d** the event store, with the in-memory enqueueing step 1 left open. *4a done: `services/api/tests/journeys` holds the ten journeys once, over a `Backend` that sets up, hands out its `Storage`, reads rows and cleans up, and a `Journey` that speaks the domain (`project`, `idea`, `scene`, `outline`, `section`, `attach`, `link`); `journeys!(OnSqlite)` and `journeys!(OnPostgres)` run them, and the restart from files stays SQLite's own. PostgreSQL gains the scene deletion it lacked. 1,638 lines became 787, and removing a listener from the wiring still fails the journey that needs it.*
   *4b done: `outbox/src/suite.rs` holds the thirteen cases every outbox owes, over a `Workbench` that sets up with a publisher and a clock, enqueues beside a write that commits or rolls back, says which entries are published and ages them. A `Switchboard` publisher is told to refuse or to listen, so each backend keeps one outbox instead of a relay per publisher. PostgreSQL and SQLite run all thirteen, and the in-memory outbox now runs the twelve that need no transaction, where it had three tests of its own. SQLite's second test file, which repeated the enqueueing, is folded into one; its waking and polling and each backend's schema stay its own. Breaking the claim, the batch size or the clean-up on any backend fails the suite.*
   *4c done: the scene store's ten row-level cases, a snapshot on create, compaction and where it stops, refused updates writing nothing, deleting taking updates and links with it, and reloading by a store that never saw the writes, live once in its suite over a `Tables` workbench: a second store on the same tables, `compact`, the rows' snapshot flags and a count. PostgreSQL and SQLite each keep only the index plan, which is their own SQL. The test that hands a case the whole bench is `bench_case!` now, since `enqueuing_case!` no longer said what it does. Moving the compaction threshold by one on either backend fails the suite.*
   *4d done: the event store's suite gained three enqueueing cases every store runs, the in-memory one included, which closes what step 1 left open: publishable events wait in the outbox, a correction or a snapshot leaves nothing, a refused append leaves nothing. Eight more run on the SQL backends over a `Tables` workbench that closes the pool, writes an unreadable row, pushes a version below zero, reads the columns, lays the schema down again and reads with another codec: two writers racing, a batch colliding halfway, an unreachable backend, an unreadable event, the columns, a second codec, the schema twice, a negative version. PostgreSQL's `announcing_tests.rs` is gone, its rollback test being the colliding batch's outbox check over again; each backend keeps only its schema catalog and, for SQLite, the nanosecond instant and the writer kept waiting. Stopping any store from enqueueing fails the suite. The test-only warning about `Corrected` from step 1 is gone with it, since the in-memory cases now use it.*
5. **Test helpers are shared only when they carry weight.** A helper that hides setup a test is not about earns its place and is reused; one that hides what is being tested goes back inline. So one-liners such as `at(seconds)` (53 copies) and `an_author()` (17) stay where they are, and the publisher stand-in written nine times becomes one `FakePublisher` in `messaging`, renamed from `Overheard`, which did not say what it is.
   *Done 2026-10-18: `messaging::FakePublisher` keeps what it is handed and refuses once told to, and nothing else; whatever a test wants from it, the last message, the ids, the count, it reads off `heard()`. It replaces seven publishers written by hand, in the outbox's suite and relay tests, the event store's and the scene store's suites and in the ideas, scenes and outline tests, and two test crates no longer need `async-trait`. The one-liners stay where they are, as decided.*

Found on the way: a board's and an outline's tombstones guard not only late messages but new commands, a pin or an attachment of something already gone, which are let go of when indexed. Every tombstone in the app is now forgotten 31 days after it was laid.* *4f-3 done the same day, boards and outlines alike: opening a project's board or outline, reading it and following it ask `Read`, every command `Write`, an author's, the board or outline read first and its project asked about, as the ideas do; 404 for whoever may not read, 403 `not-an-author` for a reader, each contract naming it, so the client already words it. The live routes read what is followed before they upgrade, to ask and to say where it stands: each feature now has its own handler, hearing first and reading after, and `live::followed_from`, used by these two alone, was taken out for `streaming_from`, now exported. A board or outline never opened is now 404 to follow, where it was a socket telling nothing; the client opens before it follows. A reader opening a project's board or outline that nobody opened starts it, as before. On a server Grace finds Ada's board and outline not there, opening and reading. 8 new tests and two journeys on both databases; 22 mutants by hand, all caught. The workspace: 2614 passed.* *The two full browser runs after it each failed the same two tests on their retry too, `the alarm and the changes it holds speak German` (speech.spec.ts) and `the status note makes room for the alarm beside it` (writing-status.spec.ts): not 4f-3 but slice 1 of the tombstones, whose `may_be_tried_again` the alarm's own spec gave its stand-in refusals while these two specs, faking refusals of their own, were missed, so the client could not read the list. Both given the field; alone they pass. Flaky the same runs, passing on their retry: without accounts `moving into the next scene brightens its paragraph and fades the one left` (focus.spec.ts), `confirmed, the dialog follows the book being rewritten until every scene is done` (project-details.spec.ts), `the languages a machine translated say so, in the language being spoken` (speech.spec.ts), `a card is moved with a thumb, and stays where it was put` (touch-board.spec.ts) and `Ctrl+Shift+2 begins a line of dialogue and ends it again` (writing-text.spec.ts); with accounts `a project's details are fetched once while moving between its views` (writing-landing.spec.ts). Without accounts 721 passed, with 721 and 4 skipped.* *Renamed the same day at the user's word, so a tombstone reads as one wherever it is kept: `gone_subjects`, `gone_places`, `gone_scenes`, `gone_ideas`, `gone_attachments` and `scene_catalog_gone` are now `subject_tombstones`, `place_tombstones`, `scene_tombstones` in statistics and in the scenes' catalog, `idea_tombstones` and `attachment_tombstones`, edited into each `0001`; `gone_at` is `laid_at`, as `forget_laid_before` has it; `mark_gone` is `lay_tombstone`, `gone_but_held` `held_though_tombstoned`, and the in-memory catalogs keep `tombstones`. `forget_subject`, `forget_place`, `forget_scene` and the scenes' `forget` kept their names, since they forget what was held as well as lay the tombstone. The notes above keep the old names, as they were written. Nothing else changed: the workspace, 2614 passed, the catalogs' suites on all three backends and the journeys on both databases among them.* *4f-4 cut the same day, the scenes being most of it: **4f-4a** statistics; **4f-4b** the scenes' REST, their list's channel and the restyling's progress; **4f-4c** the editing socket `/scenes/sync`, whose `Write` against `Read` is the user's to decide. 4f-4a done: asking a project's word counts and following them ask `Read`, 404 for whoever may not, the live route standing on `followed_if` and `ReadersOfTheProject`; the feature takes no command. The REST route has no service to ask through, so it asks the `Authorizer` itself. 3 new tests; 6 mutants by hand, all caught.* *Decided the same day for 4f-4c, option A of three: a reader attaches to `/scenes/sync` and is told every change, but the server drops what a reader sends to change the text, letting awareness through, so who is where shows for readers too; the client makes the editor read-only for a reader, in a slice of its own with the other controls a reader may not use. Refusing readers the socket would have needed a second way to show them the text, and closing it on a stray keystroke was judged too harsh.* *4f-4b done: the scenes, not event-sourced, had no agent in their service at all; now `SceneService` asks the `Authorizer`, `create`, `retitle`, `link`, `unlink` and `delete` taking who acts and asking `Write` on the scene's project, the scene loaded first, and `read` asking `Read`; `open` and `apply` stay unasked for the editing socket, which asks for itself in 4f-4c, and the project's sweep deletes as `Agent::System`. The list, the restyling's progress and both their live routes read the catalog and the tasks directly, so they ask `Read` themselves, as the statistics do. 404 `not-found` for whoever may not read, 403 `not-an-author` for a reader; a reader asking the progress of a book never restyled is told so, not that the project is not there. Some forty calls in the tests given `Agent::Local` by a script reading the compiler's errors. The sweep's tests now run with an authorizer letting only the system and one account through, so a sweep acting as anyone else fails them, which a mutant showed nothing caught before. On a server Grace finds Ada's scenes not there, listing, reading and creating. 4 new tests and the journey on both databases; 20 mutants by hand, all caught.* *4f-4c done, option A: `/scenes/sync` takes who opened it, and joining a scene asks `SceneService::access` of the scene's project, author, reader or stranger; a stranger, or anyone while the authorizer is down, is refused the scene by name, as for one not there. A reader's frames are read before they reach the scene, and those that would change the text, an update or the second step of a sync, are dropped quietly, since a reader's own editor sends them in the handshake; asking what was missed, awareness and asking who is here pass. Still open for a reader: the editor is not yet read-only in the client, and a member who loses the right keeps the scenes already joined until 4f-5. `LiveScene::react_to` went, the connection decoding first. 6 new tests, one through the socket itself; 11 mutants by hand, all caught.* *Seen once 2026-10-25, in a full accounts run after 4f-4c: `a scene carried by its grip lands below the scene it is let go under` (moving.spec.ts), the dropped scene not visible in time; passed on its retry, and in the local run just before. Another drag, so the same timing under load as the card above, it seems.* *Seen again 2026-10-25, a third time, in a full accounts run after the facade's F1: `a project's details are fetched once while moving between its views` (writing-landing.spec.ts), failing in 1.3 s and passing on its retry; the local run before it was clean. Three times now, always with accounts, so worth a look of its own: a details request counted twice, perhaps the members' channel or a sign-in redirect asking again.* *Seen once 2026-10-25, in a full local run after the ideas core: `a wrong code says so and empties the boxes, keeping the password` (password-reset.spec.ts) timed out at 30 s and passed on its retry; the accounts run after it was clean. Nothing of ideas is on that page, so load, it seems.* *With the cores slice, 2026-10-26, two full runs. The first: locally `landing where the author left off reaches a scene far down the book` (writing-far.spec.ts) failed on both tries within about 100 ms, its error not kept, and passed alone, and then 65 times in five repeats of its file; with accounts four flaky. The second, its output kept: none failing, two flaky locally and seven with accounts, every one passing on its retry. Half of them died within 150 ms on `page.goto: net::ERR_ADDRESS_IN_USE`: the machine ran out of local ports in the middle of the run, though the run began with TIME_WAIT under 500. The rest waited 5 s for something to show, one of them the theme button on a phone, which nothing of the slice touches. So the first run's failure was most likely the same exhaustion. Worth a step of its own: drain TIME_WAIT lower before a run, or have the suites reuse connections, since a run near the edge now loses tests to the machine rather than to the code.*
   *7b done: `aNewProject` makes the project through the API, as the "New project" dialog would with an English browser, waits until the projects catalog lists it, so a test going back to the list finds it, and opens its board by address. Making a project through the start page and the dialog is what `projects.spec.ts` tests, and it still does so through the page. The remote suite went from about 2.7 to 1.9 minutes, the local one from about 3.0 to 2.7. Capturing ideas through the board stays as it is: in the board specs, capturing is mostly what is being tested.*
   *7c done: `writing.spec.ts`, some 4,200 lines and 173 tests, is nine files by what they are about: `writing-flow` (headings, the cursor's way through the book, Enter, paragraphs), `writing-margin` (titles and menus beside the scenes), `writing-gaps` (adding and bringing back between scenes, nesting), `writing-status` (saving and counting), `writing-landing` (where Write opens and the offer to go back), `writing-far` (scenes far from the window, connections), `writing-tray`, `writing-sides` (the scenes outside the book) and `writing-text` (spelling, language, emphasis, dialogue, justification), between 250 and 890 lines each. Tests written late at the file's end went to the file of what they are about. A helper used by one file stayed in it; the 29 used by several went to `support/writing.ts`. Not changed, and older than the split: the configuration runs tests in parallel, and the three copy tests in `writing-text` share the machine's clipboard with each other and with `selecting.spec.ts`'s; none has collided yet in many runs, but nothing stops it.*
   *7d done: the near-duplicates named in the findings were each read against the other, and most were not: the tests about words typed while something is being made each hold back a different request, the scene's creation, its handover to its own editor or the section's, and the two about going back to where the author left off check different things. Comparing every test's body with every other's found what the names hid: "a pinned idea opens from the board" did exactly what "the bar's open button opens the idea" does, and went. "an empty book starts with its first heading" was the first half of "an empty book is written from its first heading into its first scene", which now also checks the placeholder and the heading after a reload. "Write lands where the author left off…" had been going back by the offer since 8e and is named for it now. Tests of the same shape with different inputs, the edges of a card being resized or the forms a project's address takes, are variants rather than duplicates and stay. 461 tests.*
   *6c done: `kept::{recall, keep, forget}` is the only code that touches the browser's storage, where the theme, the drawers and their widths, where you left off and the writing settings each had their own. `problem::{problem, problem_to_dismiss}` draw the error banner nine places drew for themselves. `app::project_page` reads the project from the address and puts the masthead above, which the four pages of a project repeated. `inputs.rs` gave way to Leptos' `event_target_value`, `event_target_checked` and `event_target`. Opening a project from the list or a new one goes straight to its writing view; the project's own address still forwards there, for old links, and two tests still open it. Forgetting a setting went untested: nothing reloaded after a setting was switched back, so spelling switched on again and justified text switched off again now each survive a reload in their tests.*
   *6d done: `nearness::Nearness<K>` holds what the writing view's `Viewport` and the outline's `InView` each kept for themselves: a near signal per scene or group of leaves, made under the view's owner, the two observers, and `set`, which writes only when nearness changes. Its `watched(key, place)` registers an element with the observers, lets go of it on cleanup and answers whether it is near, which both callers built by hand. Each view keeps only its own rule for staying near: the writing view landing, the cursor and a scene still saving, the outline focus and a held drag. The margins and the observers became private to `nearness.rs`. Breaking the shared watching fails eleven outline tests and seven writing tests; a `set` that never writes fails eight.*
7. **The E2E specs.** The same rule as 5 for helpers: those copied between specs move into `support/`, with one setup for a book in the writing view. Projects are made through the API where a test is not about making them, which saves two page loads per test. `writing.spec.ts`, 4,300 lines, splits along its sections, and the near-duplicates that turn up are merged.
   Four reviews: **7a** what the specs share moves into `support/`, **7b** projects made through the API, **7c** `writing.spec.ts` split, **7d** near-duplicates merged. Prettier stays as it is: after these reviews the tests read well enough, decided 2026-10-19. *7a done: `API` is declared once in `support/shell.ts`, where eight specs, `book.ts` and three of `shell.ts`'s own requests each spelled out the address, and two more specs wrote it inline. `support/writing.ts` holds `flow`, `scene`, `projectOf`, `rail`, `openTheWritingView` and `theBook`, each copied word for word into two to six specs, and `writing.spec.ts`'s `sceneOrder` turned out to be `theBook` under another name. `aBookInTheWritingView` is the project, the book and the writing view that 35 tests in `writing.spec.ts` set up line by line, and that `moving.spec.ts` and `selecting.spec.ts` had each wrapped for themselves. Helpers that only share a name, `shown`, `titled`, `tray` and the like, stay where they mean what they say. Seen while checking it, and older than it: `scenes begun in the view, taken out of the book, show among those outside it at once` (selecting.spec.ts) fails 2 runs in 10 in local mode, on this change and on the commit before it alike; the scene begun is not yet among those outside the book when the test looks. Worth finding out whether the view or the test is late.* *Found the same day: the view was late, for good. A scene begun in the book is known to that view, but the other side is a new view, which asks the scene catalog, and the catalog hears of a new scene only once its message has been relayed. In about one switch in fifteen it had not, the scene was missing outside the book, and nothing asked again until a reload; the take-out itself always reached the server. `scenes::written` now does for scenes what `ideas::written` does for ideas: a scene made in this browser is listed until the catalog lists it, and one deleted here is not brought back. The test strips the scene from the listing to stand for the lag, so it fails every time without the fix rather than one run in five.*

**Every route starts with its feature.** *Noted 2026-10-19.* The routes of a feature begin with its name, `/projects`, `/ideas`, `/boards`, `/outlines`, `/scenes`, `/appearances`, `/statistics`, so what serves an address can be told from the address. The progress of restyling a book's quotation marks is the scenes feature's, so it is `/api/scenes/quotation-restylings/{project}` rather than a `/tasks` of its own, which would group routes by how they work rather than whose they are. One route still breaks the rule: the scenes' live channel is `/api/sync`, and becomes `/api/scenes/sync`, with the browser's socket address changed beside it. *Done 2026-10-19: the socket is at `/api/scenes/sync`, in the server, the browser's `js/sync.js`, trunk's proxy and the stress seeder; `/api/sync` is gone, and a wiring test says so. The test that the socket is mounted now asks for the socket's own refusal of a request that is no socket, since `/scenes/{id}` would otherwise answer for it, reading `sync` as a scene's id.*

**Looked at and kept.** The SQL stays separate per database: Postgres and SQLite adapters are mostly alike, but each backend keeps its own queries and its own locking. No crates are merged, `eventpublishing` into `eventsourcing` or `outbox` into `messaging`, since fewer crates means bigger ones. The boards and outline tidying listeners, near clones, stay as they are for now. The contract crates, `words`, `messaging-contract`, `clock` and the store crates each earn their place.

### From here, in order

*Decided 2026-10-19.* M12, M13 and the writing view are done; what they left for later is gathered under [Later](#later--polish-and-what-can-wait), so it is not lost and not in the way. From here:

1. **[M10b](#milestone-10b--the-outlines-live-channel-), the outline's live channel**, which now covers the writing view too: another author's sections, headings and moves arrive without a reload.
2. **The writing view's stack.** *Noted 2026-10-19:* drawing the book ran out of WebAssembly's default 1 MB stack, in a debug build, once two small additions came on top of what was there; it shows as "memory access out of bounds" in whatever part of the view the stack happened to end in. The stack is 4 MB for now. The cause is how the view is built: `WriteViewState`, some thirty handles, is copied into nearly every closure, and the view is one deep generic tuple. Measure how much stack drawing a book takes, in debug and in release, then shrink it, the state passed as one small handle and the large view built in parts, and see whether 1 MB holds again. *Measured and settled 2026-10-19: drawing the book needs between 1,152 and 1,280 KB of stack in a debug build, and 256 KB or less in release, which inlines and shrinks its frames; `WriteViewState` is 6,872 bytes, its parts holding copies of one another, and nearly every closure of the view holds a copy. Only debug builds run short, and those are ours, in development and in every end-to-end run, so the stack stays at 4 MB and nothing is refactored. Should the view ever outgrow 4 MB, the end-to-end tests run the debug build and crash at once.*
3. **[M13e](#milestone-13e--last-touches-to-the-writing-view-), last touches to the writing view**: typewriter scrolling, then menus that show their keys.
4. **[M14](#milestone-14--the-app-in-five-languages-), the app in five languages.**
5. **[M9b](#milestone-9b--the-boards-live-channel), the board's live channel**, its committed changes first. *Found missing from this list 2026-10-20, and put back before M15: the outline's channel left the parts it needs built, the `live` library, `BroadcastChangesListener`, the reconnecting socket and the reload by version.*
6. **[M10c](#milestone-10c--one-way-of-pushing), one way of pushing**: the alarm and the restyling's progress pushed rather than asked for, and the ideas, the projects, the word counts and the appearances following other authors live. *Decided 2026-10-20, directly after M9b's committed half.*
7. **[M15](#milestone-15--touch-and-small-screens), touch and small screens.**
7b. **A pass for simplicity, front and back.** ✅ *Closed 2026-10-22 after slice 5: 1, 3, 4, 5a and 5b done; 2 and 5c measured and left; 11 and 12 left too, as they would mostly move code around, and accounts come first.* *Asked for 2026-10-21, for directly after M15:* in the client, the big files and the big functions; on the server, the broadcasting each feature now carries in its own way, a listener in one, a wrapper around a catalog in four, to see whether one simpler shape fits them all.
   *Findings, 2026-10-21: two surveys, the server and the client with its tests, every claim counted and the ones taken checked against the code. Taken, the ones worth most and deleting most, each a review of its own:* **1** one way to tell and one live route, on the server; **2** one way to follow, in the client: three copies of following a board, an outline and the writing view's structure, five of following a list, the version check written seven times, an upsert called `hold`, `put` and `adopt`; **3** one error path in the REST adapters, eight `ApiError`s with sixteen `From`s and eight `IntoResponse`s, and an idea's title refused as 400 where a scene's and a section's are 422; **4** one checked text, for the idea's, the scene's and the section's titles, the project's name and its description; **5** the listeners' second round: one catalogue listener for four near clones, one `aggregate_in`, nine error enums only wrapping `Failure`, one paged sweep for ideas and scenes; **11** the end-to-end helpers into `support/`, `anotherAuthor` six times, `anIdea` five, request and frame counters nineteen, `fingers`, `sceneCarried`, a 73-line header shared by two specs; **12** the Rust test helpers, `told` eight times, the drain loop four, two near-identical test wirings. *Left, as smaller or mostly moving code around:* the small server repeats, one error state in the client, the small client repeats, the big functions beyond what 2 shrinks, the stylesheets' tokens.
   - **1. One way to tell, and one live route.** *Done 2026-10-21: `live::Teller` keeps the count and makes the one frame, `{of, version, event}`, which the five read models' wrappers each made by hand, two of them without saying what it was of; the wrappers now keep what they wrap and a teller. `live::followed_at(path, broadcaster, about)` serves a live route, `about` reading what is followed from the query, a missing one turned away, and `followed_from` says first where it stands, for the board and the outline; they replace eight routers written by hand, each with a handler and a struct for its query, and four `serde` and three `serde_json` dependencies with them. The ideas' own broadcast stays its own: it looks its project up as it goes, and a listener of the library's that could do that would cost more than the forty lines it saves. Measured: 142 lines fewer in the features and the service, 91 more in the library, so about 50 fewer in all; less than estimated, and what is gained is mostly that telling and following are written once. Tests: the teller tells what it is of and each telling is newer; a follower hears what it asked for, one that says nothing is turned away, and one from a version is told it first, and nothing first where nothing stands; every live spec in the browser passes.*
   - **2. One way to follow, in the client.** *Not done, decided 2026-10-22 after measuring it: the two lists followed by version, the ideas and the projects, would share a helper of about 45 lines to save about 60; the two lists of a read model's state, the scenes and the word counts, about the same; the board's and the outline's following would cost in a shared shape what one copy costs; the version check, the seven three-line checks, comes out longer in a shared step that still has to be matched three ways, and the open project's following is its own, switching projects and refused when deleted. About 30 lines in all, from code already short and read in one place each, against the rule this pass is taken by.*
   - **3. One error path in the REST adapters.** *Done 2026-10-22: a handler answers `Result<_, Problem>`, and says at each call what a refusal becomes, `.map_err(problem_of)` for a feature's service, `Problem::unexpected` for a catalog or the deliveries; `Unreadable` becomes a `Problem` of its own, so a malformed `If-Match` needs nothing written. The feature's rest crates cannot say `From<…ServiceError> for Problem`, both being others' types, which is why each had an `ApiError`; eight error types with their sixteen `From`s and eight `IntoResponse`s are gone, the four `expected`s that only forwarded to `demanded` with them, and the outline's ways of failing that were no service's, an unknown section id, an untitled section, a kind it does not hold, are three small functions. 152 lines fewer. Every answer is as it was: an idea's title is still refused as 400 where a scene's and a section's are 422, which is for deciding apart. Tests: every feature's API tests and the journeys pass unchanged; the bad `If-Match` answered as anything but a bad request fails the boards' test of it.* *Settled 2026-10-22: an idea's title that is too long or holds a control character is now 422, as a scene's and a section's are; a bad id stays 400. The client's `checked` read only 400 and 409 as a refusal, so the idea's 422 became an unexpected failure, which the German test of a refused title caught; it reads 422 as a refusal too now.*
   - **4. One checked text.** *Done 2026-10-22: `words::TextRules` says how long a text may be, whether it may be blank and whether it may break lines, and `fitted` trims it and checks it, answering `Unfit`: blank, a control character, or too long with how long it may be and is. An idea's, a scene's and a section's title, a project's name and its description each say their rules once and are what `fitted` gives back, where each checked the same three things in its own words; their errors are `Unfit` under the names they had, so the services and the answers keep theirs. What `Unfit` says leaves out what it is about, since the answer's kind already names it, so a blank project name is told `must not be blank`; nothing in the client reads that. The checks are tested once, in `words`, and each text keeps the tests of its own rules, which is about half its tests. A title can never be blank, and the answers for titles say so as unexpected, where a shared kind could only have lumped it with something else. The name's and the description's errors being one type now, the projects' service and store say which of the two failed where each is checked, rather than by conversion. 75 lines fewer, most of them tests. Tests: what is written keeps its words without its margins, blank only where allowed, line breaks only where allowed, the limit in characters and what is said with its numbers; changing any text's own rule, blank, line breaks or limit, fails its tests.*
   - **5. The listeners' second round**, in three reviews: **5a** what a message is about, **5b** the error enums that only wrap a failure, **5c** one paged sweep. *5a done 2026-10-22: `eventpublishing::aggregate_in::<Id>` reads which aggregate a message is about as that aggregate's own id, refusing a message that carries no published event or names its aggregate by something else, `UnreadableMessage::NotItsId`. The boards, ideas, outline and projects each had it as their own: an error enum of two variants, an `event_in` that only forwarded to `published_in`, and a `board_in`, `idea_in`, `outline_in` or `project_in`; all go, and with them each feature's test of a stray message, which the library tests once. The projects' messaging no longer needs `ids`, `thiserror` or `serde_json`. 100 lines fewer. One catalogue listener for the four that load the latest, then forget what is gone or remember its summary, was weighed and left: `Handler` takes a listener's name and what it hears as constants, so each feature keeps a type of its own, and only the middle would be shared, for about what it costs. Tests: a message's aggregate read as its own kind of id, one named otherwise and one that is no published event refused; each feature still reads its own id back out of what it published.* *5b done 2026-10-22: `messaging::Message::payload_as::<T>()` reads a message's payload as its shape, refusing it as `Unshaped`, which names the routing key it came by. The listeners' nine error enums, `NotIndexed`, `NotKept`, `NotCatalogued`, `NotDetached`, the boards' and the outline's `NotLetGo`, the ideas' and the scenes' `NotSwept` and `NotRestyled`, only wrapped what their callers failed with and were turned into a `Failure` at once by `take_in`; they go, and each listener fails as a `Failure` straight away. An id that cannot be read is now told by the id library's own words, which name the prefix expected rather than the text. Six features' messaging no longer needs `thiserror`, four of them not `serde_json` either. 93 lines fewer. Tests: a payload read as its shape, and one not shaped as it should be refused, naming its routing key.*
   Then **[M17](#milestone-17--accounts-members-and-roles), accounts, members and roles**, *modelled 2026-10-22;* *chosen 2026-10-22 over another view or the test readers: they reach into every feature, and every feature added before them is one more to change; test readers build on them too. A modelling session settles first what the tenant is, where identities come from, which roles there are and what a hosted installation is.* Straight after it **[M17a](#milestone-17a--hosted-for-many-ten-thousand-at-once), hosted for many**, *planned 2026-10-25: measured first, then only what the numbers ask for.* **[M13a](#milestone-13a--moving-a-project-between-machines), moving a project between machines**, *is put off again, for some time, decided 2026-10-22.*
