### Milestone 13e — Last touches to the writing view ✅

**Goal:** the writing view polished where the author feels it, before the app learns a second language.

1. **Typewriter scrolling, as its own setting**: keeping the line being written at one height in the window. It belongs to the writing settings rather than to focus mode, since it is wanted outside it too. *Done 2026-10-19: "Typewriter scrolling" in the writing settings, off at first and kept in the browser as the other settings are. While it is on, whenever the cursor moves in the book, by typing, the arrow keys or Enter, the window scrolls so the line it is on sits in the middle; while a mouse button is held it waits, since a heading takes the cursor as the button goes down and the page must not run off under it, and the line comes to the middle once the button is let go. The book gets room above it then, measured so that at the very top the first heading already sits in the middle with nothing scrolled, the masthead staying in view (a fixed half window pushed the first line below the middle, and clicking it scrolled the masthead away); the room is measured again when the window changes size and given back when the setting is turned off. Tests: off at first and kept across a reload; the line stays in the middle through fourteen new paragraphs, and runs on down the window without it; a heading further down stays put while the button is held and comes to the middle when it is let go; at the top the first heading sits in the middle with the masthead in view, also after a resize; and turned off, the room above goes. Browser tests against a dev trunk that is running must wait for it to serve the new build, or they test the old one.*
2. **Menus show their keys.** The margin menus say nothing about the keyboard, so Shift+F10, and Tab / Shift+Tab for nesting, stay hidden unless someone tells you. Each item that has a key could show it on the right, faded, the way desktop menus do: *Nest under the previous section* with Tab, *Lift out a level* with Shift+Tab. The menu could also name Shift+F10 as the key that opens it. Mac labels would come later, if a shortcut ever needs Cmd. *Done 2026-10-20: in a section's menu, Nest under the previous section shows Tab and Lift out a level shows Shift+Tab, on the right and faded; the key is also told to screen readers as the item's shortcut, while its name stays the words alone. Those are the only menu items with a key. The scene's and the section's menu buttons name Shift+F10 when the pointer rests on them (Scene actions (Shift+F10)) and to screen readers; the menus that add, which Shift+F10 does not open, name nothing. Tests: the nesting and lifting items show and announce their keys, and an item without one shows none; the scene's and section's buttons name Shift+F10, the add buttons do not.*

**Done when:** typewriter scrolling can be switched on in the writing settings and keeps the line being written at one height, and every menu item that has a key shows it.

### Milestone 14 — The app in five languages ✅

**Goal:** Weaveling speaks to the author in their language, chosen by the reader rather than the build: English and German, which we can check ourselves, and Spanish, French and Brazilian Portuguese, the other most spoken languages of Europe and the Americas, drafted by a language model and marked as machine-translated until a native speaker has read them.

**Build:** every string in the client comes out of the markup and into a catalogue, with a language the author picks and the browser's preference as the default. Dates and times already go through `time` and will need the locale too. Nothing assumes two languages: the languages are the catalogue's, plurals follow each language's own rules, and no sentence is glued together in code, so adding a language is adding a file.

**Decided 2026-10-20:**
- **Fluent**, Mozilla's format (`fluent-bundle`): one `.ftl` file per language, built into the client. `leptos_i18n` would check keys at compile time, but it wants Leptos 0.8 and we are on 0.9; Fluent knows every language's plurals, is a format translators and language models already know, and a test checks that every language has every message.
- **The end-to-end suite stays in English**, the browser's language pinned to English in the Playwright config, so its selectors keep checking the accessible names as they do now; a few specs of their own run in German. A test id per element would have been language-free, but would have lost the accessibility check the names double as.
- **No right-to-left languages for now.** Arabic or Hebrew would mirror the margins, trays and menus, a layout of its own.
- **Later:** Italian, and Russian, the most spoken native language in Europe, once plurals have proven themselves on its several forms.
- **The interface's language is not the book's.** The book's language ([M13c](#milestone-13c--the-writing-view-) item 8) sets quotation marks, hyphenation and spelling, and covers many more languages; an author can write a Spanish book in an English Weaveling.

**In slices, a screen at a time:**

1. **The catalogue, the choice of language, the masthead and the list of projects.** *Done 2026-10-20: the catalogues are `clients/web/catalogue/*.ftl`, one per language, built into the client; `Speech`, given to the whole app, holds the language spoken and words a message in it, and `said("message")` is the text of a message that follows the language as it changes. At first the app speaks the first of the browser's languages it knows, by language alone, so Austrian German is German and European Portuguese is Brazilian; a browser asking only for languages it does not know gets English. A picker in the masthead, beside the theme, shows the language spoken by its short name (DE, ES), so it takes no more room than the theme even on a phone, and its list names each language in its own words and says of Spanish, French and Portuguese that a machine translated them, in the language being spoken (the select showed its whole option at first, as wide as the longest, and pushed the project's name out of a phone's masthead); the choice is kept in the browser and is the page's `lang`, while the book keeps its own. The masthead, the views, the theme, the status bar and the list of projects with its menus and dialogs are worded from the catalogue; the dialog's title can now follow the language. Only the language spoken is read, the first time a message is wanted, though every catalogue is built in; when something goes wrong wording a message, a name the code did not pass for instance, the console warns. German speaks to the author as du. Unicode isolation marks are off, since every language is left to right, so a name set into a message is just the name. Fluent adds 174 KB to the release build (3.31 MB to 3.48 MB, 1.08 MB to 1.15 MB compressed). Tests: every catalogue parses and words the same messages as English, asking for the same names; a message comes in the language asked for, a name set into it bare, and a message nobody words shows its own name; tags are told by language alone, and an unknown one by none. In the browser: German at first for a German browser, the masthead inside a project German too, deleting a project asked in German with the name in German quotation marks; English for an Italian browser; a language chosen is spoken at once and kept across a reload; the machine-translated languages say so in the language spoken; the book keeps its language whatever the app speaks; and on a phone the picker shows ES, narrower than the theme and on its row. On a narrow window the picker drops its chevron and sits closer to the theme, since at 375 pixels it had left the project's name 81 pixels, under the 90 the masthead test keeps for it.*
2. **The project's details**, in the new project dialog and the details dialog, with the problems the server reports. *Done 2026-10-20: the details' fields, the details dialog, asking before the quotation marks change and following the book being rewritten are worded from the catalogue, and so are the languages a book can be written in, named in the language spoken and in their own ("Englisch (English)"). The count of scenes rewritten takes the form its language gives it, one Szene, three Szenen, as Fluent's plurals do. The problems the client tells, a server out of reach, something gone or changed meanwhile, are worded from the catalogue too, a server out of reach now asking to try again in a moment rather than whether it is running, which a reader may have no way to know or change; and what has gone is named by the subject asked about, "Dieses Projekt", "Diese Szene", chosen in the catalogue, since the article follows the noun; `ApiError` no longer carries English text of its own, and the client no longer needs `thiserror`. The subject "word counts" became `word-counts`, a name the catalogue can choose by. What the server refuses still came as the server wrote it, in English, until slice 3. The completeness test now also finds the names a message asks for inside its choices. Tests: in German, a new project's details are asked in German with the book's languages named in German; rewriting the marks is asked and followed in German, one scene told as one; a server out of reach is told in German; and a project gone meanwhile is told as a project. A count's form is checked for one and for three.*
3. **What the server refuses, named, for projects.** A refusal reached the client as the server's own English sentence, "project name must be at most 200 characters, got 240", which the client could only show as it came. *Decided 2026-10-20:* the server names what it refused and gives its numbers, and the client words it from the catalogue, rather than the client checking first what it can so that refusals stay rare enough to be left in English. *Done 2026-10-20: a refusal is a problem document (RFC 9457, `application/problem+json`): its `type` names what was refused, `project-name-too-long`, its `detail` keeps the server's English sentence, and its particulars, `max` and `actual`, stand beside them. `serving::Problem` builds one, `rejected` for 400 and `refused` for 409, and `Problem::of` names what every feature shares, `not-found`, `outdated` and `unexpected`, leaving the feature to name its own refusals; an unreadable `If-Match` is `unreadable`. The projects feature names all of its refusals, the names kept in `projects_contract::refused`, which client and server share, so a test of the client's finds every one of them worded in the catalogue. The client reads a problem document after a 400 or a 409, words its type as `refused-<type>` with its particulars, and shows the server's sentence for any type it does not know; a plain sentence after a 400 is still shown as it came. So renaming a project deleted meanwhile, refused with 409, is now told as just that, where it was something unexpected. Tests: the API tells every refusal of a project by its type, with its particulars, as a problem document, and so for something not found, a stale version and an unreadable one; `serving` checks a problem's document and that a particular cannot pass itself off as the type; the client reads type, detail and particulars, leaves out what is neither a count nor a text, and takes no document without a detail; in German a title too long is told with its numbers and a project deleted meanwhile as deleted; and a refusal the client cannot word is told as the server put it.*
4. **What the server refuses, named, for boards, ideas and the outline.** Each names its refusals in its contract as the projects feature does, and `serving::refusal`, the plain English answer, goes. *Done 2026-10-20: boards, ideas and the outline answer every refusal as a problem document, each keeping the status it had, 404 for an idea not on the board or a section not found, 422 for a card without a shape, a section title refused or a section put inside itself, 409 for the rest; `Problem` gained `not_found` and `unprocessable` for them, and `serving::refusal` is gone with its tests, `Problem`'s own covering the same. The client words all of them already, in every language, rather than as their screens come, since the test that every named refusal is worded now runs over all four contracts. Refusals a request cannot bring about are not named: an aggregate not started yet or started twice, an idea captured twice, a section already there, since the server makes every id itself and an unknown id is not found before the aggregate is asked. They pass through one last arm as `unexpected`, logged as the server's own fault, and the names given them in the projects slice went again, with their words. A mutant of that last arm survives, as anything there must, since no request reaches it. Tests: the API tests of all three now tell every refusal by its type, the projects' way; new ones for a section title too long or with a control character, a section after one not beside it, detaching what is not in the outline, a kind of thing an outline does not hold, an idea's title with a control character, and a board and an outline discarded with their project refusing what comes after; in German, an idea's title too long is told with its numbers.*
5. **What the server refuses, named, for scenes, appearances, word counts and the alarm's refusals.** The four routes still answering in plain text, `features/scenes`, `features/appearances`, `features/statistics` and `services/api/src/refusals.rs`, answer with problem documents too. *Done 2026-10-20: every route of the server now answers a refusal or a failure as a problem document. Scenes name four in `scenes_contract::refused`: an id that is not a scene's, a title with a control character or too long (422, with its numbers), and a book whose quotation marks were never rewritten (404, which the client takes, as before, for nothing to follow); a scene not found is `not-found`. A scene that already exists and an update that cannot be applied are not named, since the server makes every scene's id and updates come only over the live socket, so they pass as `unexpected` with the store's, the catalog's and the restylings' failures. Appearances, word counts and the alarm's refusals only ever fail, and fail as `unexpected`, logged as before. The client words the four scene refusals in every language, the test that every named refusal is worded now running over five contracts. What still answers in plain text is axum's own refusal of a request it cannot read, a path or a query of the wrong shape, before any route of ours is asked. Tests: the scenes API tells its refusals by their type, with a title too long told with its numbers.*
6. **The board.** *Done 2026-10-20: the board, the ideas beside it, the cards' actions and grips, the zoom, the tray and the idea inspector docked in it are worded from the catalogue, and follow the language as it changes, the cards' labels too. Where a sentence named an idea, "Pin {idea}", the catalogue sets the name into it, since languages put it in different places, "The loom anpinnen"; a grip says which side it holds through a choice in the message, a side being a phrase of its own in each language. The drawer, which the tray shares with the writing view's contents, glued "Close" and "Resize" to "the tray"; it now asks the catalogue for the whole phrase by the drawer it is, `tray` or `contents`, since the article and its case change with the noun, "Breite des Inhaltsverzeichnisses ändern". An idea without a title is shown as `untitled`, "Ohne Titel", one word for every kind of thing, ready for scenes and sections in their slices. `speech` gained `say` and `say_about` for words wanted at once rather than as a signal, in the language spoken where there is one and in English where there is none, as in the client's own unit tests. Tests: switching to German on the board turns its region, tally, help, waiting ideas, pin button, a card's actions and grips, the zoom, the inspector and the tray German at once; an idea without a title is shown as untitled.*
7. **The outline.** The outline's client takes every 404 and 422 for being overtaken by someone else, so a section title the server refuses as too long would be told as overtaken; with the refusals named, it can tell the two apart. *Done 2026-10-20: the manuscript, the tray of scenes and ideas not in the book, the rows' fold, title, grip and actions, the groups of a section and the "Renamed meanwhile" question are worded from the catalogue and follow the language as it changes, the question too while it is open; its messages, `renamed-meanwhile-*`, are ready for the writing view's own. A row's actions name the section by the catalogue, "Ohne Titel nach vorn", and a scene with neither title nor words is `scene-empty`, "Leer". A group of a section is keyed `scenes` or `ideas` in the page, its name coming from the catalogue. The outline's client now reads the problem behind a 404 or a 422: a section title refused, too long or with a control character, is told as it is, and anything else still means being overtaken and opening the outline again. Tests: switching to German on the outline turns the manuscript, the tray, a new section's field, its actions and grip German at once; the question of who renamed a title meanwhile follows a language chosen while it is asked; and a section title the server refuses is told as too long, not as overtaken.*
8. **The ideas.** *Done 2026-10-20: the pool, its heading, the field and button that capture an idea, its list and what it says while loading or empty, and an idea's page while it opens are worded from the catalogue; the idea inspector came with the board. Two messages named for where they first stood became shared: `idea-asked`, "What is the idea?", which the board asks too, and `loading`, which the list of projects shows too. Tests: switching to German in the pool turns its heading, field, button, list and empty text German at once, an idea captured through them, and an idea's page says it is opening in German.*
9. **The writing view**, the largest, in three parts.
   - **9a. The book and what stands around it.** *Done 2026-10-20: the book itself, its sides, the headings' and openings' invitations, the status note with its word count and save state, the writing settings, the drawers' toggles, focus mode's line, the quiet notes and the way back to where the author left off are worded from the catalogue. The empty scene's invitation was a word of the stylesheet, `content: "An empty scene. Start writing…"`, which no catalogue reaches; it is now a custom property the book carries, `--empty-scene`, set from the catalogue, the stylesheet reading `content: var(--empty-scene)`. A count of words takes its language's plural, "1 Wort", "3 Wörter", and its thousands are grouped by a separator the catalogue holds, `digit-group`, a comma in English, a point in German, a narrow space in French. A quiet note is worded when it is told, and keeps those words if the language changes while it shows. Tests: switching to German in the writing view turns the book, its invitation and a heading's placeholder, its sides, the drawers' toggles, the word count and its hint, the save state, focus mode and the writing settings German at once; a count is told as one word or many, grouped by thousands; a text set into the stylesheet keeps its quotes and backslashes inside; and, found missing by a mutant that lived, a scene left empty where the author left off is offered back as an empty scene.*
   - **9b. The margin and its questions:** the menus beside scenes and headings, the questions the writing view asks, placing a scene and bringing one back. *Done 2026-10-20: the margin's menus and their buttons, the questions of the writing view, deleting a scene, removing a section or what is chosen, a place gone, a section removed or a heading renamed meanwhile, and the dialogs that place a scene in the book or bring one back are worded from the catalogue. The question about what is chosen was put together in code, a count, "is" or "are", and sentences added when they applied; it is now whole sentences of the catalogue, one for scenes, one for sections and one for both, each with its plurals, and the sentences that apply follow it. A menu's keys are shown in the language spoken, "Umschalt+Tab", while `aria-keyshortcuts` keeps the names the standard gives them. The completeness test now also refuses a message worded twice in one catalogue, which Fluent would let pass, keeping one. Tests: in German, the scene and section menus with their keys and titles, deleting a scene and removing two chosen scenes; bringing a scene back, finding none, and placing one from outside the book. Found missing by mutants that lived: the questions about what is chosen were never checked whole, so the English tests now read them whole, for scenes and sections together, for sections alone, chosen by Shift and a click from heading to heading, and for scenes outside the book.*
   - **9c. The drawers' insides:** the contents, the tray beside a scene, and linking an idea to a scene. *Done 2026-10-20: the contents and its folding, the tray beside a scene or a section with the ideas it draws on or notes, and the dialog that links or notes ideas are worded from the catalogue. The dialog's wording for each use, linking and noting, names messages rather than holding English, and its button counts what is chosen by the catalogue too, "Verknüpfen" for none or one, "3 verknüpfen" for more. The last fallbacks to "Untitled", a project's name read from its address and the tray's scene and section names, take the catalogue's. With that the writing view is worded whole. Tests: in German, the contents with its folding and an empty scene, the tray of a scene with an idea linked through the dialog, and the tray of a section with its dialog finding nothing. Found missing by mutants that lived: the dialog with every idea already linked, the tray of a section removed meanwhile, and an untitled section in the contents, each now tested in English.*
10. **The alarm and a page not found:** the alarm's refusals and its dialog, and the page shown for an address that leads nowhere. *Done 2026-10-20: the alarm, its note and its dialog with each change it holds, and the page for an address that leads nowhere are worded from the catalogue; with that, every word the client writes itself is. What a refused change says of itself, `plainly`, is written by the listener that gave up, on the server, and is shown as it comes, the catalogue's own sentence standing in when there is none; it is the last of the server's English the reader can meet, and would go the way the refusals of requests went if it is ever wanted. The moment a change was given up on is still told in English, "10 Oct 2026, 11:01", which the next slice changes. Tests: in German, an address leading nowhere, and the alarm, its note, its dialog and a change in it with its details.*
11. **Dates and times** in the reader's language: the projects' stamps and the writing view's notes. *Done 2026-10-20: a moment is told by a message of its own, `moment`, with the day, the month, the year and the time as its particulars and the month's short name chosen in it by its number, each language putting them in its own order, "10 Oct 2026, 09:01", "10. Okt. 2026, 09:01", "10 oct. 2026 à 09:01", "10 de out. de 2026, 09:01"; the day is no longer padded, and "an unknown moment" went, nothing being left that could fail. The projects' stamps and the moment a refused change was given up on are the only moments the client tells; the writing view's notes tell none. Tests: a moment is told by its parts, the first and last months named and the day unpadded; each language orders the same moment its own way; and in German, a project's stamp and a refused change's moment.*

**Done when:** the whole app reads in each of the five languages, the choice survives a reload, the suite passes in English, and the German specs pass.

### Milestone 15 — Touch and small screens

**Goal:** the app in a pocket, for the edits that happen away from a desk.

**Build:** the writing view and the workspace are a media query away — they are already a single column. **The board is not.** Pointer events already carry touch, but the resize grips are 7px where a finger needs about 44, `touch-action: none` on the corkboard means the browser will not help, and **pinch-to-zoom is not wired at all** — ctrl-and-wheel is the only zoom, and pinch is *the* gesture on a phone. That is a feature, not a stylesheet. *Seen 2026-10-16: on a phone the board is not really usable at all. The masthead, the writing view and its drawers were made to fit a phone along with M13c 8c, so the board is what is left.*

In slices, in this order:

1. **Measure first**, on a phone in the browser tests, what the done-when asks for. *Done 2026-10-21: `touch-board.spec.ts` emulates a phone, 390 by 844 with touch, and drives the screen with fingers over CDP, one or two at a time. Already working: an idea is captured in the pool, a scene is written, one finger on the bare board pans it, and a double tap on the bare board captures an idea there; pinning from the board means opening the drawer of ideas not on it first, which a phone folds away, and works. Not yet, each kept as a test expected to fail until its slice makes it pass: a card cannot be moved with a thumb, since the browser cancels the finger's pointer, `pointercancel`, right after its first move, while the bare board, which takes the pointer on `pointerdown` rather than once the drag begins, pans; the finger lands on the idea's name, a link that fills the card, so whether the link or the late capture is what lets the browser take the gesture is for slice 2 to find out. Two fingers pinching do nothing, as expected, and the grips are 7px against a fingertip's 44.*
2. **A card moved with a thumb**, the cancelled drag above. *Done 2026-10-21: the idea's name is a scroll container, `overflow-y: auto`, so a long title scrolls within its card, and a scroll container with the default `touch-action` lets the browser take a finger's move as a scroll of its own, cancelling the pointer the card was carried by. The card's `touch-action: none` does not reach into it, and `overflow-y: hidden` is a scroll container still, so the name says `touch-action: none` itself. Measured before the change: taking the pointer on `pointerdown`, as the bare board does, changed nothing, while the name out of the pointer's way, or its `touch-action: none`, moved the card exactly where the finger went. The link stays, so a click with ctrl or shift still opens the idea elsewhere; what a finger loses is scrolling a long title within its card, which the inspector shows whole. Test: on a phone, a card moved with a thumb goes where it is taken and the board on the server says so; it was expected to fail since slice 1, and is an ordinary test now.*
3. **Pinch-to-zoom**, two pointers tracked on the corkboard, zooming around their midpoint within the limits ctrl and the wheel keep. *Done 2026-10-21: `Viewport::pinched` pans by how far the fingers' middle moved and zooms around it by how far apart they moved, so what lies between the fingers stays between them, within the limits the wheel keeps. The corkboard keeps every finger on it, and tracks them while the event is still on its way down, `ev::capture`, since a finger landing on a card is the card's otherwise and never reaches the board. A second finger makes it a pinch: it does not reach the card under it, so it neither selects nor grabs it, and a card the first finger had begun to carry is let go of where it was. Lifting one of the two leaves the other panning, as a map does, so panning stops only once no finger is left. Panning now moves by the distance the finger moved, read from where it was kept, rather than adding up `movementX`, for a mouse as well. Tests: five unit tests of pinching, the fingers' middle followed and the zoom held within its limits, fingers on one spot only panning; on a phone, pinching apart and together zooms in and out, a pinch on the bare board and one over a card keep what lies between the fingers between them, a finger landing on a card neither selects nor moves it, a card begun to be carried stays put once a second finger lands, and lifting one finger pans with the other. Stopping a pinch's moves from reaching the cards guarded nothing once nothing carried a card during a pinch, and was taken out.*
3b. **A card carried where the pointer is.** *Found on a phone 2026-10-21, Firefox on Android: a card moved with a finger flew off into the distance. A carried card, and a grip pulled, added up each move's `movementX` and `movementY`, which Chromium reports as the step since the last move, and the emulated phone in the tests with it, while on that phone it ran away; the board's panning had stopped reading them in slice 3 and panned there fine. Done the same day: `Carrying` keeps the point on the screen where the pointer went down, and each move sets how far the card is carried to how far the pointer is from there, in the board's measure, so nothing is added up and nothing can run away. No `movementX` is read anywhere now. Tests: pointer events whose movement says a running total from where they began, while their position is true, carry a card exactly where the pointer goes, on the screen and on the server, and pull a grip exactly as far; two tests that drove the board with nothing but `movementX` give positions instead.*
4. **Targets a finger can hit**: the grips and handles at 44px on a coarse pointer, their marks as they are. *Done 2026-10-21: on a coarse pointer, `@media (pointer: coarse)`, the grips are a fingertip, 44px, across their edge or corner, centred on it as before and still unseen, and the edges leave half a fingertip at each end to the corners, since a card is only 84px tall and a full fingertip there left the side grips nothing. Only the selected card has grips there: on every card, a thumb landing near any card's edge would stretch it rather than move it, so on a phone a card is moved wherever it is touched and resized once it is tapped. A mouse keeps its 7px grips on every card. Tests: a selected card's eight grips are each a fingertip across, a thumb near the edge of a card not selected moves it whole, and a finger landing just beside a selected card's edge resizes it; the grips' test, expected to fail since slice 1, is an ordinary test now.*

**Done when:** an idea can be captured and a scene written on a phone; the board pans and pinch-zooms with two fingers; and a card can be moved with a thumb. **Met 2026-10-21**, in the browser tests on an emulated phone and on a real one, Firefox on Android.

### Milestone 17 — Accounts, members and roles

**Goal:** a server installation knows who is signed in, and each book is seen and changed only by the people its owners let in. Local mode stays as it is, with no accounts at all.

**The model**, settled in a modelling session 2026-10-22:

- **The project is the tenant.** A book's people vary from book to book, so there is no workspace or team that holds several projects: a team that only ever held one book would be a second name for the project. Everything a book holds already hangs off its project, and nothing spans two.
- **Glossary.** An **account** is a person's sign-in, outside any project: email, name, credentials. A **member** is an account in one project, with a **role**. An **invitation** offers a role, or only an account, until it is taken up. The **operator** runs the installation. "User" is not used: it names someone using software, and blurs with member.
- **Roles in a project.** **Owner**: everything, including the members and deleting the project. **Author**: reads and writes. **Reader**: sees the whole project as it is, read only; this is not the later test readers, who get a sample through a link of their own. A project has at least one owner, and all owners are equal: the last owner cannot leave or step down, only hand over or delete. Only owners invite, for every role. What a member wrote stays when they leave; its events keep their account.
- **Deleting a project goes to a trash** for 30 days, from which any owner restores it; only then is it swept as today. One co-owner cannot wipe a shared book for good on their own.
- **Accounts come from our own sign-up**: email and password, Argon2id, sign-in throttled by account and address; optional TOTP, with recovery codes; optional FIDO2 keys. An account holds credentials of several kinds, so the external logins of Later become one more kind. The email is verified by a six-digit code, as is a password reset.
- **No mail provider yet.** A `Mailer` port: in the server's log for now, in a fake mailbox for the tests, SMTP later. *Noted 2026-10-24:* worth a look first is plain SMTP with [Mailpit](https://mailpit.axllent.org/) catching mail locally, and maybe Resend's free tier (3,000 a month, 100 a day) to send for real. Amazon SES, at about $0.10 per 1,000, is the cheap way once volume grows, and only config changes behind SMTP.
- **Sessions on the server**, behind an HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Strict cookie, revocable at once; the browser sends it with the upgrade of every live socket and `/sync`.
- **Accounts are event-sourced**, like everything else, for their history. Their events say that a password was changed or a key registered; the secrets themselves, the password's hash, the TOTP secret and the recovery codes, are kept beside them, latest only, so a leaked log holds no old hashes. Deleting an account is the one exception to an immutable log: an anonymising event, a snapshot, and the earlier events removed; listeners hear of it and forget what they hold. The books' events keep only the account's id, then a former member. An invitation's address is kept out of the project's events, so a book's log holds no one's email.
- **Two modes, one code.** Local mode has no accounts, credentials or members: one implied person owns everything. Every request carries who acts, and an `Authorizer` decides, allowing everything in local mode and checking the role in server mode, so no feature asks which mode it is in. A server may be self-hosted or, later, a platform anyone signs up to.
- **Signing up** is open or by invitation, set per installation. By invitation, an account is made with an invitation to the installation, from the operator, or to a project, from an owner; one invited into a project signs up first, then takes it up. Every account may start projects of its own; licences may limit that one day.
- **The operator** lists accounts, locks and unlocks them, and invites to the installation. They see no book unless they are a member of it, as an ordinary account. One becomes the operator only by a code the server logs at start while there is none, used once; no invitation makes an operator.

**Slices, proposed:**

1. **Who acts, through every request.** `Agent::User` becomes `Agent::Account(AccountId)`, and every request says who acts. Nothing changes for the author. *Decided at its start, 2026-10-22: local mode's one person is `Agent::Local`, which `Anonymous` becomes, since once there is signing in nobody acts unsigned on a server; and the `Authorizer` comes with slice 4, when there is something to decide, rather than as a port that only ever allows.* *Done 2026-10-22: `Agent` is `Local`, `System` or `Account(AccountId)`, stored as `local`, `system` and `account:…` and published with those kinds; an agent stored as `anonymous` before reads as `Local`. The REST handlers of the boards, ideas, outline and projects take who acts from the request, an `Extension<Agent>`, where they named nobody before, and the app puts `Agent::Local` on every request under `/api` for now; slice 2 puts the signed-in account there instead. A request with nobody on it is not served, so a route a later layer forgets fails rather than acting as anyone. Tests: an idea is captured by whoever the request says acts, and a request nobody is said to act in is not served; an `anonymous` agent stored before reads as `Local`. Mutating the capture to act as `Local` whatever the request says fails the first.*
2. **Signing up and in.** The accounts feature, event-sourced: sign-up with email and password, verified by a code through the logging `Mailer`; sessions; signing in and out, throttled; a password reset. Open sign-up only. The client's sign-up and sign-in in server mode. *Cut 2026-10-22 into four reviews, so the app is never unusable between them:* **2a** the accounts feature on the server, signing up and verifying the address; **2b** sessions over REST, signing in and out and who is signed in, not yet required; *signing in with an address nobody has runs the same Argon2 verification against a fixed stand-in hash and is refused in the same words as a wrong password, so how long it takes says nothing of whether the address has an account; Argon2's own comparison of the hashes is constant-time already, and the address needs none, being a key to look up rather than a secret;* *and only an account's current, verified address signs it in, the one its events name: while an address is being changed the account holds the new one too, a reservation until it is verified, and a password typed with it is refused like any wrong pair, after the same Argon2 work; an address and a password from two accounts never sign in, since the password is only ever looked up through the address's own account;* *Cut 2026-10-22 into **2b-1** signing in, in the domain alone; **2b-2** sessions, a port with its adapters, and the stage a session is at; **2b-3** REST and the cookie. The stage waits for 2b-2, since in the domain there is nothing yet to decide it by, and a variant nothing makes is dead code.* *2b-1 done 2026-10-22: `AccountService::sign_in` names the account an address and password sign in. The address must be held, by an account that was written, whose current address it is, with a password kept that admits what was typed; anything else is `SignInRefused`, in the same words, and an address nobody has is checked against `PasswordHash::stand_in`, hashed once like any password, so it takes as long as a real check. Only with the right password does an unverified account hear `NotVerifiedYet`, so the client can offer a new code; without it, it looks like no account. An address claimed by an account a stopped server never wrote is refused too, rather than failing as the server's fault. Nothing over REST yet. Tests: the right pair, in other letters; a wrong password and an unknown address alike; a pair from two accounts; an address held but not the account's own; an account never written; unverified with and without the right password; and an unknown address taking at least a quarter of a password check, a bound only from below, which load can only make easier, ten runs of ten passed. A token prints as `SessionToken(…)`, so no log shows one. A `Session` names its account, its `Stage`, `SignedIn` or `SecondFactorOwed`, and when it is over, thirty days on, to the second; only one signed in all the way `may_act`. The `Sessions` port opens, finds and ends a session by its digest, in memory, on PostgreSQL and on SQLite, the stage kept as a word, a word it does not know refused as no stage; `account_sessions` joins the first migrations. Over REST a throttled try is 429 `too-many-tries`, with `Retry-After` in seconds and the minutes, rounded up, for the client, which now takes 429 as a refusal and says "Too many tries. Please wait a minute" or "N minutes", in five languages, one and many apart. `serving::Problem::throttled` carries the `Retry-After` header with it. On the way, the service's constructor reached eight arguments, which clippy refused; the five stores of secrets now travel as one, `SecretStores`, made by `SecretStores::kept_by` where one store keeps them all, and a test standing in for one of them replaces just that one. Tests: the rule's ends in the domain as at 2f-1; five wrong passwords free and the next try waiting a minute, the right password after the wait signing in and clearing, each wrong one past the free doubling the wait, a waiting try not counted, an address nobody has waiting alike, the right password clearing before the address is confirmed, a day forgetting; a problem throttled saying how long to wait, one not saying nothing; over REST, the sixth try 429 with its wait and no cookie; a wait part way through a second rounded up to the next whole one, by `whole_seconds_rounded_up`, so 59.5 seconds are told as 60 and the client never knocks too early; in the browser, a minute and three minutes said. Nine mutants by hand, all caught. Sending codes is throttled next, 2f-3.* *2f-3 done 2026-10-23: every mail to an address counts as a code sent, signing up (whether the address is new or already has an account), a code sent again and a reset asked for alike, in one count per address; three are free, then the same doubling waits as signing in, forgotten after a day. The wait is asked before anything is sent and a send refused for waiting is not counted; an address nobody has is counted alike, so waiting tells nothing of whose it is; a sign-up refused for what it asks (a short password, say) sends nothing and counts nothing. Over REST it is the same 429 `too-many-tries`, and the client's forms all say it already. No sign-up throttle beyond that: per address it is this one, and many sign-ups with many addresses could only be held back by the IP address, which we refused for shared NAT; sign-up by invitation is the answer where that matters. Tests: sign-up and two codes again free and the next waiting a minute, a reset counting with the codes again, signing up again and again with one address waiting too, an address nobody has waiting alike, a waiting send not counted, an invalid sign-up not counted; over REST the fourth reset asked for 429 with its wait. Eight mutants by hand, all caught.* **2c** the client's sign-up, verification, signing in and out, and requiring a session: a server puts the signed-in account on every request and answers 401 without one, and the end-to-end tests sign in through a fixture; **2d** a password reset, and throttling, which became 2f; *the throttling includes asking for a code again, per address, since each new code starts with five fresh guesses, so resending without limit lets a patient attacker guess on for ever; asking again for an address nobody has answers at once, for a known one only after issuing and sending, which is to cost the same either way; and a code is to be compared in constant time, though five tries over a network leave the plain comparison nothing to leak.* *Decided then: whether a server keeps accounts is an explicit setting, not read from where it keeps its work, so a server on SQLite or a local installation in memory are possible.* *Done 2026-10-22, straight after 2a, rather than with 2c as first planned, since 2a had stood in for it by keeping accounts everywhere but on SQLite, the very coupling it was decided against: `WEAVELING_ACCOUNTS` is `on` or `off`, in any case and with spaces around it, unset or blank meaning `off`, since local mode is what needs no setting and must never show accounts by accident; anything else stops the server rather than being guessed at, so a server meant to require signing in cannot start open because of a typo. `Adapters::assembled` takes it and assembles the accounts only when it is on; the server's first log line says which. Accounts on SQLite are not built, so asking for both stops the server at start, saying so. Nothing sets it yet; 2c turns it on where the browser tests and a server need it. Tests: the setting read, blank, in other cases and refused; a server in memory takes sign-ups only with accounts on.* *Next after the follow-up to 2a, noted 2026-10-22: accounts on SQLite, a `SqliteSecrets` through the same conformance suite and the feature's SQLite wiring, so the SQLite journeys run with accounts on as PostgreSQL's do; SQLite is not only local mode, and the tests set it off for now only because nothing is built for it.* *Done 2026-10-22: `SqliteSecrets`, the same tables and the same one-step `UPDATE … RETURNING` for an attempt, moments as text as the SQLite catalogs keep them, through the same conformance suite, so each case now runs in memory, on PostgreSQL and on SQLite; the feature's SQLite wiring beside PostgreSQL's. The SQLite journeys run with accounts on, as PostgreSQL's do, and a server on SQLite keeps a signed-up address in its accounts file; only the test of starting again from the files keeps them off, since it is local mode's promise it tests. The test that took SQLite for local mode went: accounts off is tested in memory, apart from where the work is kept. Two mutants of the SQLite statements by hand, both caught.* *Addresses and passwords apart, done 2026-10-22, asked for after weighing several credentials per account: an account will hold a password or none, a TOTP secret, any number of security keys, recovery codes and, later, external logins, each with its own table and port, since each has its own shape and rules; what they share is only the account. `Credentials`, which kept an address with its account and password in one row, became `Addresses`, renamed `EmailAddresses` the same day since an address alone could as well be the IP one throttling counts by and `Emails` would read as the mails themselves, claiming, naming the holder of and releasing an address, and `Passwords`, keeping the latest hash per account. So an account can one day sign in without a password, by a key or elsewhere, and changing an address can hold the new one before letting the old one go, which one row per account could not, its account being unique. Signing up hashes the password first, claims the address, writes `SignedUp`, then keeps the password; if keeping it fails, the account stands without one until a reset gives it one. The first migrations changed in place. An `Account` is signed up with an address and a name, by itself, and verified once; its events hold the address and the name, nothing secret. Beside them, in their own tables, `Credentials` keep each address's account and its password's Argon2id hash, the address taken once, and `Verifications` the pending six-digit code, until when it counts and how often it was guessed wrong. An address is kept trimmed and in lower case. A password is 12 to 256 characters, kept as typed. A code lasts 15 minutes and five wrong guesses use it up; asking again draws a new one and forgets the guesses. Signing up with an address already taken is answered as any other sign-up, makes no account, and mails the owner that someone tried; asking for a code for an address nobody has, or one verified, sends nothing and says nothing; a code for an address nobody signed up with is just wrong. So nothing a stranger types tells them whether an address has an account. `libraries/mailing` holds the `Mailer` port, a `LoggingMailer` writing each mail to the server's log, and a `FakeMailbox` for the tests; for now the accounts feature picks its mailer itself, until SMTP brings a setting. `POST /api/accounts`, `/api/accounts/verification` and `/api/accounts/verification/code`; every refusal is 422 with its kind and numbers. If the event cannot be written, the claim is released, by the account that made it only, or the address would stay blocked by an account that never came to be. `Verifications` no longer has `pending` and `failed`, but `attempt`, which counts an attempt and hands back the code in one step, or says nothing was issued or the tries are used up; on PostgreSQL one `UPDATE … WHERE tried < $2 RETURNING`, which concurrent attempts wait on row by row. Two browser tests hold the room under it to at least what lies between two fields; the old rule fails both, on servers of the run's own, having passed on servers left running, which a run reuses.* *3d-6c done 2026-10-24, asked whether open signing up had the form too and whether setting up should live at `/setup`: it does now, as a page of its own, `clients/web/src/setup.rs`. Signed out, `/setup` is the form with the code; signed in and nobody operating, it is becoming the operator with the code, moved there from the instance's page; an operator it sends on to the instance's accounts; with someone else operating, it says the instance is set up and an operator can make one an operator. Setting up is offered wherever nobody runs the instance, where signing up is open as well, beside "No account yet? Sign up", and the account's menu offers it to whoever is signed in then. The instance's pages are the operators' alone and send anyone else to `/setup`. Said in five languages. Tests in the browser: `/setup` opening the form, the whole first way where signing up is open, the menu leading to becoming the operator, setting up saying so where someone operates, the instance sending the others to `/setup`, and an operator giving up the role being told the instance is set up; every account-related spec passing, seventy-nine. Six mutants in the browser, five caught; the sixth showed going on to the instance after becoming the operator was done twice, by the page and by its redirect, so the page's own went.* *3d-5 cut in four: **3d-5a** chores, background work a feature hands over and the server does now and then, **3d-5b** the invitations' list forgetting what is long settled, **3d-5c** the accounts' tickets keeping when they ended and being cleared, **3d-5d** "Invite again" in the client.* *3d-5a done 2026-10-24: `wiring::Chore`, a name and `done()` saying how many things it cleared, handed over by `Wired::doing_chores` and gathered by `Assembled::chores` and `Adapters::chores()`; `Chores` in the server does every chore at start and then once an hour, `CHORES_EVERY`, logging what was cleared, warning of a chore that failed and going on with the rest, and stopping with the server before the outbox's relays do. No feature hands one over yet. The operator code's line in the log now says to open `/setup`, not `/instance`; the wiring's README lost a link to a roadmap heading that is no more. Tests: a chore done at once and again each round, one failing keeping neither itself nor the others from coming round again, stopped chores done no more, and the next round waiting its time; ten runs of ten. Four mutants by hand, all caught, one by hanging.* *3d-5b done 2026-10-24: the invitation keeps when it was settled, `settled_at`, set as it is accepted, withdrawn or declined and kept in its snapshot, the stored one reading it as none where it was written before. Its summary says when it may be forgotten, `forgotten_after`, decided as it is remembered: an open one thirty days after it runs out, `EXPIRED_LISTED_FOR`, so the operators see it expired for a month; a declined one thirty days after, `DECLINED_LISTED_FOR`, so they see why; one taken up or withdrawn a day after, `SETTLED_KEPT_FOR`, being off the list already. `InvitationCatalog::forget_due(now)` forgets what is past its moment and counts it, in memory, on PostgreSQL and on SQLite, the column added to the list's 0001; the feature hands over the chore `forget-due-invitations`, and the server does it with the others. Expired invitations' events stay, the address in them with them; whether an address should be wiped from an old invitation's events joins the security review. Tests: the moment settled and none for the open; each kind's moment; the stored snapshot reading back when it settled; in each store what is due forgotten and counted, one at its very moment kept, and a newer summary's moment winning; the feature's chore forgetting the due; the server doing it only where there are accounts. Fifteen mutants by hand, all caught.* *Reshaped after review 2026-10-24: each chore says how often it comes round, `Chore::every()`, and has a round of its own, so an hourly one never waits on a daily one, the server's single `CHORES_EVERY` gone; `done()` became `carry_out()`, since `chore.done()` read as asking whether it was; the trait and its runner left `wiring` and the server for a library of their own, `libraries/chores`, with a README, as the outbox's relay lives in `outbox`; `ForgetDueInvitations` has a file of its own, `features/invitations/wiring/src/forgetting.rs`, and comes round hourly. Tests: each chore at its own pace, stopping not waiting for a slow one's next round, beside the four before; ten runs of ten. Six mutants by hand in the new places, all caught, one by hanging.* *3d-5c reshaped, asked whether accounts should clean their tickets by a rule of their own: no, invitations tell them. An invitation retires, an event of its own, so the cleaning lives in one feature and the accounts' tickets need neither a chore nor a time: **3d-5c-1** invitations retiring, **3d-5c-2** accounts dropping the ticket of a retired invitation.* *3d-5c-1 done 2026-10-24: `InvitationCommand::Retire` and `InvitationEvent::Retired`, any invitation retiring once, how it stood unchanged, the snapshot keeping that it did; stored and told as `invitation.retired`, which accounts hear and let be for now. The summary's moment is now `retire_after`, and one taken up or withdrawn retires a day after or once it runs out, whichever is later, so a late or re-driven issue can only ever make a ticket that has run out. `InvitationCatalog::forget_due` gave way to `due_for_retiring(now, at_most)`, soonest first, and `forget(invitation)`; the list's listener forgets an invitation whose latest standing is retired, however late any message about it comes. Accounts need no chore and no time of their own: invitations say when, and never before an invitation runs out, so a ticket made again afterwards from a late issue has run out already. Tests: in each store a dropped ticket leaving nothing, open, taken or marked, and the others staying; through the listener a retired invitation leaving no ticket nor mark, and retirements heard.
