Three mutants in the browser, all caught. **3d is done.*** *Changed after review 2026-10-25: an invitation run out no longer offers "Withdraw", since its link already leads nowhere and withdrawing only hid it early and wrote withdrawn where it ran out; it offers "Invite again" alone and leaves the list when it retires. The server still lets one be withdrawn, harmlessly. The browser test holds it, and the old rule fails it.* *Tidied 2026-10-25, three small things in one slice as asked: the accounts' list has its case for a page filled exactly, which promises no next, so the survivor of 3d-3f-3 is caught; every free function still imported by name is called through its module, as CONVENTIONS.md asks, twenty-three files that imported `published_in` or `aggregate_in` and fourteen more, among them `window` in the client, `kept::keep` and `catalogue::knows` where a method of the same name had made the import tempting, the outbox's `enqueue` in its tests, `wiring::assemble` in the server and the tests' `wiring::at` and `wiring::an_operator`, a scan of every `use` finding only modules and macros left; and the whole browser suite run.*
3. **The operator.** *Noted 2026-10-22 for this slice and the password reset in 2d: a session is checked before it is opened, so a password changed or an account locked in between leaves a session opened on what was before; a transaction across the ports would not help, since the events and the secrets are kept apart. So a session is to carry when it began, the account to record, in its events, when its sessions last stopped counting, by a lock or a new password, and finding a session to treat any begun before that as over; ending every session of an account is then a comparison rather than a sweep, whichever side of the change a session landed on.* The code logged at start, listing, locking and unlocking accounts, invitations to the installation, and sign-up by invitation. *Decided 2026-10-23:* **the code** is redeemed by an account signed in, which covers open sign-up, where one may have an account before there is an operator; and sign-up takes it in place of an invitation, since by invitation nobody could sign up before there is an operator to invite. It is logged at every start while there is no operator, so restarting the server always recovers one. **More operators, one way only:** an operator makes an existing account an operator, and takes the role away again, the last operator kept, as the last owner of a project is; someone new is invited to the installation as anyone is, and made an operator once signed up, so invitations stay without roles and no invitation makes an operator. An operator cannot be locked, by another operator or themselves; the role is taken away first. **A locked account** hears "This account is locked" at signing in, but only after the right password, as "not verified yet", so it tells nothing to whoever does not know the password. **An invitation** is a link mailed with a random token, bound to the address invited and good for 7 days; it signs up that address only. **Invitations are a feature of their own**, `features/invitations`, which slice 5 extends with invitations into projects. *Cut the same day by layer:* **3a** becoming the operator: **3a-1** the account's `BecameOperator`; **3a-2** an `Operators` port beside the secrets, keeping the code, hashed, latest only, and who the operators are, so the server knows at start whether there is one without reading every account; **3a-3** drawing the code and redeeming it in the service; **3a-4** the code drawn and logged at start while there is no operator, and redeeming it over REST; **3a-5** the client's place to enter it. *Cut finer at its start, 2026-10-23, a layer each.* *3a-1 done 2026-10-23: `AccountCommand::BecomeOperator` records `BecameOperator`, once, and only for an account whose address is verified, `AccountError::NotVerifiedYet` otherwise, since an operator's address must be shown to be theirs; `Account::is_operator` says so, and the snapshot and the codec carry it, without a default for snapshots written before, there being no real data yet. Tests: an account no operator until it becomes one, becoming one twice changing nothing, an unverified address refused, nothing before signing up, the snapshot holding it, and the codec carrying the event and the snapshot. Found by a mutant that lived: a snapshot applied to an account already standing, as happens when a stream holds one after earlier events, was tested for no field at all; a test now applies a further account's snapshot to one behind it. Nine mutants by hand, all caught.* *3a-2 done 2026-10-23: `Operators` beside the other secret stores keeps the code that makes an operator, as its digest, one at a time, a new one replacing the last, so a code logged at an earlier start is good no longer; `take_code` takes it only if it is the one kept, once, in one statement, so twenty sent together give it to one; and it keeps who has been appointed, `any_appointed` saying whether anyone is, for the server to ask at start. Kept in memory, on PostgreSQL and on SQLite, in two tables of `0001`, `account_operator_code` holding a single row, its key `single` a boolean that can only be true, so keeping a new code is one upsert, and `account_operators`. Not yet among `SecretStores`: the service takes it in 3a-3, with the first thing that asks it. Tests: six cases of the conformance suite on all three, a code taken once, only the latest taken, none kept taking nothing, many at once giving it to one, nobody an operator until appointed, appointing twice as once. Fifteen mutants by hand, all caught.* *3a-3 done 2026-10-23, 3a-3 having been cut in two at its start, the service apart from the server: an `OperatorCode` is 128 random bits in hex, 32 characters to copy from the log, kept only as its SHA-256 as a session token is, never written out by `Debug`, and taken with the space around it trimmed, as a copy from a terminal brings. `draw_operator_code` draws one and keeps it while nobody is appointed, and draws nothing once someone is; `become_operator` asks first that the account's address be verified, then takes the code, records `BecameOperator` and appoints the account, so a code refused for the address is still good once the address is verified, and a wrong one is `OperatorCodeWrong`. `Operators` joins `SecretStores`. No throttle on guessing: 128 bits are not guessed. Tests: the code drawn makes a verified account an operator and appointed, none drawn once there is one, a code making one operator only, an earlier code good no longer, a wrong code making none, an unverified address refused with the code left for after; the code's length, its trimming and its `Debug`. Nine mutants by hand, all caught. *Asked on review: what if the server goes down before appointing?* The account is an operator by its events, but nobody is appointed, so the next start draws a code again, and redeeming it records nothing new, being what the account already is, and appoints it; until then a fresh code stands in the log, which only those who could read the first one can see. The order is on purpose: appointing first and going down before the event would leave someone appointed and nobody an operator, and no code drawn ever again. A test fails appointing once, sees the account an operator yet a code drawn again, and the next code finish it; it alone catches the order swapped. Whether to appoint instead by a listener on `BecameOperator`, through the outbox, so no crash can leave it undone, is for 3c, where keeping the last operator counts on the appointed being right.* *3a-4 done 2026-10-23: at start, with accounts on and nobody appointed, the server draws a code and warns "there is no operator yet: sign in and become one with the code …", seen so on a server started for it; with accounts off it draws nothing. `POST /api/operators` with `{ "code": … }` makes whoever is signed in an operator, 204, behind the same gate as the work: the accounts' routes now come in two, `router` open and `guarded_router` behind signing in, and the gate hands on the account's own id beside the `Agent`, so a route of the accounts needs no id read back from the agent. A wrong code is 422 `operator-code-wrong`, said in five languages; `GET /api/sessions/current` says `operator` now, for the client of 3a-5. Tests: over REST the code making whoever is signed in an operator, a wrong code refused as such, nobody becoming one without signing in; in the service's wiring, the route behind the gate, and a code drawn at start only with accounts on. Seven mutants by hand, all caught, among them the accounts' guarded routes left out, and left open.* *3a-5 done 2026-10-23: the client's own page, `/instance`, first `/operator` and renamed on review with its heading, with no way to it in any menu until one is the operator, so nobody else is shown it; the log line says to open it. Signed in and not the operator, it asks for "the code the server wrote to its log when it started" and, taken, turns into the operator's page, which 3b-3 fills with the accounts; the operator reaches it again by "Manage instance", "Instanz verwalten", in the account's menu, which is also the page's heading; without accounts it says there is no operator to become. The client keeps whether the account is the operator, as `/api/sessions/current` says. In German "Betrieb übernehmen" for becoming one, without a person's gender; and an installation is "this Weaveling instance", "diese Weaveling-Instanz", in every language, as the author put it. Tests in the browser, against the stand-in server: the code making whoever is signed in the operator, the way back through the menu, a wrong code said and making none, and without accounts no code asked for. Six mutants by hand, all caught. *Asked the same day: how does anyone sign up when signing up is by invitation only?* As decided for slice 3: the sign-up form asks for an invitation, and the code from the log does there too, so the first account signs up with it, verifies its address, and only then is made the operator, `become_operator` asking for a verified address; that is built with the invitations, in 3d-3 and 3d-4.* **3b** locking, *decided at its start, 2026-10-23: a locked account asking for a reset is sent the code as anyone is, since refusing would tell that it is locked, and the reset may go through, but signing in stays refused, so a reset never unlocks; the operator gives a reason for locking, kept in the event, for the list of accounts to show; unlocking brings no session back, the account signs in anew:* **3b-1** `Locked` and `Unlocked` on the account, a lock setting `sessions_since` so every session ends at once; **3b-2** locking and unlocking in the service, by operators only, and a locked account refused at signing in; **3b-3** the accounts listed as a read model, cut at its start 2026-10-23 into **3b-3a** the accounts publishing their events, **3b-3b** an `AccountCatalog` port with its adapters, guarded by version, and **3b-3c** a listener keeping it, wired into the server; **3b-4** listing, locking and unlocking over REST, for operators only, 403 for anyone else; **3b-5** the client's page of the instance. *Cut finer at its start, 2026-10-23, a layer each, as 3a was.* *3b-1 done 2026-10-23: an account holds its locks keyed by who locked it, a `LockKind`, so each is lifted only by its own: for now `Operator` alone, and `PendingDeletion` with slice 9, whose waiting must not be ended by an operator's unlocking, nor an operator's lock by undoing a deletion; it is locked while it holds any. *Reworked on review the same day from a single lock, once deleting an account was decided to wait 30 days.* `AccountCommand::Lock(kind, reason)`, the reason up to 500 characters, never blank, a few lines allowed, records `Locked`, and the account keeps that kind's `Lock`, its reason and since when, setting `sessions_since` so every session begun before ends; locking twice by one kind changes nothing, not even the reason; an operator is refused, `AccountError::OperatorCannotBeLocked`. `Unlock(kind)` records `Unlocked` and lifts that kind's lock but leaves `sessions_since`, so no session ended by the lock comes back; unlocking a kind not locked changes nothing. The snapshot and the codec carry the lock, an unreadable reason reading as nothing. Tests: locked for a reason from the moment it is, sessions ended, twice as once, an operator refused, unlocking lifting the lock but no session, unlocking the unlocked as nothing, neither before signing up, the reason's rules, the snapshot, applied and born, and the codec. Fifteen mutants by hand, all caught.* *3b-2 done 2026-10-23: `AccountService::lock(by, account, reason)` and `unlock(by, account)`, refused `NotAnOperator` unless `by` is an operator, the reason refused `InvalidLockReason` when blank or too long, the lock told as the operator's doing, and an operator locking themselves refused by the account. Signing in with a locked account is refused `Locked`, but only once the password is right, so a wrong one is refused as ever and tells nothing of the lock; a locked account may still ask for a reset and set a new password, and stays locked. *Found by its first test:* a session begun in the same instant as the lock was still admitted, since `sessions_since` counts a session begun at that moment as begun after it, which is right for a new password and wrong for a lock, and the same gap lets in a session whose signing in was checked just before the lock and begun just after. So `signed_in` admits no session of a locked account at all, and `sessions_since` keeps the sessions the lock ended ended once it is lifted. Not yet over REST: a locked account signing in would be answered 500 until 3b-3 names the refusal, which nothing can reach before 3b-3 lets anyone lock. Tests: a lock ending the sessions at once, told as the operator's doing, a locked account refused at signing in only with the right password, only an operator locking or unlocking, an unlocked account signing in again, an operator not locking themselves, no lock without a reason, a reset leaving it locked, and unlocking bringing no session back. Seven mutants by hand, all caught.* *3b-3a done 2026-10-23: the accounts publish their events as every feature does, `accounts_messaging::message_for` in a crate of its own, in place of `nothing_told`, on `account.signed.up`, `account.email.verified`, `account.password.reset`, `account.became.operator`, `account.locked` and `account.unlocked`, all under `account.#`; the test wiring publishes the same way rather than by a stand-in of its own. **Nothing personal travels:** no address, name or reason, only which event happened to which account, and a lock's kind; messages are kept, and refused ones shown under `/api/service/refusals`, which is open even with accounts on, and the catalog of 3b-3c reads the account itself, as the projects' catalog does. Nobody listens yet, which the dispatcher counts as quiet, not as a failure. Tests: every event on the key the contract declares, the subscription covering them, the names on the wire, nothing personal in any message, a lock and an unlock saying whose, no snapshot published, and the account read back out of a message. Four mutants by hand; the one that lived told a snapshot, which the library never asks the body for, dropping snapshots by their metadata first and tested so; the arm stays only because the match must name every event.* *Asked on review of 3b-3a, 2026-10-23: a free function is called through its module rather than imported, `eventpublishing::message_for` instead of `use … as message_carrying`; written into CONVENTIONS.md and swept through the whole codebase at once, 209 files, nothing but imports and call sites, by a script that told a function from a module or a macro by how each file uses the name, called through the module's shortest path that clashes with nothing, and left two values shadowed by a parameter of the same name to be done by hand. Run first on a copy of the index until the workspace, the wasm client and every test passed; then 2109 Rust tests, 132 of the client and 680 in the browser.* *3b-3b done 2026-10-23: `AccountCatalog` in the core, `AccountSummary::of` an account at its version, its name, address, whether verified, whether an operator, when it signed up and its locks; kept by `features/accounts/adapters/catalog` in memory, on PostgreSQL and on SQLite, in `account_summaries`, the locks as JSON in a column of their own, read whole for the list only; on PostgreSQL a `JSONB` column, *asked for on review*, so the database checks it on writing and the list can later be filtered by its locks there, written and read as text through a cast so both adapters share one shape; on SQLite `TEXT`, which its JSON functions read as well. **Guarded by version from the start:** a summary older than the one kept changes nothing, since messages may come late or twice, which the projects' catalog still lacks (see Later). **Paged from the start**, newest first, `listed_after(after, at_most)` going on past the last account of the page before, since an instance open to anyone may hold many; the id orders it, under the C collation on PostgreSQL. Nothing feeds it yet; 3b-3c brings the listener. Tests: six cases of a conformance suite on all three, an account listed as it was, nothing listed when empty, a newer summary replacing an older, an older arriving late changing nothing, the newest first, and a page holding at most what was asked with the next going on after it; the collation; and the summary of an account in the core. Nineteen mutants by hand, all caught, after making a case tell verified and operator apart.* *3b-3c done 2026-10-23: `CatalogueAccountsListener`, in `accounts_messaging` beside the publisher, hears `account.#`, reads the account as it now stands and remembers its summary, so a message come late or twice still leaves the newest; `accounts_wiring::wire` lists it, the feature's `Ports` carry the catalog on each backend, and the server registers the accounts' listeners beside the features', wrapped for flakiness alike. The accounts' test bench now runs on a real dispatcher and the feature's own wiring, as the projects' does, and settles by relaying and draining. **Found on the way, by the journeys on PostgreSQL:** the catalog's `0001` and the secrets' `0001` both went into the default ledger of the accounts database, which refused the second as "migration 1 was previously applied but has been modified"; the catalog keeps a ledger of its own now, `_sqlx_migrations_account_summaries`, as the libraries do, so would have any local accounts database. Tests: an account listed once it has signed up, and verifying, becoming the operator, locking and unlocking reaching the list; the ledger. Four mutants by hand; the one that lived, the server not registering the accounts' listeners, waits for 3b-4, whose list over REST is the first thing outside the feature to read the catalog: a journey there signs up and finds the account listed, and the mutant is run again.* *3b-4 done 2026-10-23: for operators only, behind the same gate as the work, `GET /api/instance/accounts` lists the accounts a hundred at a time, newest first, each with its name, address, whether verified and whether an operator, when it signed up and its locks, the next page asked for by `?after=` the `next` the page before gave; `PUT /api/instance/accounts/{id}/lock` with `{ "reason": … }` locks it and `DELETE` on the same unlocks it, both 204, a lock given twice changing nothing. Under `/instance`, as the client's page is, rather than beside `POST /api/accounts`, since the gate wraps the guarded routes before they are merged with the open ones, and one path in both would leave its methods guarded apart. The service lists through the catalog, which `AccountService::new` now takes, asking first that whoever asks be an operator. The refusals with no name of their own got one: `account-locked` 403 at signing in, `not-an-operator` 403, `operator-cannot-be-locked` 409, `lock-reason-blank`, `-control-character` and `-too-long` 422, the last with its numbers, `account-id-invalid` 400 and `account-not-found` 404, each said in five languages. Tests: over REST, the operator listing every account newest first with what it is, locking for a reason and unlocking, the lock shown in the list and refusing the sign-in, anyone else refused for all three, nobody signed out reaching them, a reason refused by its kind, the operator not locked, an account missing or no account's id said so, and a long list a page at a time; on the server, an account signed up, verified by the code from the mail drop, made the operator by the code drawn at start, and found in the list once relayed. Twelve mutants by hand, all caught, among them 3b-3c's that lived, the server not registering the accounts' listeners. *Found before handing it over:* the listing test failed in 13 of 30 runs, since both its accounts signed up in the same instant of the fixed clock, and an account's id sorts by time only to the millisecond, the rest being random; the test now lets a minute pass between them, and passed 30 runs of 30. Accounts signed up in the same millisecond keep no order between them, which the list may live with.* *3b-5 done 2026-10-23: the page of the instance lists the accounts for its operator, under the heading "Accounts", each with its name, address and when it signed up, marked "Address not confirmed", "Operator" or "Locked since …" with its reason below, and "Show more" fetching the next page while there is one. "Lock…" opens a dialog naming the account, saying it is signed out at once and the reason kept and shown, and asking for the reason; a refusal is said in the dialog, which stays. "Unlock" acts at once, being undone as easily. The operator's own row offers neither. A lock or an unlock is shown as soon as the server took it, not by fetching the list again, since the catalog hears of it only a moment later through its listener, and a list fetched at once could still show the account as it was. Only the operator's lock is shown as one; other kinds, as pending deletion, come with their slices. The placeholder "The accounts will be listed here." went, in all five languages. Tests: in the client, a page read as the server sends it, only the operator's lock shown as one, and a moment that is none refused; in the browser against the stand-in server, every account with what it is, locking for a reason and unlocking, a lock without a reason refused with the dialog staying, cancelling locking nobody, the dialog's hint standing apart from the reason, and a long list a page at a time. Nine mutants by hand, all caught; the whole browser suite, 686, passed.* *Tidied on review the same day: the lock dialog left `listing.rs` for `lock_dialog.rs`, and knows the list no more, being given the account, what to do once it is locked and how to close; and the list's parts went one to a file, `listed.rs` what the server lists, plain data, `roster.rs` the `Roster`, the list as the page holds it, loading, paging, marking and unlocking, and `listing.rs` only what is shown.* **3c** more operators: making an account an operator and taking it away, the last kept, in the domain, over REST and on the operator page. *Decided at its start, 2026-10-23:* **appointing stays where it is, not in a listener**, as weighed at 3a-3: a listener would count the operators only a moment late, and two operators taking each other's role at once would both still see two and leave none; so `Operators` keeps the last one in a single statement, taking the role away from the table first and telling it after, and making an operator tells it first and appoints after, so a crash anywhere leaves the table counting too few, which at worst refuses a dismissal that could have passed, and doing it again mends it. **A locked account cannot become an operator**, or no-one-locked-is-an-operator could be gone round by locking first. **An operator may give up their own role**, but not the last one. The role counts at once, being read from the account on every request; no session ends. *Cut the same day, a layer each:* **3c-1** the account's `StoppedBeingOperator`; **3c-2** `Operators::dismiss`, keeping the last, with its adapters; **3c-3** the service and REST, `PUT` and `DELETE /api/instance/accounts/{id}/operator`; **3c-4** the page of the instance, "Make operator" and "Take away the operator role", each asked again first. *3c-1 done 2026-10-23: `AccountCommand::StopBeingOperator` records `StoppedBeingOperator` for an operator and nothing for anyone else, so taking a role away twice is no failure; `BecomeOperator` refuses a locked account, `AccountError::LockedCannotBecomeOperator`. The codec stores it, and it is published on `account.stopped.being.operator`, so the list hears of it. Tests: an operator stopping being one, no operator stopping being none, a locked account not becoming one, nothing before signing up, and the event in the codec's and the publisher's lists. Five mutants by hand, all caught.* *3c-2 done 2026-10-23: `Operators::dismiss` takes an operator out of the appointed and says so, `Dismissed`, or refuses to take the last one, `LastOne`, or finds nobody to take, `NotAppointed`. Keeping the last one is where two at once matter, and each backend does it its own way: in memory behind the lock; on PostgreSQL in a transaction that first holds every operator's row, `SELECT … FOR UPDATE`, since a single `DELETE … WHERE (SELECT count(*) …) > 1` lets two transactions taking two different operators each count two and leave none, while held rows make the second wait and then find the first's gone; on SQLite in one statement, as it has one writer at a time, asking afterwards only to tell the last one from nobody. Tests: four cases of the conformance suite on all three, one of two dismissed and the next dismissal finding her gone, the last kept, someone never appointed not dismissed, and ten dismissed at once leaving one. Without `FOR UPDATE` that last case failed five runs of five on PostgreSQL, and with it all three passed ten of ten. Seven mutants by hand, all caught.* *3c-3 done 2026-10-23: `AccountService::appoint_operator(by, account)` tells `BecomeOperator` first and appoints after, so an account refusing the role, its address not confirmed or it being locked, is never appointed; `dismiss_operator(by, account)` takes the account from the appointed first, refused `LastOperator` for the last, and tells `StopBeingOperator` after, so the last keeps the role in the events too; one not found among the appointed is told all the same, which is what mends a dismissal broken off between the two. Both only for an operator. 4. **Projects have members.** The `Authorizer`: a port deciding whether who acts may do a thing in a project, allowing everything in local mode. Whoever starts a project owns it; the list holds only one's own projects; every route and every live socket asks the `Authorizer`, and the projects' channel tells each follower only their projects. it should be declined so when the address signs up. *5d-6c-2a done:* the invitation keeps its token's digest, from `Issued` into its state, its snapshot and the stored snapshot; the list keeps it in a `digest` column, unique, on memory, PostgreSQL and SQLite, and `InvitationCatalog::with_digest` finds the invitation a link was for, however it stands, until it is forgotten. 1 conformance case on all three, 1 core case (through a snapshot, either way), 1 case finding a catalogued invitation by the digest of the token mailed for it; 12 mutants, all caught, one more dropped as equivalent (the digest of the summary `invite` answers with, which nothing reads). *5d-6c-2b done, cut again since features had no routes open to anyone:* `Wired::serving_anyone` and `Assembled::open_routes`, which the server merges beside `/service`, outside the sign-in guard; `InvitationsCore::decline_by_token` finds the invitation by the token's digest, refuses one run out (`invitation-expired`) and one nobody was given, taken up or withdrawn (`invitation-invalid`), and declines it `TurnedDown` as the system; `POST /api/invitations/decline` with the token, answering 204, not throttled as a token is 256 random bits. 5 cases in invitations, 1 on the server (served without signing in where there are accounts, not at all without); 11 mutants, all caught, one dropped as equivalent (trimming the token again). *5d-6c-2c done:* accounts' `keep-invitation-tickets` hears `DECLINED` too and forgets the ticket, as on `WITHDRAWN`, so a link turned down signs nobody up after, nor an issue told again. 1 listener case; a journey on PostgreSQL and SQLite: Grace, who has an account, is invited into Ada's project, turns it down by the link signed out, the link then signs nobody up (`invitation-invalid` where it was `email-address-has-account`, nothing made either way) and Ada sees it turned down. 2 mutants, both caught. With this 5d-6c is done; next 5d-7a, the bell. *5d-7a done:* a bell in the masthead, beside the account, there only while signed in: it lists `/api/invitations/mine` and asks again on every frame of `/api/invitations/mine/live` and on every reconnect, the frames carrying nothing but that something changed; its label and a badge tell how many wait ("Invitations: 2 waiting"). Opened, each invitation shows its project's name ("a project" when not yet known), "From Ada · Author", Join and Decline, answered at `…/invitations/{id}/accept` or `/decline`; the answered one leaves the list and a quiet note says so ("You joined Shared. It appears in your list in a moment."), a refusal shows in the bell. Signing out drops the bell and with it its socket. On a phone the bell's list spans the page, less a gutter. Texts in all five catalogues. 3 unit cases, 9 browser cases (both answers, live arrival, a refusal, signed out, German, 1280 and 320 px); 9 mutants, the bell's own through the browser, all caught. The whole browser suite: 749 passed, one flaky (below). The bell moves to the one socket with M17b. *Reworked the same day, the user asking that other notifications fit in later, not only invitations:* the bell is the client's `notifications` module, holding `Notice`s, an enum with one kind so far, `Invitation`, each in a `Group` with a heading of its own ("Invitations") under "Notifications" ("Notifications: 2 new", "Nothing new just now."); a kind of notice brings its own source, which lists and follows its channel, and its own row and answers, as `notifications::invited` does for invitations. With M17b a source is a topic on the one socket. *Noted for later:* notifications that are not invitations (a role changed, a mention, a comment) want a feature of their own on the server, keeping them with read and dismissed, designed when the first of them comes; until then the client gathers its sources. 2 more mutants on the grouping, both caught. *Scheduled the same day, the user naming a changed role as the first such notification:* **5g, notifications kept for the account**, right after 5e (and 5f). 5e tells a role changed while one is online, with a quiet note on `access-changed`; but one changed while away should still be told on coming back, which neither the client nor a socket can do. So a `notifications` feature of its own on the server: it hears `members.role.changed` (later mentions, comments and the like), keeps a notice for the account it names, with when, read and dismissed; `GET /api/notifications` lists the account's own, `POST /api/notifications/{id}/dismissed` puts one away, and they are told live (a topic on M17b's socket). In the client, a second source beside the invitations, a `Notice::RoleChanged` ("Ada made you an author of Shared"), dismissed from the bell; removed and left projects perhaps next, as notices of the same kind. Old notices retire as invitations do. *5d-7b done:* the invitation's page answers either way. Signed in, Accept and Decline side by side (below each other on a phone), Decline at `…/invitations/decline` with the link's token, then the projects and a quiet note, "You turned down the invitation."; signed out, beneath signing up, "Decline the invitation instead", at `/api/invitations/decline` without an account, then "Invitation declined" and that whoever invited sees it and the link signs nobody up any more. A link run out or leading nowhere says so where it was declined and stays. Texts in all five catalogues. The page does not name the project or who invited, a link alone telling nothing; it could once invitations answer what a token is for, noted for later. 8 browser cases; 4 mutants, all caught. The mocked server's withdrawing answered `POST /api/invitations/decline` too, being registered later, and now answers DELETE alone. With this 5d-7 is done; next 5d-8, the Share dialog and avatars. *Proposed 2026-10-29 by the user, for after slice 5: **invitations by link**, shared by text message or anywhere else, so nobody needs a mailbox to be invited.* Today's link is never shown to whoever invites, only mailed, and that is what lets it prove the address: signing up through it skips confirming the address, as the link could only have come through that mailbox. Shown to the inviter, it would let them sign up under someone else's address without that address ever being confirmed. So not the mailed invitation's link shown, but a second kind of invitation, **bound to no address**: an owner (and the operator, for the instance) chooses a role and is shown a link once, to copy; whoever opens it joins signed in, or signs up with their own address, confirmed by a code as any sign-up is. One use by default, seven days as now; listed in the Invited tab as "Link · Author · open until …", withdrawn like any other. Questions for then: more than one use (a link for a writing group), whether it counts against throttling, and how the operator's invite-only signing up treats it. *5d-8 cut in two, from the design drafts (A's Share dialog over B's Members page): 5d-8a the members' avatars in the top bar, 5d-8b the Share button and dialog.* *5d-8a done:* beside the project's name, signed in, the people icon becomes a stack of the members' initials, three and "+N" more (on a phone the first and "+N"), each in one of five tints chosen steadily from the account's id, in both themes; labelled with every member's name ("Members: Ada Lovelace, Grace Hopper, Not known by name yet"), leading to the Members page as before; while the members are not yet known it is the people icon still. The account's own button wears its tint too, so one is the same colour in both places. `avatar` holds the initial (moved from the account's menu) and the tint. The stack asks once on opening the project; it follows changes with 5e's live members. 1 unit case more, 3 browser cases; 6 mutants, all caught. *Noted 2026-10-29 by the user: the masthead on a phone holds too much by now* (the wordmark, the project's name and details, the members' stack, the theme, the language, the bell, the account, with the views below) *and will not fit what is still coming.* On a phone, some of it moves into a menu behind a vertical three-dots button at the far right; which things go there is decided when it is built (likely the theme and the language). Before the Share button of 5d-8b adds to it. *Done 2026-10-30:* up to 30rem wide the masthead holds no theme and no language any more but, at its far right after the account, a vertical three-dots **More** button, opening a menu with the theme's three swatches and the language. The masthead draws them in one place or the other, never both, by a width it follows as the window is resized, so a choice made in one is the one the other shows. The phone's one button turning through the three themes is gone with it, the menu holding all three side by side. 2 browser cases in its place (choosing both from the menu, and growing past a phone's width brings them back), the masthead's fitting checked with the menu; 4 mutants, all caught. *5d-8b done:* signed in, beside the members' stack, **Share** (its icon alone on a phone) opens the dialog of draft A: "Share “The Silent Loom”", for an owner the invite form at the top (address and role), then the people with access, then who is invited, and a link to the Members page with all of it; one who does not own the project sees the people alone, and the invitations are not even asked for (5e hides more of what a role may not do). It is built of the Members page's own parts: the invitations' listing is split into its form and its list, sharing one state, which is asked for only once the project is known to be one's own. Texts in all five catalogues. 5 browser cases; 4 mutants, all caught. With this 5d is done. *Seen with it:* on a phone, signed in inside a project, the masthead leaves the project's name a letter or two (the stack, Share, the bell, the account and More beside it); to settle with the masthead's More, perhaps Share and the members' stack moving into it on a phone. *Decided 2026-10-29, the user proposing it:* after 5d and before 5e comes [M17b](#milestone-17b--one-socket-for-everything-live), one socket for everything live, so that 5e's live members state and 5f's closing on the session's end are built once, on it; the bell of 5d-7a follows `/invitations/mine/live` until then and moves over with the rest. 2 cases (101 invitations across two pages, and one taken up, swept twice); 6 mutants, the check for an open invitation surviving, as withdrawing a declined one changes nothing, and so gone. *Noted the same day, the user asking that old invitations be cleaned up:* they retire already (the chore `retire-due-invitations`: the list forgets one and accounts drop its ticket, a month after it ran out or was declined, a day after it was taken up or withdrawn), but an invitation's events stay in the store for good, and they hold the address it went to. Deleting a retired invitation's stream some time after it retired is the first time the event store would delete anything, so it goes with slice 9's deleting someone's data, beside sweeping the dead letters. *Planned for the client, before 5e:* a toast, announced politely (`aria-live`), with its texts in the five catalogues; then in 5e, on `access-changed` the client asks its role again and, if it differs, says "Your role has been changed to …"; on 4403 it stops reconnecting, says it no longer has access and goes back to the projects. *Changed in review the same day, the user pointing out that a project and a role apart can be half set, and would look odd beside any later kind of invitation: the scope crosses every boundary as one tagged value, `ScopeDTO`, `{ "kind": "instance" }` or `{ "kind": "project", "project": …, "role": … }`, in the invite request, the summary and the issued message, defaulting to the instance; the stored event keeps it so too, `StoredScope`, an invitation stored before scopes still reading as the instance's. Half a scope no longer deserialises at all, so a project invitation's missing role is a malformed request rather than `role-unknown`, which now answers a role nobody knows alone. The catalog keeps its two columns, read back only together. 5 mutants by hand on what moved, all caught.* *And whether an operator issued it is kept in the invitation's state and its snapshot too, the user asking: until then it was told once, in `Issued`, for accounts to keep, as the digest is, since nothing the invitation decides depends on it; kept, it answers `is_by_an_operator` should invitations ever need it, and a snapshot no longer forgets it. The stored snapshot defaults it to false. 1 new case and a snapshot not an operator's in the codec's round trip, after its mutant survived; 5 mutants, all caught.*
   **After 5, before 6: writes that hold together.** *Scheduled 2026-10-29, the user asking, from [the transaction audit](./TODO.md).* Before the trash adds more writes across ports, in this order: the snapshot after an append made best-effort, so a landed command is never reported failed and appended twice; the [`transacting` library](#later--polish-and-what-can-wait), [shaped in ARCHITECTURE](./ARCHITECTURE.md#a-transaction-in-the-port-signature--decided), the event store joining it and the inbox with it, then its first users — the invitation ticket with the account's append (taking up, signing up by invitation, declining), and the registry claim with `Start`; [tombstones on every catalog that forgets](#later--polish-and-what-can-wait), so `remember` cannot bring back what is gone; the throttle counting before it decides; then the rest of the audit's items that do not heal, each as its own slice. The items that need a second consumer stay with [M17a](#milestone-17a--hosted-for-many-ten-thousand-at-once) unless one is cheap on the way.
6. **The trash.** A deleted project restorable for 30 days.
7. **TOTP**, with recovery codes.
8. **FIDO2 keys.**
9. **Deleting an account**, collapsed as above, *after waiting 30 days, decided 2026-10-23:* deleting first locks the account by `LockKind::PendingDeletion`, ending its sessions, and the account is collapsed only once the 30 days are over, so a hijacked account cannot be wiped for good and a mistake can be undone, by signing in, which offers to undo it, or by the operator; the confirmation says the data stays these 30 days. Whether an operator can be deleted, and what their role becomes, is decided here. *Noted 2026-10-22: every secret beside the events goes too, so each store gains its `forget(account)` here, with the first slice that calls it: `Passwords`, the pending codes, and by then the TOTP secret, the security keys and the recovery codes; `EmailAddresses` already lets go of an address by `release`. A password is not released, as an address is, since it blocks no one else; removing one before deletion comes only with signing in without a password.* *Noted 2026-10-24, asked to keep in mind: retired invitations are collapsed the same way, here, with the account's own: once `Retired` is applied, an `Anonymized` event takes the address out, a snapshot follows, and every event before that snapshot is deleted, so nothing older than the snapshot holds the address. The event store gains that collapse here, for the account's aggregates and the invitations' alike, as most aggregates will need it when their author goes; until then a retired invitation keeps its address in its events, which the security review knows.*
10. **Assets: images kept and served.** *Noted 2026-10-23, asked for by the author:* the first files Weaveling keeps that are not text, wanted first for the picture of slice 11 and later for [images in ideas](#after-phase-3-sketch-only). A feature of its own, `features/assets`, after the author's assets in Kicherkrabbe (`C:/repo/kicherkrabbe/backend/features/assets`, and its manager in `frontend/management/.../modules/assets`), which are prior art to read first: an `Asset` aggregate, event-sourced, with its content type, where it is kept and when it was made, and deleted by an event; the bytes behind a `Storage` port, a folder in local mode and on a server whatever the operator chooses, the database first; an image made on upload into variants of fixed widths, there 384, 768, 1536 and 3072 pixels, as WebP and never wider than the original, and served by `GET /api/assets/{id}/content?width=…` as the smallest variant at least that wide, cached by the browser for good since an asset never changes; **references**, which thing uses an asset and its name, an account's picture, an idea, kept by listening to the features that use them, so an asset in use cannot be deleted and can be found by what uses it; and a limit on what one installation keeps, with how much is used. The **assets manager** in the client lists every asset one may see, as a grid of pictures, found by what uses them and by kind, sorted by when or how large, to upload, look at and delete. *To decide when it starts:* whose an asset is, an account's or a project's, and so who may see it through the `Authorizer`: Kicherkrabbe lets anyone fetch content, which here would leave a book's pictures readable by anyone with the address; the image crate to resize with in Rust, `image` and a WebP encoder, pure Rust if it can be, so it builds for the [demo in the browser](#after-phase-3-sketch-only) too; and whether the variants are made at upload or on first asking, as Kicherkrabbe does both. Likely three or four reviews by layer, as slice 2 was.
11. **A picture for each account** — *noted 2026-10-22, moved here 2026-10-23:* shown in the masthead, in the round account button where the account's initial is now, and wherever members are listed; uploaded as an asset of slice 10, cropped to a square in the browser first; the initial stays where there is none.
12. **Who is writing where.** *Noted 2026-10-23, asked for by the author:* in the writing view, a scene someone else has open shows them in its margin, their picture and, on hovering it, their name, so two authors see at a glance that they are in the same scene; several in one scene stand side by side. It needs presence, who is in which scene now, told to the others as it changes and gone when they leave or their connection drops: Yjs carries this as *awareness* beside a document's updates, and the client's `sync.js` already sends and takes awareness messages on the scenes' socket, so the account's id, name and picture may ride there, the server relaying them to the scene's other editors. Later perhaps more of the same: where in the scene they are, as a coloured cursor with their name, and who is in a book at all, on the projects list. Comes after 11, since a picture is what makes it readable; with only initials it would work as well, but is less worth it.
13. **A security review** of accounts, signing up, signing in, sessions and the second factors, once they stand, before the milestone counts as done: *asked for 2026-10-22, so no grave error slips through.* Worth at least: the steps of a reset, not one transaction but ordered on purpose, as told at 2d-2; likewise becoming the operator, the code taken, then the event, then the appointing, as told at 3a-3; the flows against the OWASP authentication and session cheat sheets, enumeration, by what is answered and by how long it takes, brute force and throttling, races between steps that are not one transaction (among them the address claimed and its account not begun, 2f), what a leaked invitation link gives (an account at its address and, since 3d-4c-1, a session at once; links are used once, hold seven days and can be withdrawn), who may sign up with the operator code while nobody is an operator (3d-6a; whoever reads the log, any number of them until one takes the code), addresses kept in old invitations' events (3d-5b forgets the list's, not the events'; collapsed with account deletion in slice 9), what the logs and the events hold, the cookie's attributes and CSRF, and the codes', keys' and hashes' parameters.

**Where it stands, 2026-10-25:**

- **Done:** slices **1** (who acts), **2** (signing up and in, sessions, the password reset, the address), **3** (the operator, invitations to the installation); of slice **4**, **4a**–**4e** (members, their read model, founding, the `Authorizer`, each account's projects list and its channel, `/members/live`) and of **4f** **4f-1** projects, **4f-2** ideas and appearances, with the context map in `eventsourcing` it needed, and **4f-3** boards and outlines.
- **Done beside it:** tombstones laid with when, and forgotten 31 days after by a chore in each of the five features that keep them, in batches; refused changes past 30 days no longer tried again; the tombstone tables named as such.
- **4f done** with 4f-4, scenes, statistics and the editing socket `/sync`: every route and socket asks on entry. *4f-5, sockets closed when a member is removed or loses the right, moved into slice 5, the user agreeing 2026-10-25:* nobody can leave, be removed or change role yet, `MembersService` holding `found` alone, so it could be tested only by publishing messages by hand; the facade below moves following into each core, where closing belongs, so built now it would be built twice; and the members' channel and `catalogue-members` hear the same message, so a socket asking again on hearing `members.removed` may be answered by a list not yet told, a race to settle once, where it lands.
- **Done before slice 5:** **one core a feature.** *Asked by the user 2026-10-25:* each feature's core gets one struct, `ScenesCore`, `ProjectsCore` and so on, the sole entry to it, a facade: every write, read, follow, consequence of a message and chore goes through its methods, which ask the `Authorizer`, validate and hand on to the services and ports behind it, so reading the struct tells everything the feature does. Today five REST adapters ask the authorizer themselves (appearances, ideas, projects' following, scenes, statistics), since their catalogs and live routes pass the service by; those checks move into the core, and the adapters only translate HTTP, sockets and messages. Live stays out of the core: it offers to follow, asked and answered with what to listen to, and `libraries/live` keeps the socket. The facade delegates rather than gathering the logic into itself. Agreed to finish guarding everything in 4f first, then switch, scenes first as the pilot, since its checks are spread over the service, REST and the socket, then one feature a slice. *Shape agreed the same day: `SceneService` stays behind the core, a bigger feature may keep several services but one core; live stays in the adapter, the core answering only whether one may follow; the restyler is handed to the core's `restyle` by its listener, since `LiveScenes` is built from the core; one error for the core, `ScenesError`; named `ScenesCore`, plural as the feature. The pilot cut in three: **F1** the core and REST, **F2** the listeners and the chore, **F3** the socket, the restyling and `SceneService` private to the core. F1 done: `SceneServiceError` became `ScenesError` in a file of its own, gaining the catalog's and the tasks' failures; `ScenesCore::new(store, catalog, tasks, authorizer, clock)` hands commands and `read` on to the service, and answers `list`, `restyling_progress` and `may_follow` itself, asking `Read`; the REST adapter holds only the core, its routers taking it alone and their live routes gated by it, so it no longer depends on `authorizing`. The core is given the telling catalog and tasks, for F2's writes. Until F3 the wiring still builds a second `SceneService` for the listeners and the socket. 1 new case, a live route failing when the authorizer is down, after its mutant survived; 7 mutants by hand, all caught.* *Changed in review the same day, the user asking that every permission be asked in the core rather than the service: `SceneService` lost the `Authorizer` and every agent, `new(store, clock)`, doing only the work; `ScenesCore` opens a scene and asks before handing on, through `authorized_to_write`, `authorized_to_read` and `authorized`, named so in review since `written_by` did not say it asked, and answers `access` for the socket, from the scene's project. Since they were the only callers asking outside REST, the socket's `LiveScenes` now holds the core, through `open`, `apply` and the announcements it hands on unasked, and the sweep went into the core as `sweep(project, after, at_most)`, deleting a batch as `Agent::System` and passing over a scene gone meanwhile, its listener only carrying on. Cataloguing, unlinking and restyling still hold a `SceneService` of their own until F2. Tests building `LiveScenes` build the core; the service's own tests lost their agents. 2 new cases, a scene gone before the sweep reaches it and a store failing mid-sweep, after both their mutants survived; 19 mutants by hand on the core's checks, all caught.* *F2 done the same day: the cataloguing, unlinking and restyling listeners and the tombstone chore hold the core. Cataloguing calls `catalogue_created`, `catalogue_retitled`, `catalogue_opening` and `catalogue_deleted`, the core reading the ids and the title, so the validation moved with it; unlinking `unlink_everywhere`; the chore `forget_tombstones_laid_before`. Restyling went into the core as `restyle(restyling, at_most, restyler)`, claiming or beginning the task, counting the book, restyling a batch, passing over a scene deleted meanwhile and telling the progress, and answering the scene to carry on after, if any; `QuotationRestyling`, what a message asks for, moved into the core with it, and the listener only reads the message and carries on. The wiring builds no `SceneService` any more; only the service's own tests and one sweep test still do, for F3. Interrupted once by the disk filling: `target/` had grown to some 960 GB of incremental caches and stale builds, cleaned by the user, and my runs now go with `CARGO_INCREMENTAL=0`. 3 new cases after their mutants survived, none of them made by the move: a restyler failing otherwise than by a scene gone fails the batch; a catalog out of reach fails every kind of cataloguing, so the message is tried again; a title heard again at the version the catalog holds changes nothing, which pins the version handed on. 15 mutants by hand, all caught.* *F3 done the same day, and with it the pilot: `SceneService` is no longer exported, so nothing outside the core can reach it; its tests, `scene_service.rs`, became the core's, `scenes_core.rs`, going through `ScenesCore` as anyone would, and the sweep's tests look into their own store rather than through a second service. No mutants: nothing of behaviour changed, only who may reach it. Browser runs after F2: 728 passed, and with accounts 724 and 4 skipped, no flakes. The workspace: 2629 passed.* *Ideas done by hand the same day, the first event-sourced core, to settle the shape before agents take the rest: `IdeasCore::new(store, catalog, authorizer, clock)` asks every permission; `IdeaService` lost the `Authorizer` and the catalog, keeping the agent only to record who acted, takes parsed ids, and is no longer exported. The core answers what someone does, `list`, `capture`, `read`, `retitle`, `discard` and `may_follow`, and what the feature does itself, `catalogue`, `sweep` as `Agent::System` passing over an idea discarded meanwhile, and `project_of` for the broadcast. The REST adapter no longer reads an idea unasked after writing it: it reads through `read`, as its caller. The cataloguing and broadcasting listeners no longer build an `EventSourcingService` of their own, so the event store is held by the core alone. One change in behaviour: retitling read the title before asking, so a stranger sending a title too long was told so; now the core asks first, and a stranger is told the idea is not there. `idea_service.rs` became `ideas_core.rs`. 5 new cases after their mutants survived, none made by the move: the sweep's tests let only the system through, so a sweep acting as anyone else fails them; an idea discarded before the sweep reaches it is passed over; an authorizer out of reach fails the sweep and the live route; a change naming no idea is passed over by the broadcast rather than retried. 15 mutants by hand, all caught.* *The other eight in one slice, 2026-10-26, as the user asked, to be reviewed at once and the browser suites run once at the end; done by hand rather than by agents, since the builds and the browser runs would not have run in parallel and the judgement calls were the work. Each feature now has `<Things>Core` as its one way in and `<Things>Error` for its errors, its service unexported, every adapter, listener and chore through the core, and every permission asked there. Shared by all: **a write answers with what it wrote** (ideas amended to match: `capture` and `retitle` answer with the idea), so no REST route reads back unasked; **the system's own work is done and recorded as `Agent::System`**, and new cases now pin that for every consequence; and the old service tests became the core's. **Projects:** `start` asks nothing and answers with the project as started, since its starter owns it only once the members have heard, so a checked read would 404; renaming and describing ask `Manage` before the name or details are judged, as ideas does; the list's sieve and the one project's gate ask the core. **Appearances**, catalog-only: the REST `Readers` went into the core, which also tells the sieve apart from the gate, an idea of a project not known yet followed but not told. **Boards and outline:** the letting-go logic in their messaging crates moved into the core (`index_pins`, `index_attachments`, `let_go_everywhere`), `discard` is no longer public, the sweep alone discards, and outline's `Changed` and `Added` carry the outline as written. **Statistics**, catalog-only, as appearances. **Members:** `MembersCore` founds as the system, catalogues and decides who hears a change on the members' channel; `MembersAuthorizer` is unchanged; its error is `MembersCoreError`, since `MembersError` is the aggregate's, and the file `members_core.rs`, since `core.rs` would shadow the `core` crate. **Invitations:** the operator check moved into the core; taking up and declining pass over an invitation already settled there rather than in the listeners; the chore calls `retire_due`. **Accounts:** `AccountsCore` hands most calls on as they are, signing up and in being identity, not permission; locking, unlocking, listing, appointing and dismissing ask whether the agent is an operator, and a non-operator sending a lock reason too long is now told they are no operator; cataloguing moved into the core; the five tests reading an account read through their own store, so the core has no unasked `get`; `AssembledAccounts::core` and `accounts_wiring::core` replace `service`. ARCHITECTURE's feature anatomy and CONVENTIONS' naming say so, and every feature's README. New cases after surviving mutants, none made by the move: a reader opens a project; the projects' list and an appearance followed end when the authorizer is down; the statistics' live route answers 500 then; boards and outline run their sweep and tidying tests with only the system and the author let through, record the sweep and letting go as the system's, and fail letting go when the authorizer is down; the founding, a decline and a retirement are recorded as the system's. Mutants by hand: projects 13, appearances 13, boards 19, outline 19, statistics 7, members 7, invitations 11, accounts 7, all caught. The workspace: 2643 passed, clippy clean; `target/` at 26 GB with incremental off.*
- **Next:** slice **5**, inviting into a project, with 4f-5, the designs drafted, B as the base with A's dialog as a shortcut; then writes that hold together, from the transaction audit; **6** the trash; **7** TOTP; **8** FIDO2 keys; **9** deleting an account; **10** assets; **11** a picture for each account; **12** who is writing where; **13** the security review. [M17a](#milestone-17a--hosted-for-many-ten-thousand-at-once), hosted for many, follows the milestone.

**Done when:** on a server, two people sign up, one starts a book and invites the other as an author, both write in it while a third account cannot see it exists; local mode looks as it does today; and the security review has found nothing grave left open.

### Milestone 17b — One socket for everything live

*Proposed by the user 2026-10-29, scheduled between slice 5's 5d and 5e.* Each feature serves a socket of its own today, `/projects/live`, `/members/live`, `/boards/live`, `/outlines/live`, `/invitations/mine/live` and the rest, each with its route, its upgrade, its streaming and its reconnecting in the client; a tab holds three to five of them. Instead, **one socket, `/api/live`**, signed in, over which the client subscribes to what it is looking at and unsubscribes when it looks away.

**Topics, not routing keys.** A subscription names a topic and what it is about, `{ "subscribe": "board", "board": "…", "since": 42 }`, `{ "subscribe": "invitations", "mine": true }`, `{ "subscribe": "members", "project": "…" }`. *Settled 2026-10-30, the user asking:* **one topic a feature, its scope a parameter**, not a topic for each view: `invitations` is the account's own (`mine`, by its address), a project's (`project`, for its owners) or the instance's (no scope, for operators), each authorized by its own rule; `members` a project's or one's own (the list of projects) alike. As a RabbitMQ topic exchange would be a feature's. The broker's routing keys say *what kind* of thing happened (`members.joined`); a view wants *which* thing it is looking at, and the keys are the messaging's own contract, not something the browser should be bound to. **No wildcards:** a subscription is authorized once, when it is made, and again only when access changes, not frame by frame. (For RabbitMQ a topic exchange per feature is likely the same shape, noted for when it comes.)

**Authorization by the kind of scope, not by feature.** Project-scoped messages carry their project in the `AggregateContext` since 4f-2, so one rule, *may read this project*, covers boards, outlines, scenes, ideas, statistics and a project's members. Besides it: the account's own (invitations to its address), the operator's (the instance's invitations), and lists whose frames each name another project ("my projects"), the one place a frame is still sifted on its own. The gates of today, `IdeasGate`, `ProjectsGate`, `ScenesGate`, `StatisticsGate`, `BoardsGate`, `OutlinesGate`, map onto these almost one to one.

**What the server answers, per subscription:** frames, carrying the version as now; `resync` from a version when the subscription fell behind, where a feature's socket today is closed and opened again, so one slow subscription never stalls the others; and `ended` with why, `access-lost`, in place of closing the whole socket with 4403. When the session ends, the one socket closes (5f).

**What it does not change:** what reaches the browser goes through the same contract DTOs and the same redaction as now, nothing raw from the broker; the scenes' editing socket `/sync` stays as it is, as it carries a CRDT both ways rather than news one way, so a tab holds at most two sockets; and [M17a](#milestone-17a--hosted-for-many-ten-thousand-at-once)'s question of more than one process is the same with one socket as with five.

**Build, roughly:** in `libraries/live`, the socket, its subscribe and unsubscribe, a `Topic` port each feature registers (how it is authorized, which broadcast it hears, how a frame is sifted) and the per-subscription catch-up; each feature's live route becomes a topic, one at a time, the old route kept until its view has moved; in the client, `live.js` and `reconnecting.js` become one connection that resubscribes everything after reconnecting, and `Following` subscribes through it. About a week.

*M17b-1 done 2026-10-30, the library:* `live_socket` serves `/live`; the client asks `AskedDTO`, `{ "do": "subscribe", "id": …, "topic": …, "about": { … } }` or `{ "do": "unsubscribe", "id": … }`, and is told `ToldDTO`, a `frame`, a `notice` (`access-changed`) or `ended` with why (`unknown-topic`, `not-there`, `failed`, `access-lost`, `behind`), each under its id. A feature hands over a `Topic`, its name and `subscribe`, answering a `Subscribed` (the `Hearing`, a version to tell first, an `Admission`, a `Sieve`, as its own route takes now) or `Refused`. Each subscription is a task of its own, so one ending ends alone; subscribing again under an id replaces it; closing the socket ends them all. A subscription falling behind ends `behind` and the client subscribes again, where a feature's socket today is closed and opened again. 9 cases in `live`; 11 mutants, all caught, one more case after one survived (closing the socket leaving subscriptions hearing). *M17b-2 done, the server's half:* a feature hands its topics over, `Wired::followed_live`, `Assembled::topics`, and the server serves them all on `/api/live`, behind signing in where there are accounts, as the local agent where there are none. `invitations` with `mine` (`InvitationsTopic`, by the account's address; `address_of` takes the agent now) and `members` with `mine` (`MembersTopic`, sifted to whom a change names, as `/members/live`) are the first; any other scope is not there yet. The client still uses the feature routes, which stay until it moves (M17b-3). The instance's invitations will want the operator, which an `Agent` does not tell: the topic then takes what acting tells, decided when it comes. 3 feature cases, 2 on the server (one socket telling a project's founding and an invitation, each under its own id; `/api/live` behind signing in); 8 mutants, all caught, one after its case was made to ask with an account the invitations know. Next, M17b-3: the client's one connection, and the bell and the list of projects on it. **Done when:** a tab with the board, the projects list and the bell open holds one socket beside `/sync`; losing access to a project ends that project's subscriptions and nothing else; a subscription that falls behind catches up without the others noticing; and no feature serves a live route of its own.

### Milestone 17a — Hosted for many: ten thousand at once

*Noted 2026-10-25, asked how many sockets one server holds:* an instance hosted for its own author or a writing group is far from any limit; Weaveling hosted as a platform, for any number of authors and their books, is planned for **10,000 people working at once**. Not needed while M17 is built; planned for straight after it, and **measured before anything is changed**.

1. **Measure first.** A load test of the server as it stands: N followers on the list of projects and on open projects, M changes a second, the time a change takes to reach them, the memory a socket holds, the queries a second. Every step below is judged by it, and any that the numbers do not ask for is left.
2. **More than one process.** The step that is not tuning but a decision: the `Broadcaster`, the `InProcessDispatcher` and the live scenes (`yrs`) live in the memory of one process, so a change made in one is told only to those following there, and two people editing one scene must reach the same process. Ten thousand at once likely means several processes, so either a change is passed between them (PostgreSQL `LISTEN`/`NOTIFY`, or a broker) and a scene's editing is routed to one process by its id, or one process is shown to carry the load. To settle before hosting begins, not after.
*Noted 2026-10-30, the user asking whether 64 waiting frames a socket is too few for 1000 people:* it is per socket, so a slow reader holds up only itself; the number that counts with many at once is the `Broadcaster`'s backlog, 256 frames a feature shared by everyone hearing it, which one hearer falls behind on if more are told across all projects than it reads meanwhile. To size by measuring, or to become a channel per topic. 3. **The list's sieve without a query a frame.** `/projects/live` asks `may(Read)` once a frame a follower; a socket keeps what its follower may read instead, refreshed by `/members/live`, which tells them exactly when it changes.
4. **The broadcaster by topic.** One `broadcast` channel a feature wakes every follower of the feature for every frame, each checking the topic; a channel a topic wakes only those it concerns.
5. **One socket a tab.** An open project follows five to eight channels; one socket carrying them all, by topic, cuts the connections to a fifth.
6. **What the host must give.** File descriptors well above the usual 1,024 a process, the connection pools sized to the load measured, and what a process does when it is asked to hold more than it can.

**Done when:** a load test of 10,000 followers, across more than one process if step 2 decided so, keeps a change reaching everyone it concerns within a second, and the numbers and the decisions are written here.

### Milestone 13a — Moving a project between machines

**Goal:** a project leaves one installation as a file and arrives in another whole.

**Delayed 2026-10-07.** Not essential while local mode already keeps the work in files an author owns; the writing view comes first, because it is the heart of the app and should be found early.

**Build:** export a project — the projects rows, every aggregate's event stream with metadata, each scene's CRDT state via `Scene::everything()` and its links — and import it. **Catalogs are not exported**, deliberately: import replays each stream in the file through its projector, so a read model is rebuilt rather than restored, and the file's contents are the enumeration the event store cannot give. That makes import the first wholesale rebuild of a projection, which the [open TODO on enumeration](./TODO.md) notes is otherwise missing.

**Done when:** a project exported from one instance and imported into an empty one has its ideas, board, outline and scenes exactly as they were — with every catalog rebuilt by replay.

---

### Milestone 16 — A history of the work, and goals for it

**Moved here 2026-10-19 from the writing view's later ideas; not urgent.** How many words were written and how much time went into writing and planning, day by day, so an author can see their work add up. On top of that, a goal to aim for — words for the day, or for this sitting — that the status note can count towards.

### Later — polish and what can wait

*Gathered 2026-10-19* from the milestones that are done, so that none of it is forgotten and none of it is in the way. Each is taken up when it starts to hurt, or when the milestone it waits on arrives.

**Code splitting, when the bundle hurts.** *Noted 2026-10-22.* As features grow, the client is one WebAssembly file holding every view. Measured that day: 124 MB in a debug build, which a phone took twenty seconds to load over the network, and 3.9 MB in a release build, 1.3 MB compressed, for the writing view, the board, the outline, the projects and five languages. Taken in this order, each only once the one before is not enough:
1. **What costs nothing in the code:** the release profile sized for the web, `opt-level = "z"`, `lto` and `wasm-opt`, and the files served compressed, gzip or brotli, which the development server does not do; usually another 30–50% off.
2. **The editor only where it is written in:** the ProseMirror bundle is plain JavaScript that only the writing view needs, and a dynamic `import()` loads it when that view opens, without a change to the Rust.
3. **Views loaded as they are opened:** Leptos 0.9, which this client uses, splits a function marked `#[lazy]` into a chunk of its own, built on `wasm_split`, and `leptos_router`'s `LazyRoute` makes a whole view such a chunk, with `preload` to fetch it ahead, on hovering its link. The split is a step after compiling, which `cargo-leptos --split` runs; Trunk, which builds this client, is not known to, so the first thing to settle is whether Trunk can, by a hook, or the build moves to cargo-leptos.
**When:** once the compressed release bundle passes 2 MB, measured as above. Meanwhile each view keeps one entry, `app/board.rs`, `app/outline.rs` and their kind, with little reaching between views, so making one lazy later stays a change in one place.

**A library of its own for tombstones.** *Noted 2026-10-25, the user's idea:* the `Tombstones` port, `ForgetOldTombstones`, `TOMBSTONES_KEPT_FOR` and `TOMBSTONES_EACH_BATCH` live in `libraries/chores`, which otherwise only runs chores; a `libraries/tombstones` would own them, and perhaps the case every feature's wiring copies, that no more than a batch is forgotten at once, as a suite to run against each feature's tombstones. **When:** whenever a slice touches tombstones again, or before a fifth feature lays them.

**What the system did, and who caused it.** *Noted 2026-10-25, asked whether a project's sweep should act as the owner who deleted it:* no, the system acts as `Agent::System`, and stays so. Whether the owner may delete was asked once, of the command; the sweep and the other consequences, an idea unpinned, detached and unlinked once discarded, carry that decision out rather than make it again, and asking again as the owner would race the members swept by the same message and could leave a sweep half done. What is wanted is the cause: an event the system records on someone's behalf names what caused it, the deletion or the discard and so its agent, beside `Agent::System`, so a history can say "removed when Ada deleted the project". Today it can be traced only by hand, through the conversation a sweep's batches stay in back to the event that opened it, whose envelope names who acted. **When:** with the first history an author or an operator reads, at the latest with the security review of [M17](#milestone-17--accounts-members-and-roles).

**From [ideas and passages](#milestone-12--ideas-and-passages-):**

**Scenes and ideas reorderable within their group, the way sections are.** The model already allows it — `Attach` takes an `after` and detaches first, so attaching to the same section behind a different neighbour *is* a reorder, with no new command or event. What's missing is purely the client: a grip on a leaf, landing between leaves, and keyboard moves (`Alt+↑/↓`) to match the sections. **Within its group, never across** — a section shows its scenes and its ideas as two groups, and a note belongs to its section rather than to a place between two scenes, so an idea is reordered among ideas and a scene among scenes.

**The scene page as two columns.** Writing comes first and must be possible without distraction, so the scene’s metadata — its title, the ideas it draws on, whatever joins them — moves out from under the editor into a **collapsible sidebar** on the right. Step 6 puts it below the editor as a stopgap; this is where it ends up when the editor is redesigned.

**A paged, searched link dialog.** The dialog on the scene page loads every idea in the project and filters in the browser — fine for a book, not for 10k+ ideas. When that bites: a server-side route that searches by title and pages with a cursor, the dialog asking it as the author types. The cursor half already exists — the ideas catalog’s `in_project_after` is what the project sweep pages with; searching by title is the new part, and wants an index of its own (prefix or trigram) rather than a scan.

**With authorization: messages name their project, and appearances store it.** Every published message carries an aggregate's `{ id, kind, version }` and nothing about which project it belongs to, so a projection cannot scope by project without a lookup of its own. Authorization will force the change anyway — once requests are scoped to projects, so is everything announced about them — and the right shape is the envelope, once, in `eventpublishing`, rather than each feature adding it to its payloads. Then [appearances](./features/appearances) gains a `project_id` column: project deletion becomes one `DELETE` on `project.deleted` instead of riding the idea sweep, and reads are scoped to a project, so an id from another project answers nothing. Not before: correctness does not need it today, and a lookup table kept only for this — or a project on some rows and not others — is worse than waiting.

**Controls for the tree as a whole** — collapse everything, expand everything, and whatever else turns out to be worth one click rather than a click per section. Client-only by construction: folding is a `RwSignal` in the view, and it has to stay there — expansion state in the event stream is named in [ARCHITECTURE.md](./ARCHITECTURE.md#the-outline-arranges-sections-not-scenes) as the structural failure of tree-as-model. Whether the fold should survive a reload is a separate question, and the answer there is browser storage, never the aggregate.

**From [local mode](#milestone-13--local-mode-):**

**Refused messages scoped to the project.** Step 7 shows every refused message, from every project, to whoever asks. Better is the open project's only, and once there is authorization it is a must. It waits on [messages naming their project](#milestone-12--ideas-and-passages-): then a dead letter can carry its project, and the route answers per project.

**Deleting a dead letter, once a rebuild can heal it.** Acknowledged refusals pile up. Deleting one is safe only when the projection it missed can be rebuilt from events ([TODO](./TODO.md)) — then it becomes an ops-level clean-up rather than something an author decides.

**With a second service: refusals gathered across services.** Deliveries are one per service today. A second deployable brings a broker, dead letters move to a dead-letter queue per listener queue, and `/api/service/refusals` needs one place that collects them — each service draining its own into a table, or one small service consuming them all. The `RefusalDTO` contract is what keeps the client out of that.

**With a broker: sweeps that cannot fork.** *Noted 2026-10-29, asked whether a redelivered project deletion could make two processes sweep at once:* yes. A sweep that publishes its continuation and dies before the ack is handed over again, and publishes a second continuation with a fresh id, so two chains run side by side. Each discard is idempotent, so nothing goes wrong, but the work doubles. Exactly-once is not on offer from a broker; at-least-once with idempotent effects is, and that is already the case. Owed before the broker: a continuation's id derived from `(listener name, project, after)`, so a forked chain publishes an id the inbox has already seen. See [TODO](./TODO.md).

**Transactions per feature: a `transacting` library.** *Noted 2026-10-29, from the transaction audit; scheduled the same day into [M17, after slice 5](#milestone-17--accounts-members-and-roles).* A feature's writes are spread over several ports — event store, catalog, secrets, registry — and today a write that needs two of them is ordered so a crash between them heals, which the audit found was not always done carefully: `take_up` uses up the invitation ticket before `TakeUpInvitation` is appended, so a failure in between leaves the ticket gone and the invitation open for good. The way out is not to forbid that ordering harder but to make it unnecessary: **every port of one feature lives in that feature's own database**, so one transaction across them is possible, only nothing lets the adapters share it.

**The shape is [decided in ARCHITECTURE](./ARCHITECTURE.md#a-transaction-in-the-port-signature--decided), 2026-10-29:** a `Transaction` that names no database, begun by core as a rule and passed in by an inbound adapter when a use case warrants it; `&mut Transaction` required on every driven write method, optional on a core method through `join`, so no method is doubled per caller; committed explicitly and rolled back when dropped; downcast by the adapter, checked at runtime; in memory, the feature's one lock and an undo per write. Built as:

1. **The library.** `Transaction`, `Transactions`, `Joined`; the PostgreSQL, SQLite and in-memory backends; the warning for a transaction dropped after writing; a conformance suite proving a dropped transaction leaves nothing.
2. **The event store joins.** `append` takes the transaction and enqueues its outbox rows in it; `carry_out` takes one, `execute` keeps opening its own; the snapshot moves after the commit, best-effort.
3. **The inbox, in the same step.** A listener whose effects must not happen twice begins the transaction, records `(listener, message id)` in the feature's own database within it, and hands it to core. Which listeners need it, and whether `deliveries` gains `UNIQUE (listener, message_id)` too, settled then.
