4. **The first users**, below.

First users, in order: accounts' invitation ticket together with the account's append (taking up, signing up by invitation, declining); a catalog's `forget` with its tombstone and `remember` with its tombstone check, already one transaction inside one store and a model for the rest; boards' and outlines' registry claim with the `Start` append. Until it exists, the rule for a write across two ports is that **the append is the commit point**: what comes before it may be taken again by the same actor, what comes after it is driven by the event or idempotent.

**Version guards and tombstones on the catalog upserts.** The ideas, projects, boards and outline catalogs re-read the latest state, so a late message cannot roll them back — as long as one listener's queue is never handled twice at once. A `WHERE version < excluded.version` on the upsert removes that assumption for a row that is still there; ideas and projects already store a version, boards and outlines would need one. *Noted 2026-10-29, from the transaction audit, and scheduled into [M17, after slice 5](#milestone-17--accounts-members-and-roles):* a version guard alone is not enough, because `forget` deletes the row and the guard has nothing left to compare against. Run A reads v3, run B reads v4 (gone) and forgets, A remembers v3, and the gone thing is back for good, since nothing follows a deletion. **Every catalog that forgets lays a tombstone in the same transaction, and `remember` refuses an id that has one** — the shape appearances and the boards' piece index already use. The invitations catalog has the same gap, and there it is more than a ghost row: `retire_due` takes `LIMIT 100 ORDER BY retire_after`, a resurrected retired invitation is picked again every hour and never leaves, and a hundred of them stall retiring altogether. More urgent than it reads: it turns from latent to live with the first broker, a prefetch above one, or a second process.

**The alarm learns more.** The dialog shows dead letters only. Next to them belong the client's own failures — the requests the views catch as `ApiError`, collected for the session. Server logs in the browser would be a different, much bigger thing. And asking every 5 seconds is a start: once there is a live channel for it, the server can say when something is refused instead.

**Revisit the wiring again.** Step 0b is good enough to build local mode on, not where it should end. Candidates noticed on the way: every feature now spells its wiring three times over — `Ports`, the free `wire` and `service` its tests use, and the `Feature` impl that mostly delegates to them; each backend adds a `#[cfg]` pair of trait methods with defaults, so the impls grow per backend; and `Storage` is an enum the trait has to keep in step with. Worth a fresh look once SQLite has shown what a third backend really costs. *Partly done by [M13d](#milestone-13d--simplify-)'s third finding: each feature builds its ports in `Feature` from the `Context`, and the `Ports` constructors are gone. Still as described: the free `wire` and `service` beside the `Feature` impl, a `#[cfg]` pair of methods per backend, and the `Storage` enum.*

**Two executables, one per audience.** What M13 ends with is one binary that picks its backend from the environment, which is right for us starting it from a terminal and wrong for an author. The target is two executables over the same library:

- **A local one for authors** — SQLite, and local mode without being told: when nothing is configured, the work goes to the platform's data directory (`%APPDATA%\Weaveling`, `~/Library/Application Support/Weaveling`, `~/.local/share/weaveling`). A `--data <directory>` flag moves it, falling back to `WEAVELING_DATA` — `clap` with its `env` support gives both from one declaration, and `--help` makes it discoverable.
- **A server one** — PostgreSQL, configured by `WEAVELING_DATABASE_URL` as now, since containers and hosting platforms configure by environment and it keeps a connection string out of the process list.

In memory stays what development and the tests run on. `Backend::chosen` survives as the shared core; each executable only decides its default and where its settings come from. This revisits *one binary, the backend chosen at runtime* from the top of this milestone: the runtime choice stays, but the binary each audience runs is narrowed to what that audience needs.

**From [the writing view](#milestone-13c--the-writing-view-):**

**Work in progress in the status bar, once there is more than one kind.** *Noted 2026-10-19:* restyling a book's quotation marks is the only long-running work an author starts and watches, and it is over in seconds. Once there is a second, export or import of a project ([M13a](#milestone-13a--moving-a-project-between-machines)) the likely one, the status bar gets one place for running work, showing its name and how far it has come, fed by every kind without knowing them. The status bar learns of work it did not start itself, after a reload or from another tab, by asking when the project opens and again now and then while nothing runs. On the server each feature keeps its own progress, as the scenes feature keeps `quotation_restyling_tasks`; should asking every kind on its own add up, they share one small contract, the kind, a name, how many are done of how many and whether it is finished, each still behind its own feature's route rather than in a registry of tasks no feature owns.

**The contents rail on a big book.** *Noted 2026-10-19, from the 10,000-scene stress book:* the "Contents" rail feels sluggish there, and once a scene has been chosen from it the writing view goes to that scene as it should, but the rail itself stops working. Not measured yet. A rail of 10,000 scenes in 300 chapters wants the same care slice 6 gave the flow, measured first, likely drawing only the entries in view and not rebuilding every entry when one changes.

**Changes applied as they come, rather than reloaded.** *Noted 2026-10-19, with [M10b](#milestone-10b--the-outlines-live-channel-):* the outline's live channel already sends each change beside its version, and the client only reloads. Reloading a whole outline per change costs a few hundred kilobytes and a recomputation of everything drawn from it on a book of 10,000 scenes. Decide then whether the client applies a change that follows directly on the version it holds, reloading only on a gap, or whether the server sends the changed sections it already answers its own commands with, which the client knows how to take. *Decided 2026-10-20: the client takes in an event that follows directly on the version it holds and opens again only on a gap, an event it does not know or one that does not fit; it is part of [M10c](#milestone-10c--one-way-of-pushing), slice 2, for the board, the outline and the writing view's structure, and the rule for every feature after.*

**Keys typed right after Tab in the outline can be lost.** *Noted 2026-10-19, from a test that failed once in a full run:* Tab in a section's title saves it and asks the server to nest the section; when the answer comes, the row is drawn again in its new place, and whatever was typed in the moment between goes with the old row. Under load that moment is long enough to type in. Worth keeping the field, or what was typed into it, across that redraw, as the writing view keeps its cursor.

**Attaching to a big outline gets slower.** *Noted 2026-10-19:* seeding the 10,000-scene stress book, writing the prose stayed steady at under a second per 1,000 scenes, but attaching the scenes to the outline went from 15 seconds per 1,000 at the start to 28 seconds per 1,000 at the end, every attachment costing more the more the outline already holds. An author attaches one scene at a time, so nobody feels it yet; worth a look before importing a whole book ([M13a](#milestone-13a--moving-a-project-between-machines)) attaches thousands at once.

## After Phase 3 (sketch only)

- **Keeping the work safe: backups, a scene's earlier versions, and a bin.** *Noted 2026-10-16.* The largest gap before [an actual book is written in it](#after-phase-3-sketch-only), because that book has to survive everything that follows. Four parts. **Data that is kept**: there is no point yet from which a project is real data, so schemas are still edited in place; that point needs naming, and from it every schema change is a migration and every upgrade makes a backup first. **Backups**: in local mode a book is a few SQLite files that nothing copies; a backup made on a schedule and before every upgrade, kept for a while, and a way back from one. **A scene's history**: Yjs keeps what it needs for snapshots, so an author can look at a scene as it was yesterday and take a paragraph back, or the whole scene. **A bin**: deleting a scene today cannot be undone; a deleted scene, idea or section could wait in a bin for a number of days before it goes for good, which also softens the sweep that deleting a project starts.

- **Find, and replace, across the book.** *Noted 2026-10-16.* Since M13c slice 6 lets far scenes go, the browser's own Ctrl+F only finds what is near the window, so finding a word in a long book stopped working without anyone deciding it should. A "Find in the book" searches every scene's text on the server, lists the hits with their sentence and scene, and lands on each as the walk lands anywhere; replace goes hit by hit or all at once, and edits through each scene's live document, as [renaming a character](#after-phase-3-sketch-only) would. Plain matching over stored text is enough for one book; search projections over Tantivy, listed below, come when it is not.

- **Bringing in a manuscript that already exists.** *Noted 2026-10-16.* Import today only means moving a project between machines ([M13a](#milestone-13a--moving-a-project-between-machines)). An author who arrives with half a book in Word, Markdown or Scrivener has no way in, and so no realistic way to try Weaveling. A first import takes .docx and Markdown: headings become sections, the text between them scenes, a scene break inside a chapter a new scene, emphasis kept. Scrivener's projects are a folder of RTF and an XML binder, and can come after.

- **Installing and running local mode.** *Noted 2026-10-16.* Today local mode is `trunk serve` beside `cargo run`, which no author will do. What an author gets is not decided: one binary that serves the client as well as the API (the missing `ServeDir` in TODO.md), started like any program and opening the browser, or a desktop wrapper such as Tauri around the same thing. With it come updates, how an author learns of one and what it does to their data, which is why it waits on keeping the work safe.

- **Accounts, tenancy and auth** — *now [M17](#milestone-17--accounts-members-and-roles); still open here: external logins, licences limiting who starts projects.* Looming. It changes port shapes (`list(owner)`, not `list()`) and puts auth on the sync socket. Not a filter to bolt on. *Noted 2026-10-21, with [M10c](#milestone-10c--one-way-of-pushing) 5a:* the live channels need the same. `/api/projects/live` tells every project's change to whoever follows it, since the list of projects is everyone's today; once there are accounts it must tell each follower only the projects they may see, which means the broadcast knows a project's readers, or a follower's hearing filters by them, and the list it asks for on connecting is `list(owner)` too. Every other live socket, by board, outline, project or the refusals, is joined by naming an id, as `/sync` is, and needs the same check on connecting.
- **Finding an account on the page of the instance.** *Noted 2026-10-23, asked for by the author while paging through 120 test accounts:* a search field above the list of accounts, narrowing it by name or address as one types. Done in the catalog, not in the browser, since the list comes a page at a time: `listed_after` gains what to look for, matched without regard to case, `ILIKE` on PostgreSQL and `LIKE` on SQLite, and paged as before. Perhaps also filters, only the locked or only those not confirmed, which the locks kept as `JSONB` were chosen for.
- **Mails in the reader's language.** *Noted 2026-10-23, asked for by the author:* every mail the server sends is English today: the code to confirm an address, the code to set a new password, the note that someone tried to sign up with an address already taken, and an invitation. An account is to have a **language it prefers**, set at signing up to the language the page was read in, which the client already knows, and changed in the account's menu as the page's language is; every mail to an account is written in it. An invitation goes to someone with no account yet, so it is written in the language the operator chose in the invitation's form, defaulting to their own, and the sign-up page it leads to opens in it. The words of the mails then live beside the page's, in the same five catalogues, so the server reads Fluent too, the mails' messages in a catalogue both may read rather than copied into Rust. Weighed then: whether an address without an account, asking for a reset, hears in the browser's language, sent with the request.
- **Maintenance mode.** *Noted 2026-10-23:* for an update that must migrate data, the instance as a whole refuses signing in and writing for a while, and the client says so, rather than every account being locked and unlocked one by one; asked about while locks were being made, and kept apart from them.
- **Notice and takedown, before Weaveling is hosted for others.** *Noted 2026-10-24.* Once a Weaveling instance hosts other people's books, it stores content it did not write, and it is only shielded from liability for that content while it acts on notices, under the host privilege of the Digital Services Act (Art. 6). What it needs, before the first paying author: **a single point of contact** (Art. 11 and 12), an address named in the imprint and in the terms; **a way to report**, a form reachable without an account that asks for where the content is, why it is unlawful, who reports it and that they report in good faith (Art. 16), and confirms its receipt; **a decision in good time**, by a person, kept with the notice; **the author told why** when their content is restricted, with what was restricted, on which ground and how to object (Art. 17). Restricting means locking the project or the scene, not deleting it, so a wrong decision can be undone and the author keeps a way to export their own work. Most of a book is never public, so notices will be rare; it is the sharing and inviting of [M17](#milestone-17--accounts-members-and-roles) that make it reachable by others. Should Weaveling ever publish books to the public, it becomes an online platform, and an internal complaint process (Art. 20) and a yearly report join the list.
- **Export** — Typst → PDF and HTML → EPUB, with a byline of its own, a pen name or several names, since an account's name is not always the name on the cover. Still an **unspiked risk**: embedding Typst means implementing its `World` trait for font and file resolution, plus font licensing. Worth its own rung before it becomes a milestone.
- **Limits, stated and enforced.** *Noted 2026-10-16.* Weaveling should say how big a project can get rather than slow down until it stops. The stress tests measured 10,000 scenes as workable after M13c slice 6 and the outline's cost per change, and nobody writes a book larger than that; past a known limit nothing has been measured, and the cost grows quietly. Proposed, the numbers still to settle: **10,000 scenes per project**, **10,000 ideas on a board**, and a cap on what one outline holds, its attachments and its sections, set from the same measurements.

  **Where a limit lives decides how exact it can be.** A limit inside one aggregate is exact: the board refuses the pin past the limit and the outline the attachment, as a domain rule with its own refusal, the same on every backend. A limit across aggregates is harder, because each scene and each idea is its own: scenes per project would be counted by the scene store at creation, inside the transaction that creates the scene, which SQLite and PostgreSQL do exactly; a catalog cannot answer it, since it trails the write it follows.

  **How it feels at the limit.** A refusal that says what the limit is and why, never a failure that looks like a bug, and a note somewhere quiet well before it, at nine tenths perhaps, so it is never a surprise in the middle of a sentence.

  **Others worth deciding at the same time:** the size of one scene's text, since one pasted novel in a single scene defeats every per-scene measure; ideas per project; sections per outline; links per scene; the length of an idea; projects per installation; and the size of a request and of a sync frame, which bound what a broken or hostile client can send. Titles already have one, 2,048 characters for a scene's.

- **A board that holds ten thousand ideas** — three related changes, and the order matters because the first probably makes the second unnecessary.

  **Cull to the viewport first.** Today every placement becomes an `<article>` with a real `<a>` inside; at ten thousand that is twenty thousand nodes for Leptos to diff and the browser to lay out. But at any zoom where a card is *readable*, only tens of them fit on screen — ten thousand cards at 150px is over a kilometre of board. So rendering only what is in view is both the cheapest fix and very likely the sufficient one.

  **Canvas is the tempting answer and it costs more than it looks.** One element and draw calls would be fast, and it would break two decisions this codebase made deliberately: cards are real anchors, because [`<A>` over a click handler](./ARCHITECTURE.md#frontend--full-stack-rust) is what makes an idea ctrl-clickable and its link copyable; and the E2E suite selects by **role and accessible name**, which a canvas is opaque to. A canvas would take the links, the screen reader and the test strategy with it. The honest split is by zoom: DOM plus culling while cards are readable, and canvas only for a zoomed-out overview where titles cannot be read anyway — and where losing the link therefore costs nothing.

  **Measure first, the way M13c slice 6 did.** Seed a board with 10,000 ideas, most of them pinned, with the same kind of seeding script and harness (`clients/web/stress/`), and measure against running servers: time until the board can be used, DOM nodes, heap, frames while panning and zooming, and the time a drag takes to land. Seeding is likely to measure the server too: the board aggregate holds every placement in one stream, so a pin or a move may cost in proportion to the board, as each change to the outline does to the book.

  **Loading per viewport is the interesting one, and it pressures the frontend join.** Spots are coordinates, so `within=x1,y1,x2,y2` is a natural query — but it cannot be served from the board aggregate, which holds every placement in one stream, so it wants a region-keyed projection. Worse, windowing the placements alone buys nothing: the client resolves titles by fetching *the whole pool*, which at ten thousand ideas is the larger payload. Either both sides get windowed together, or the board's read model carries the title — and that second option denormalises an idea's title into `boards` and needs a listener on `idea.retitled`, which is exactly the [client-side join](./ARCHITECTURE.md#the-board-renders-through-a-frontend-join) we chose instead. So the real question is not "add a spatial query" but **whether the board keeps joining client-side once the pool is too big to fetch whole.**

- **Test readers** — sending a sample out for comment, and getting a review back. The shape the author wants: **one link per reader**, each carrying a token, so a single reader's access can be revoked without touching anyone else's. The link opens a **sample** — a subset of the outline, or the whole book. The reader sees the outline as a table of contents alongside the text, and leaves comments on a **range of text, a paragraph, or a whole section**. When they *commit* their review it becomes visible to the author, who works through it.

  **A project's readers read here too** (the user, 2026-10-28): a member with the role Reader never sees the writing view, only this reading view, so it serves two kinds of reader, the project's members and the test readers with their links.

  **When: the reading view is a strong candidate as soon as the board and the outline are finished and both carry their live channel** — after M10 and [M9b](#milestone-9b--the-boards-live-channel), and after whatever M9b turns out to be for the outline. It sits in this section by topic rather than by date, the same way M9b does.

  It can come that early because **the two halves have very different prerequisites.** Reading your own book needs no capability model at all — no tokens, no accounts, nothing that leaves the machine — so the reading view is buildable long before the auth work that sending a sample out would demand. And it earns its place right after the structure work rather than in spite of it: finishing the outline is the moment the author most needs to see whether the structure actually *reads*, and until then they have only ever seen the book as parts. The token, the sample and the review lifecycle are the second half, and they wait for accounts and tenancy.

  **The strong half of the idea is that this is a reading view, not a separate product.** The alternative — a second micro frontend for outsiders — builds a whole reader that the author never uses. One view with two roles gives the author something they would otherwise have to ask for separately: a way to read their own book end to end and leave comments on it like anyone else. Build the reading view; a test reader is that view with a token and no edit rights.

  **It is the first time anything leaves the author's hands, and that is the real cost.** [Ids are not capabilities](./TODO.md) today — `/sync` lets anyone join a scene by naming one that exists, and the REST layer has the same tenancy gap. A per-reader revocable token *is* a capability model, and it would be this app's first. Smaller than accounts and tenancy, pointing the same way, and probably the thing that forces that milestone rather than the other way round.

  **A sample is frozen — decided.** The author keeps writing while readers read, and a live sample would mean prose shifting under someone mid-chapter and feedback arriving about sentences that no longer exist. So a sample is pinned to a moment: the outline read through to a version, the section and scene titles as they stood, and the prose as it was.

  **Freezing the prose means copying it, and the copy should be a Yjs state rather than exported text.** Two reasons, and the second is the interesting one. First, reconstructing a scene as it *was* is not free — Yjs can restore a prior state only with garbage collection disabled, which costs memory for the lifetime of every document, and [compaction is already owed](./TODO.md) precisely to stop the log growing. Copying the bytes at freeze time is simpler, self-contained, and means deleting a sample deletes its prose with it. Second: because the frozen copy is derived from the same document, it shares item identity with the live one, so **a comment anchored by relative position in the sample still resolves in the manuscript the author is writing now.** Exporting to text would sever that and leave the author matching quotes by hand. Freezing does not remove the mapping problem — it moves it to the one place it can be solved.

  Sending a revised chapter is then a new sample rather than an edit to an old one, which is also how an author would describe it.

  **A review is its own aggregate**, one per reader per sample, with a lifecycle: comments accumulate privately, then commit makes them visible. That draft-then-submit shape is why it cannot just be comments hanging off scenes. Section-level comments anchor to a `SectionId` and are easy; paragraph and range anchoring is where the work is. And a reader needs a name the author recognises, which probably means the author names the link when they make it, rather than the reader introducing themselves.

- **Write an actual book in it, and find out whether any of this helps.** Everything so far rests on a suspicion: that pools of ideas and scenes, a board and an outline are what an author actually needs. Nobody has tested that, and no amount of green suites will. The intended first test is a **children's story** — a girl in the early medieval period, with the turn of a fable or a fairy tale, and possibly a princess. Small enough to finish, structured enough to exercise parts, chapters and scenes.

  **It is too early, and the reason is specific.** The writing view is not finished and there is no reading view at all, so an author could capture and arrange a book without ever being able to read it back — which is exactly the part the test needs to judge. That puts this after [the reading view](#after-phase-3-sketch-only), which is why the two belong together: the reading view is what makes the test possible, and the test is what tells us whether the reading view was the right thing to build.

  **What to watch for when it happens** is not bugs but silences — the moment the author stops using the board, or keeps a list somewhere outside the app, or cannot find a scene they know they wrote. Those say more about whether the model is right than any feature request would.

- **An assistant, reached over MCP rather than built in.** The author wants an LLM for consistency checking (does this dialogue actually belong to this character?), planning and brainstorming. The instinct not to call a paid API from inside Weaveling is right, and for a stronger reason than cost: a local-first tool that needs a vendor key to be useful is not local-first. Exposing Weaveling as an **MCP server** puts the key, the model choice and the bill where they belong — with the author, in whichever assistant they already use.

  **It is not another client; it is a fourth adapter.** That is the whole reason it is cheap. `features/*/adapters/mcp` would sit beside `rest`, `catalog` and `messaging`, over the same services, exactly as [the dependency rules already allow](./ARCHITECTURE.md#dependency-rules) — a manuscript-shaped tool surface (`list_scenes`, `read_scene`, `outline`, `search`) rather than a second application. Calling it a client makes it sound like the work is a UI; it isn't.

  **Drop spelling from the list.** It is the weakest item there by a distance: a dictionary does it better, offline, instantly and for nothing, and spending an LLM round trip per typo is the wrong tool. What an assistant is uniquely good at is the things a dictionary cannot see — a character who was described as left-handed in chapter two, a scene that contradicts a timeline, a thread that was dropped. Those need the whole book, which is the argument for tools over prompts.

  **The tools want to be search-and-slice shaped**, because a novel does not fit in a context window. An agentic client pulling the three scenes it needs beats the app stuffing a manuscript into a prompt — which makes the [search projection](#after-phase-3-sketch-only) considerably more valuable than it looks today. It is the tool the assistant would lean on hardest.

  **Reads first; writes are a separate decision.** "Create scenes for these five ideas" and "reorder the outline" are the useful ones and also the frightening ones. Event sourcing is the right substrate for that — every change is already attributed and reversible — and `Agent` is `Anonymous | System | User(AgentId)` today, so an `Assistant(AgentId)` variant would make the audit log say plainly which changes an author made and which a model suggested. That distinction should exist *before* the first write tool does, not after.

  **`Assistant` is not `System`, and the two must not be collapsed.** `System` is the application acting on its own — a projector, a migration, a scheduled sweep — with no human behind it. An assistant acts *on the author's behalf*, at their prompting, and its changes are ones the author may want to review, attribute or undo as a group. Reusing `System` would make those changes indistinguishable from the app's own bookkeeping, which is exactly the question the log would be asked. The name should say the role rather than the technology, too: `Assistant`, not `Ai` — the log records who acted, and "AI" describes what it is built from.

- **Threads through the text: mentions, dialogue and refactoring.** *Noted 2026-10-16.* Parts of a scene linked to what they are about: a character's name where it is mentioned, each spoken line to the character who speaks it, and later a passage to an idea or anything else in the codex. It turns a book into something that can be read the way code is read, with references that can be followed and renamed safely. It waits for the codex, since most of what is linked lives there.

  **What it is for.** From a character, every scene they are in and every line they speak. And refactoring: rename a character late in the book and every mention changes with it, not only the full name but every form it takes, "Christopher Columbus", "Chris", "Columbus", and in German "Christophers". The codex entry holds those forms; renaming goes form by form ("Christopher → Cristóbal", "Chris → Cris"), with a preview of every occurrence in its sentence, each accepted or skipped, as an IDE renames with a preview.

  **Decided: links live in the scene's own text, as ProseMirror marks.** A mark such as `mention → character` or `spoken_by → character` is stored by y-prosemirror as attributes on the Yjs text, so it moves with every edit, survives two authors typing at once, and travels through the sync the scene already has. Keeping positions outside the text and patching them after every edit was the alternative and it is fragile. Nothing built so far stands in the way, and the one thing to protect is that scene text stays a ProseMirror document in Yjs with its marks kept; the plain-text readings, word counts and openings, ignore marks already.

  **The index is a read model**, like the word counts and the scene catalog. The live scene already tells what it holds after storing an edit; it would tell its mentions and lines the same way, `scene.mentions.changed`, and a codex projection answers "every mention of Columbus" and "every line he speaks" without opening a scene.

  **Linking is suggested, not guessed silently.** Dialogue: a quote followed by "said Chris" is easy to find, and the author confirms the speaker. Mentions: the codex lists the unlinked occurrences of a character's known forms, to link one at a time or all at once, and a known form typed afresh can be offered as a link there and then.

  **Renaming edits the text through each scene's live document on the server**, as ordinary edits, so an author typing in that scene at the same moment merges rather than being overwritten.

  **More refactorings it makes possible**, candidates rather than decisions: **merge** two characters who turn out to be one (the innkeeper is Marta), their mentions and lines moving to one entry and the old names becoming forms of it; **split** one into two, walking the mentions and giving each to one of them; **reassign** a line, or everything a cut character said, to someone else; **delete safely**, a codex entry saying it has 37 mentions and 12 lines and offering to unlink, reassign or keep them; **spelling consistency**, near-variants of a known form that are in no form list ("Colombus"); **extract** a selected passage into a scene of its own with its links. And reading that comes free with the index: where a character first appears, scenes they are in but never speak, each character's share of the dialogue, characters who never meet, and who is in this scene, in the tray. Further out, and needing language tooling: following pronouns to their character, so that a change of gender or pronouns can be made, and checking the facts on a character's sheet, eye colour or age, against the text.

- **Grammar checking.** *Noted 2026-10-16.* Spelling is covered by the browser's own checker, which already runs in every scene, and by the spelling toggle and the book's language in M13c slice 8. Grammar is not covered at all, and today it comes from browser extensions such as LanguageTool's, which work on top of the editor as they are. Three ways to bring it in, none chosen: **LanguageTool behind a server the author chooses**, their own in local mode, which is Java and heavy to bundle, or LanguageTool's service, which sends the text to a third party and so may only ever be something an author turns on, never a default; **Harper**, a grammar checker written in Rust that runs in WASM, offline and fast, but English only, so it serves half the books; or **leaving it to extensions**, which costs nothing and for one author may be the honest answer for a long time. Whatever is chosen, the text only leaves the machine when the author has said it may.

- **Tags for projects.** *Noted 2026-10-16.* Tags or categories on a project, to filter the projects list once it is a shelf of more than a few books. Not important yet; it belongs beside a project's details (M13c slice 8b) when the list grows.

- **A demo that runs wholly in the browser.** *Noted 2026-10-22, asked for by the author, for presentations:* the client and the server both in the page, the work kept in memory, so anyone can try Weaveling with nothing to install and nothing kept past a reload, which the page is to say plainly. Possible because the in-memory store needs no database, the database adapters being features that can be left out, and because an axum `Router` is a tower `Service` that can be called within the process, request in, answer out, with no network between. Planned way: one transport in the client, behind which `http.rs`, the live channels and `/sync` all speak, being `fetch` and WebSockets as now and, in the demo's build, calls straight into the router in the same WASM, the live channels as channels in memory. A service worker answering `/api` was weighed and left, since it cannot stand in for WebSockets, on which the live channels and the scenes' text depend. To adapt: the server's crates for `wasm32`, tokio on one thread without `net` or `signal`, the outbox's relays on the browser's timers rather than tokio's, `getrandom` and `time` with their WASM features, axum without hyper; and a build of the demo, without accounts, simplest for showing. Milestone-sized, in about three slices: the server built for WASM, the transport, the demo's build and page.
- **Notes on a scene.** *Noted 2026-10-22, asked for by the author.* A section "Notes" in the scene's tray, the inspector on the right, holding every note taken on the scene: to jot down quickly, while writing, what was just done or still owed, or to sketch the scene out before writing it. Unlike the notes in the text below, they belong to the scene as a whole, not to a passage. To weigh then: whether a note is plain text or may carry marks and line breaks, whether notes are ordered by when they were taken or by hand, whether one can be ticked off as done, and whether they are counted in the scene's words (they should not be).
- **Richer ideas: images and links.** *Noted 2026-10-23, asked for by the author.* An idea, and so its card on the board, may hold images, a face for a character or a place, a map, a picture that sets the mood, and links, to research or a reference, shown on the card as a small picture or the link's title. Images are assets, kept and served by the assets feature of [M17 slice 10](#milestone-17--accounts-members-and-roles), which an account's picture brings first; an idea then references the asset, and on a server only a project's members may fetch it. Links are text and could come first. *Candidates for more that helps planning, to be weighed:* labels or colours on cards, to sort plot, character and world at a glance; a checklist in an idea, for what a scene still needs; ideas linked to the scenes they became and to each other, "leads to", "contradicts"; a card's cover image filling the card, a mood board of its own; and a timeline, ideas placed by when they happen in the story rather than where they are told.
- **The author's own notes in the text.** *Noted 2026-10-16.* A remark pinned to a passage, "check this date", "too slow", for the author, not for readers. Test readers' reviews are planned; these are not. They could be one more kind of mark, as [threads through the text](#after-phase-3-sketch-only) are, and leave the text untouched for export.

- **One place for the keys.** *Noted 2026-10-16.* Beside menus that show their keys (noted in M13c), a list of every key the app knows, or a command palette, so that what the keyboard can do is found rather than told.

- **An accessibility pass.** *Noted 2026-10-16.* Roles and accessible names are good, because the browser tests find everything by them, but nobody has used the app with a screen reader from start to end, nor checked contrast in both themes, focus order across views, or reduced motion everywhere. One pass with each, and what it finds.

- **Catalogues fetched when wanted, not built in.** *Noted 2026-10-20.* Every language's catalogue is built into the client, and only the language spoken is read, the first time a message is wanted. Fetching a catalogue over the network instead would keep the others out of the bundle, but the first page would wait on it, or show English for a moment, switching languages would wait on the network, and local mode would need it kept offline. At about 1.2 KB a language now, and perhaps 15 to 20 KB once the whole app is worded (some 6 KB compressed), five languages cost some 3% of the bundle; it pays only with many more.

- Then, in some order: the codex, the timeline, threads, search projections over Tantivy.

- **A tutorial, as a small example project.** *Noted 2026-10-17, and for late, once a first version is really usable.* A short project an author can open and walk through, set up as a tutorial: a few guiding steps that say why things are where they are and how to use them, starting in the writing view, "here is where you'll write your story", and pointing out the places that are easy to miss, focus mode, the tray that shows a scene in more detail, the contents. Simple rather than complete; it explains the ideas, not every key.
